#5719. How to Make a Monster

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: How to Make a Monster

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Around 1733, a former manservant to a president of London’s Royal Society sold his hand-illustrated compendium, A Short History of Human Prodigies, and Monstrous Births, to the Society’s current president; one memorable entry was titled “A Wild and Hairy Irishman.” Fifty years later, a Scottish surgeon and anatomist harvested the skeleton of Charles Byrne, known as the Irish Giant, which was displayed in the Royal College of Surgeons of England until 2018.

Since classical antiquity, Western travelers, geographers, and naturalists have often described atypical individuals—and distant peoples, real and imagined—as monsters. Medieval maps depicted giants, human-animal hybrids, and figures gnawing on human limbs inhabiting the edge of the world, where harsh climates supposedly gave rise to brutish species. Centuries later, when explorers and colonial administrators wielded European science to serve empire building, perceptions of the Irish paralleled earlier commentators’ descriptions of “wild” and “monstrous” peoples.

Around 1733, a former manservant to a president of London’s Royal Society sold his hand-illustrated compendium, A Short History of Human Prodigies, and Monstrous Births, to the Society’s current president; one memorable entry was titled “A Wild and Hairy Irishman.” Fifty years later, a Scottish surgeon and anatomist harvested the skeleton of Charles Byrne, known as the Irish Giant, which was displayed in the Royal College of Surgeons of England until 2018.

Since classical antiquity, Western travelers, geographers, and naturalists have often described atypical individuals—and distant peoples, real and imagined—as monsters. Medieval maps depicted giants, human-animal hybrids, and figures gnawing on human limbs inhabiting the edge of the world, where harsh climates supposedly gave rise to brutish species. Centuries later, when explorers and colonial administrators wielded European science to serve empire building, perceptions of the Irish paralleled earlier commentators’ descriptions of “wild” and “monstrous” peoples.

Field Notes from an Extinction: A Novel, Eoghan Walls, Seven Stories Press, 272 pp., $19.95, March 2026

Derry-born poet and creative writing lecturer Eoghan Walls evokes such monster-making narratives in his second novel, Field Notes from an Extinction. Set in 1847 during the Great Famine, the story follows English ornithologist Ignatius Green on a quest to study the world’s last colony of great auks—or garefowl—on Tor Mor, an uninhabited rock off the coast of the Irish island of Inishtrahull. The novel unfolds through Green’s field notebook, in which he depicts Irish people as freakish, grotesque, and animal-like.

Walls has crafted an immersive, unsettling meditation on morality and monstrification, or the act of denying the humanity of individuals or groups. In contrasting Green’s empathy for the auks with his lack of any similar sentiment for the colonized Irish, the novel illuminates how ostensibly objective scientific language and analytical frameworks can narrow the parameters of who is considered worthy of compassion.

The novel’s central plot begins when the residents of Inishtrahull upend Green’s plans to study the auks. Instead of delivering Green’s provisions to Tor Mor as agreed, the villagers requisition some for themselves and deposit in their place a feral child, the offspring of an allegedly murderous, cannibalistic Irishwoman. The irate Green must tend to the child, negotiate an encounter with her fearsome mother, fight poachers, and stretch rapidly dwindling resources—all while trying to study the auks.

When it comes to the Great Famine, Green understands that Ireland is suffering “a sickness of the body & the crops.” However, he doesn’t acknowledge that the British government’s refusal to control grain exports and implement effective relief measures exacerbates death tolls. By its end in 1852, the famine would kill roughly 1 million people; nearly 3 million would emigrate. But Green frames the scarcity of food mockingly and through his own comfort: He has “coal enough for weeks, as it appears the Irish cannot eat coal.”

Actors Jean Aubrey and Diane Aubrey look at the skeleton of Charles Byrne, also known as the Irish Giant, at the Hunterian Museum in London on April 1, 1959. Evening Standard/Hulton Archive via Getty Images

Green casts himself as a victim of robbery, an inadvertent accessory to human trafficking, and the survivor of attempted murder-by-abandonment. The rowboat sold to him for transportation by his provisions coordinator, Liam McGonigle, is a chicken-poop-encrusted “vessel of death” that leaks, “albeit slowly enough you can bail with a cup that has been attached to the seat by a length of twine for precisely this purpose.”

To Green, the islanders’ crime against the auks is even worse. In his notes, he accuses McGonigle and four accomplices who hunt and kill auks for the stuffed bird trade of “a deliberate act of extinction.” In reality, the last known sighting of a living great auk was off the coast of Newfoundland in 1852. (One of the last great auks in the British Isles was captured in Ireland in 1834. Fed an inappropriate diet, it eventually succumbed to a choking death by potato.)

Green rows to Inishtrahull to return the child to McGonigle, who refuses to take her back. Desperate to observe the imminent auk-hatching, Green returns reluctantly to Tor Mor with the child (a “dark day for science & the garefowl”), whose name, he learns later, is Bridget. It’s the first of many moral dilemmas he faces as he weighs scientific discovery against personal convenience, Bridget’s wants and needs, and even potential starvation.

Bridget’s experience is filtered through the lens of the crochety Green, who routinely describes her as he would an animal: “flaccid as shot game”; “naked as a piglet”; with “skin [like] roast pork.” Since she speaks no English and Green no Gaelic, he “[m]ight as well have chatted to the garefowl.” As he tries to clean her, brandishing taxidermy scissors to clip away her matted, lice-filled hair, he observes the “fuzz on her neck & shoulders” and contemplates whether she “remains within the realms of normality for a human child of hirsute lineage.”

Northern Ireland’s most outlandish plan comes to fruition in Jan Carson’s new novel.

Once cleaned and dressed, Green observes that if Bridget were given ringlets and scented in “liberal buckets of rosewater …  she could almost pass as English”—but that, “[s]haved as she is, the effect is more simian.” Indeed, Green approaches Bridget and the Irish like an anthropologist, drawing on a discipline pervaded with sexist, racist, and ethnocentric stereotypes in both method and terminology. He describes some of McGonigle’s relatives and various islanders as “scrawny, muttering in their guttural tongue”; and as “skinny monkey youths” resembling “sea-otters in their hirsuteness, weak shoulders & sloped brows.”

Such stereotypes date back at least as far the 12th century, when Ireland was invaded by descendants of England’s Norman conquerors. In the Middle Ages, chroniclers described Ireland as a land of barbarity, bristling with werewolves and human-ox hybrids. Ireland became England’s first Atlantic colony during the 16th century, when the English Crown confiscated Irish land and granted it to planters and military adventurers. By dehumanizing Irish people and denigrating their customs, English writers framed the conquest of Ireland as acceptable, even necessary.

An illustration featuring Irish Chief Secretary Arthur Balfour addressing a dummy representing the potato famine published in Moonshine in London on Oct. 4, 1890. Paul Popper/Popperfoto via Getty Images

Green’s animal metaphors—particularly those involving monkeys—thus activate a longstanding European shorthand. Medieval Christian theologians warned that people were merely conditionally human, their souls slipping back into those of beasts if they ignored the spiritual contract with God. Apes were the last animal God made before fashioning Adam, and it was with apes (broadly construed) that the least “civilized” people supposedly overlapped.

Narratives of “enfreakment,” meanwhile, framed bodily differences as spectacular errors of nature. In the 19th century, impresarios exhibited “bearded ladies” and “wild” individuals as acceptable viewing for family entertainment and scholarly study. At the height of the British Empire, when white Englishness was the ultimate aspiration, showmen highlighted how individuals departed from those norms. By pathologizing difference, they erected a permission structure for touching and gaping at people—especially women and girls—like Sarah Baartman, a Khoikhoi woman trafficked from South Africa, and Krao, an atypically hairy Laotian child whose captor advertised her as Darwin’s “missing link.”

White Englishmen also framed figures such as Baartman and Krao as transgressing gender norms—just as Green does when he records Bridget’s “moustache of puckered scabs” and describes her tall, strong mother, Aisling O’Leary, as a “slut” and a “grinning giantess,” noting that he “thought her a man” because of “the sheer bulk of her.”

While Green dehumanizes Bridget, Aisling, and other Irish characters, he anthropomorphizes the auks, even naming the colony’s denizens after monarchs and Shakespearean characters. Obsessed with their well-being, he calls the death of Canute, a bird named after a legendary 11th-century English king, a “sorrow” and a “scientific & human loss.”

After Canute’s death, Green dissects and begins to preserve the bird, but when he turns his back on the body, which is potentially infused with arsenic from the embalming soap, Bridget grabs the heart and begins to eat it. Panicking at the potential loss of his specimen—a “scientific tragedy”—Green persuades Bridget to spit out the heart. He even fears for Bridget’s safety, signaling a turning point in his feelings toward the child. The impact is limited, however: Once Bridget’s basic needs are met, Green continues to choose observing birds over fulfilling her desires.

A month after Bridget’s arrival, the pair leave Tor Mor again for Inishtrahull, where the situation has grown dire. Green observes the sick, the dead, and the dying on every road and in nearly every dwelling. He arrives at the bleak realization that the reason the Irish chose to kill and sell the auks—whose international market value rose in part because of the extinction it hastened—was a matter of survival. Killing the last great auks (those aforementioned “deliberate act[s] of extinction”) meant affording grain, which could be the difference between life and death by starvation.

An illustration of Krao Farini at Castan’s Panoptikum in Berlin in 1890.Bildagentur-online/Universal Images Group via Getty Images

In interviews, Walls has observed how the far right in Ireland describes immigrants with the same language that the English used to dehumanize Irish people in the 19th century. During a time of widespread xenophobia, Field Notes reminds readers that millions of Europeans—Irish, Italian, and more—were once refugees, their humanity dismissed by privileged elites.

How does a nation remember collective trauma? Was the Irish takeaway from the tragedy that saw nearly half the country perish or emigrate “never again”—or “never again to anyone”? As a European country and a former British colony, Ireland has a unique position. The country often stands in solidarity with other oppressed peoples, including with the South African anti-apartheid movement and, more recently, with Palestinians in Gaza. That sensibility is at odds with increasingly vocal and violent racist and anti-immigrant sentiment in the country today.

Between 1999 and 2001, Walls lived in Rwanda and witnessed famine in the aftermath of genocide. He saw firsthand human compassion amid immense suffering and economic inequality, but also the moral dilemmas facing outsiders who observed those struggles. Field Notes illustrates how such empathy and understanding grow through extended contact—and how intersecting catastrophes can lead individuals to abandon their humanity and preserve certain beings at the expense of others.

Surekha Davies is a historian of science. She is the author of Humans: A Monstrous History (University of California Press, 2025) and Renaissance Ethnography and the Invention of the Human: New Worlds, Maps and Monsters (Cambridge, 2016).

#5717. Hasan Piker lashes out at Republicans, calls them 'terrorists' in event with Ilhan Omar

Leftist Twitch streamer Hassan Piker joined Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., at a campaign event in Minnesota on Monday. (TWITCH / @HASANABI,  MINNEAPOLIS, MN, 10/26/2026)

Leftist gaming site commentator Hasan Piker and Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., met up in Minnesota to cohost a campaign event on Monday night, bashing President Donald Trump's administration as well as Democrats who are willing to work with him.

Piker, who streams his commentary on Twitch, called Republicans "terrorists" during the event, and Omar referred to the party as the "pedophile protection party."

They made the comments during a Q&A session led by failed Minneapolis mayoral candidate Omar Fateh.

ILHAN OMAR ON TRACK FOR FIFTH TERM AS MINNESOTA VOTERS MAINTAIN FAR-LEFT STREAK

Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., is scheduled to appear alongside left-wing streamer Hasan Piker at a sold-out Minneapolis event Monday as Piker's participation draws backlash from fellow Minnesota Democrats. (Getty Images)

"Trump's obsession with us started in 2016, when he arrived at the airport and begged for our vote. We said, no thank you. We continued to say, no thank you. He lies and says he's won Minnesota three times, as if," Omar said.

"This defeat is going to be the fourth time! We remind him that Minnesota is not interested in a pedophile protection party. Minnesota doesn't send people who are corrupt and are interested in enriching themselves. And Minnesota does not come after the refugees, they send one to Congress," she added.

TRUMP ADMINISTRATION SLASHES TIME REFUGEES HAVE TO CHALLENGE LOSS OF LEGAL STATUS

Piker went after less radical Democrats later in the interview, saying many in the party had ceded arguments to Trump in 2024 when they "started also saying that migrants were a national security threat."

"That's unacceptable. Migrants are not a national security threat. They are our neighbors. They are allies. They work alongside us. They are not the burden, but a benefit. They are what make this country strong, and they're what make it this country great. And any Democrat ... any Democrat that refuses to recognize that, in my opinion, is un-American," Piker said.

Rep. Ilhan Omar (D-Minn.) and influencer and left-wing political commentator Hasan Piker stand with Wisconsin gubernatorial candidate Francesca Hong during a campaign rally for Hong on August 2, 2026. (Joshua Lott/Getty Images)

FEDERAL APPEALS COURT COMPARES TRUMP MIGRANT DETENTION POLICY TO JAPANESE AMERICAN INTERNMENT

"I'm not even gonna bring up the Republicans because they are the most un-American force in this country. They're terrorists. They are terrorizing our neighborhoods. They're destroying what remains of the social fabric that holds us together. It's completely unacceptable. But to the Democrats who think this problem is going to go away, they were wrong in 2024, and they're wrong now. A paperwork violation does not require deportation. That is cruel and unjust punishment," he added.

Piker had largely been sidelined by Democratic campaigns following a disastrous appearance with Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan last month.

HASAN PIKER'S INFLUENCE TESTS BREAKING POINT OF DEMOCRATIC PARTY

Piker’s commentary has stained El-Sayed’s insurgent progressive campaign recently, particularly his assertion that America "deserved" the 9/11 terrorist attacks. Even El-Sayed was driven to put space between himself and Piker after their connection became public.

Abdul El-Sayed, left, pictured alongside Hasan Piker, right. (Evan Cobb for The Washington Post via Getty Images; Dia Dipasupil/FilmMagic)

He said that "all Americans deserve the right to advocate for what they believe without fear of violence," and acknowledged that "antisemitism is a scourge, and we need to address it in all its forms," and added that "nobody speaks for this campaign besides me and my campaign spokespeople."

"And while politicians and pundits try to make this race about a Twitch streamer, we’re going to be talking about gas, groceries, good schools and healthcare," he said.

Anders Hagstrom is a reporter with Fox News Digital covering national politics and major breaking news events.

#5716. Lucid’s 25,000 Bolt robotaxis will run on Nvidia’s self-driving software

Lucid’s European robotaxi deal now has a driver. Bolt announced today that its fleet of at least 25,000 autonomous Lucid vehicles will run Nvidia’s Alpamayo Stack self-driving software, starting with a data-collection program in Madrid in 2027.

That gives Lucid two robotaxi customers on two different autonomy systems: Nuro’s software for Uber’s Gravity robotaxis in the US, and now Nvidia’s for Bolt in Europe.

#5715. Filming Among the Rubble of Nazi Germany

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Filming Among the Rubble of Nazi Germany

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In Pawel Pawlikowski’s spare, deeply-felt new film Fatherland, the great novelist Thomas Mann (Hanns Zischler) returns to Germany from his Californian exile, and travels with his daughter Erika (Sandra Hüller) through their ruined, occupied homeland. Toward the end, they walk into a bomb-damaged church, and sit listening as two men play Bach’s “Jesu, Joy of Man’s Desiring” on the organ. Mann weeps for all that is lost.

Pawlikowski has picked his moment well. On April 26, 1945, at the conference in San Francisco that created the United Nations, U.S. Secretary of State Edward Stettinius lamented “how close our whole civilization has come to utter ruin.” That conference played a key part in building a new global settlement that aimed to ensure, through guaranteed rights and freedoms, through mutual trade and prosperity, that such a catastrophe could never happen again.

In Pawel Pawlikowski’s spare, deeply-felt new film Fatherland, the great novelist Thomas Mann (Hanns Zischler) returns to Germany from his Californian exile, and travels with his daughter Erika (Sandra Hüller) through their ruined, occupied homeland. Toward the end, they walk into a bomb-damaged church, and sit listening as two men play Bach’s “Jesu, Joy of Man’s Desiring” on the organ. Mann weeps for all that is lost.

Pawlikowski has picked his moment well. On April 26, 1945, at the conference in San Francisco that created the United Nations, U.S. Secretary of State Edward Stettinius lamented “how close our whole civilization has come to utter ruin.” That conference played a key part in building a new global settlement that aimed to ensure, through guaranteed rights and freedoms, through mutual trade and prosperity, that such a catastrophe could never happen again.

But today, that postwar order is coming under ever heavier bombardment. Predictions of its destruction are commonplace. It’s a good time to look through the lens of the 1940s, and contemplate just why that order was created in the first place.

Back then, Hollywood was filming in the ruins for real. Between 1947 and 1949, four great directors each went to Germany and shot an American spin on what the postwar Germans called the Trümmerfilm, or “rubble movie.”

The 1940s was perhaps Hollywood’s apotheosis. The war fostered new depth and seriousness; television had not yet weakened cinema’s dominion. This was the era of Humphrey Bogart, Ava Gardner, and film noir; of movies like Casablanca, Double Indemnity, and Notorious. The four rubble films themselves are hardly classics, but between them, they captured how a newly super-powerful United States was trying to understand how the world should be rebuilt. They dramatized the dilemmas and nightmares Americans faced as they decided whether to entangle themselves with Europe in the long term. Today, they remind us that the creation of the settlement we’re watching die was driven not only by idealism, but by guilt, hard-faced realism, and fear.

In Jacques Tourneur’s Berlin Express, shot in summer 1947, the wreckage of Frankfurt—the capital of the U.S. zone—stands for the dangers of failing to build a new, peaceful world. Agents of the revanchist Nazi underground kidnap a revered advocate of international unity to prevent him attending a conference on German reunification, hiding him in a derelict brewery.

This dramatized the dread felt by many, not least in Hollywood, that desperate Germans could all too easily turn to fascism once again. Among the attendees at that 1945 San Francisco conference was Orson Welles, who reported in his column in the New York Post that in the hotel lobbies and committee rooms, “the phony fear of Communism is smoke-screening the real menace of renascent Fascism.” Following this logic, Berlin Express strains to cast the Soviets as just one of the four friendly wartime allies.

But all this is undercut by an overarching, noir-ish sense of uncertainty. As a worldly Frenchwoman tells the film’s naive U.S. liberal, Europeans have learned the hard way to maintain “suspicion of everyone and everything.” Even Hollywood couldn’t quite gloss over the impossibility of the dream of a one-world utopia.

That same summer, another Hollywood director, Billy Wilder, was in Berlin, shooting a film that was much less optimistic about the United States’ chances of transforming Europe. Wilder was born to Jewish parents in Galicia, then part of the Austro-Hungarian Empire; his career began in Berlin before he had to flee in 1933, first to France, then the United States. This was not his first return to Germany. In 1945, he had served as head of the film unit of the U.S. Information Control Division and had been involved with making Die Todesmühlen (Death Mills), a grueling 20-minute montage of footage from the liberated death camps, designed to force Germans to look their starvation-ravaged victims in the eye. Among the Holocaust’s victims were Wilder’s mother, stepfather, and grandmother.

The movie Wilder shot in 1947, A Foreign Affair, uses the city’s broken buildings to confront Americans with the moral ruin that Nazism, bombing, and invasion had wrought on the German population—and the degrading effect that this was having on U.S. soldiers. Characteristically, this is played as a screwball comedy, sending up the difficulty of “denazifying” people who are scrabbling in the rubble to survive.

Millard Mitchell (center) and Marlene Dietrich in A Foreign Affair (1948).Paramount Pictures/Sunset Boulevard/Getty Image

The film follows a pious Iowa congresswoman who arrives intent on tackling the moral turpitude of fräulein-chasing GIs. (In this, she is reflecting an outdated domestic U.S. attitude: Soldiers had so widely ignored rules forbidding fraternization with civilians that the ban had been scrapped.) The congresswoman presents an army captain with a birthday cake baked by his beloved back home—which he promptly barters on the black market for a mattress for his mistress, Erika von Schlütow, a nightclub singer and former consort of a Nazi general, who now scrapes along in a derelict building.

Erika, played by Wilder’s old Berlin friend and fellow Hollywood exile Marlene Dietrich, is the picture of European worldliness. As she explains, “We’ve all become animals with exactly one instinct left—self-preservation.” She invites the congresswoman to imagine what it was like “to be a woman in this town when the Russians first swept in.”

Wilder has no solution to any of this; he was just trying to coax Americans to be honest about the depth of the problem they were trying to solve. Unsurprisingly, government officials soon pressured his studio to pull the film from U.S. cinemas. It was not seen in Germany until 1977.

Like Wilder, Fred Zinnemann grew up Jewish in the Austro-Hungarian Empire and began his movie career in Berlin before moving to Hollywood, only to lose close family to the Holocaust. While his old colleague was shooting in Berlin, Zinnemann was at work in the ruins of Ingolstadt, Munich, Nuremberg, and Würzburg. He too wanted Americans to face what had happened in Europe, but his approach was both more emotional and more practical, explicitly intended to raise awareness of one of the war’s most painful consequences.

The Search is based on reports of the United Nations Relief and Rehabilitation Association (UNRRA) about the teams trying to reunite lost and displaced children with any surviving family, and on interviews the director conducted with some of the children in UNRRA’s care. A few of them appear as characters in the film, blankly recounting stories of murder and enslavement. Zinnemann consciously left out the worst of what he had heard, reasoning that U.S. audiences would not be able to stomach it.

Movie poster for The Search (1948).LMPC/Getty Images

His film uses Germany’s ruins to evoke the destruction of the most basic human bonds—trust, motherhood, medical care, kindness. It follows a 9-year-old Czech boy, Karel Malik, who has survived Auschwitz but can barely speak. He has forgotten his name and his family. When he and his friend are put in an ambulance for medical treatment, past experience tells them they are going to be gassed, so they break out and flee into the rubble. Trying to swim across a river to escape the uniformed officials giving chase, Karel’s friend drowns. All this is intercut with Mrs. Malik’s refusal to give up searching for her son, even as each is told the other is dead. Finally, thanks to the compassion of an English UNRRA official and of a U.S. soldier (played by Montgomery Clift), mother and son are reunited, years after their cultured family life was torn apart.

Zinnemann’s film is pointedly international in its focus—the displaced children speak Polish, Czech, French, German; the adults trying to help them are from various Allied nations. It well expresses the horror that informed the Universal Declaration of Human Rights in December 1948, a few months after the film’s release, and the 1950 European Convention on Human Rights.

Read More A bright lime-green vintage car, labeled "Trabant," driving along a paved street. Attached to its roof is a large German flag featuring blue lettering reading "AfD" overlaid with a blue arrow. A bright lime-green vintage car, labeled "Trabant," driving along a paved street. Attached to its roof is a large German flag featuring blue lettering reading "AfD" overlaid with a blue arrow. What’s the Matter With Eastern Germany? German far-right populism traces back to reunification—but historians disagree about why. Analysis | Paul Hockenos

German far-right populism traces back to reunification—but historians disagree about why.

Yet in all three of these films, there is something missing. As the critic J.E. Smyth has noted, Zinnemann had chosen to make his young protagonist Czech to draw attention to the newly free Republic of Czechoslovakia as a symbol of “future European regeneration.” But by the time it premiered in March 1948, Prague had fallen to a Stalinist coup. The new government banned The Search; the young actor who played Karel was forbidden to travel to the United States to receive his special juvenile Oscar. For all their sincere objections to tyranny, none of these films criticized Stalin’s Soviet Union—not even Wilder’s, even though the director had done so in 1939’s Ninotchka—despite its advance across Eastern Europe, and its brutalizing of both German civilians and displaced persons. As should have been obvious, the fear of Stalinism wasn’t “phony” after all.

Within months of the Czech coup, the Soviets had blockaded Berlin, determined to starve the city into submission and force the western Allies out. Instead, they launched a vast rescue operation, flying in supplies for 2 million West Berliners for over a year. This prompted the final U.S. rubble film, George Seaton’s The Big Lift—which captures what had happened to the one-eye-shut idealism of 1947. When Seaton started shooting in July 1949, the North Atlantic Treaty was a few weeks old; by the time he wrapped, China had fallen to communism and the Soviets had the atom bomb.

Montgomery Clift (left) and Paul Douglas in The Big Lift (1950). 20th Century-Fox/Getty Images

Like The Search, The Big Lift stars Clift as a warm-hearted U.S. officer—a pilot assigned to the airlift. But this time, his kindness leaves him open to being duped by a desperate German woman hiding a Nazi past. This makes the film an instance of an ideological turn toward anti-totalitarianism: hostile both to the Nazi past and the present Soviet threat.

This was distinct from the vicious hard-right anti-communism that was spreading at the same time and had spurred the House Un-American Activities Committee to summon Hollywood leftists to Washington, D.C., to be show-trialled. The Big Lift specifically sets its face against the United States’ own aggressive authoritarianism. Clift’s fellow officer, a Polish American called Hank Kowalski, is shown struggling to overcome his drive to revenge himself for the torture he endured as a prisoner of war, and his tendency to tyrannize his German girlfriend. She forces him first to make the case for democracy, and then to live up to it, citing the U.S. Constitution at him, and even calling him a “storm trooper.” She also calls out U.S. antisemitism.

Hollywood’s capitulation to the Red Scare is sometimes exaggerated: This was far from the only film that struck a liberal, pro-democratic, anti-totalitarian stance. And while Sen. Joseph McCarthy had launched his mendacious reign of terror by the time The Big Lift came out, this strain of liberal anti-totalitarianism long outlasted him.

But today, Fatherland is being released in a very different United States, where that ideology has been in retreat for years, terribly discredited by the war in Iraq, while the heirs to HUAC and McCarthy are in their pomp. The lesson from those old 1940s rubble films is that it’s not only the postwar order that lies in ruins. The robust liberal United States that once built that settlement is in dire need of reconstruction too.

Phil Tinline is a documentarian and journalist and the author of Ghosts of Iron Mountain: The Hoax of the Century, Its Enduring Impact, and What It Reveals About America.

#5713. Kia’s electric SUV and van are getting a new job as police cars

Kia’s electric vehicles could soon be the ones pulling you over. Kia converted the EV5 and PV5 into patrol vehicles as part of its push for greener police cars.

Electric vehicles make great police cars — they have instant torque, require less maintenance, and are nearly silent.

Several police departments have used electric patrol cars for years, including the Ford Mustang Mach-E and Tesla Model Y.

Kia is eyeing the market with its midsize electric SUV and van. At the 2026 International Public Security Industry Exhibition in South Korea, Kia showcased two patrol vehicle concepts based on the EV5 and PV5.

The EV5 and PV5 patrol car concepts feature custom lights, sirens, and dedicated police badging on the side.

This year’s theme was “A New Era of K-Public Security by Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Robotics,” so Kia took the opportunity to showcase its latest security equipment and tech.

Kia said the EV5 is a perfect fit for police use, offering “best-in-class” interior space and luggage capacity of up to 965 liters. It features an added luggage board to store equipment and other supplies.

Earlier this year, Kia developed AI patrol cars with the Korean National Police Agency and has been holding training programs to implement electric vehicles.

The AI Patrol Car is based on the PV5, Kia’s 5-seat electric van. The PV5 AI Patrol Vehicle is equipped with three 4K AI cameras and even a drone landing pad mounted on the roof.

The cameras located on the front and rear of the vehicle use AI to detect dangerous situations and automatically alert the driver.

For example, the system can identify guns, knives, bats, and other weapons, as well as emergencies in which someone collapses.

The electric patrol van is equipped with a roof-mounted drone for ground surveillance, while the docking station charges it on the go. The drones have advanced thermal imaging cameras with 90X zoom that can read license plates and pinpoint someone on the run.

Kia’s AI Patrol Cars have been in operation by the Seoul Metropolitan Police Agency’s Wide Area Prevention Patrol Division since last July.

“We intend to showcase the competitiveness of Kia’s electrified mobility and its potential for use as police patrol vehicles,” a Kia official said. The company plans to continue developing custom solutions for police and other public sector use based on its electric vehicles.

#5710. FBI raids Beverly Hills mansion as part of Eric Swalwell investigation

Federal authorities raided a California mansion related to an investigation into former Rep. Eric Swalwell, D-Calif., on Tuesday morning.

BEVERLY HILLS – Federal authorities raided a California mansion belonging to billionaire Stephen Cloobeck on Tuesday morning, Fox News has learned.

The FBI targeted Cloobeck's Beverly Hills mansion in connection with an investigation into disgraced former Rep. Eric Swalwell, D-Calif. Swalwell resigned from Congress and withdrew from the California gubernatorial race earlier this year after facing multiple accusations of sexual misconduct.

Law enforcement executed the raid at roughly 6 a.m. local time with at least two dozen agents participating. Authorities searched the massive property for several hours but offered no details about what they found.

Several people were taken out of the house in handcuffs, but they were all released soon after. Cloobeck was not at the residence at the time of the raid.

Rep. Eric Swalwell (D-Calif.) speaks during a House Judiciary Committee hearing with FBI Director Kash Patel in the Rayburn House Office Building on Sept. 17, 2025. (Win McNamee/Getty Images)

The raid was linked to a sex worker reportedly connected to the investigation.

Cloobeck is a longtime supporter of Swalwell's political campaigns.

In August, the FBI searched Swalwell's D.C. home and seized his electronic devices at San Francisco International Airport during an operation. The operation was related to a probe into sexual misconduct, according to sources familiar with the incident. He was not arrested or detained.

Cloobeck was arrested earlier this year in an unrelated case, in which he faced charges of attempting to influence witnesses in a criminal case against his fiancée.

At least five women, including one former staffer, have accused Swalwell of sexual misconduct or rape.

#5709. Why Putin’s Defenders Are Increasingly American

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Essay: Why Putin’s Defenders Are Increasingly American

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In mid-September, internet sleuths scoured the website of an obscure Russian organization, the International Club of National Unity. Populated by various Russian politicians, state media propagandists, and security services operatives, the club declares that it was created in “accordance with Decrees of the President of the Russian Federation.” Although the club claims to support “humanitarian missions to the peoples of Ukraine … and other countries facing dire straits” on the basis of “respect, peace, harmony, and mutual assistance,” many of the supernumeraries associated with it are vocal advocates of nuclear strikes against Western capitals and Ukrainian cities.

What makes this instrument of Russian propaganda unusual, however, is that it contains the names of several Americans as members of its leadership team. The most prominent is Columbia University economist Jeffrey Sachs, who has promoted Kremlin talking points that delegitimize the Ukrainian state, including the canard that the 2014 Maidan Revolution was a coup backed by the United States. The recent evolution of Sachs from a widely respected academic to a willing propagandist for a dictatorial regime perpetrating an increasingly bloody and genocidal war cannot be explained without examining the phenomenon of the fellow traveler.

In mid-September, internet sleuths scoured the website of an obscure Russian organization, the International Club of National Unity. Populated by various Russian politicians, state media propagandists, and security services operatives, the club declares that it was created in “accordance with Decrees of the President of the Russian Federation.” Although the club claims to support “humanitarian missions to the peoples of Ukraine … and other countries facing dire straits” on the basis of “respect, peace, harmony, and mutual assistance,” many of the supernumeraries associated with it are vocal advocates of nuclear strikes against Western capitals and Ukrainian cities.

What makes this instrument of Russian propaganda unusual, however, is that it contains the names of several Americans as members of its leadership team. The most prominent is Columbia University economist Jeffrey Sachs, who has promoted Kremlin talking points that delegitimize the Ukrainian state, including the canard that the 2014 Maidan Revolution was a coup backed by the United States. The recent evolution of Sachs from a widely respected academic to a willing propagandist for a dictatorial regime perpetrating an increasingly bloody and genocidal war cannot be explained without examining the phenomenon of the fellow traveler.

The term “fellow traveler” has a long pedigree. It appeared in the early days of the Soviet Union, when the nascent communist state sought external support and hoped to foment revolutions in the capitalist world. Today, the term has come to describe a range of Western public figures who sympathize with Russia, its leadership, and its war.

Following Russia’s invasion of Crimea and the eastern Donbas in 2014, many of these allies were European. Nowadays, however, they are predominantly American and span the political spectrum. To be sure, one can still find pro-Kremlin stalwarts in Europe, such as former German Chancellor Gerhard Schröder and former Austrian Foreign Minister Karin Kneissl, who now lives in St. Petersburg. Both used to work for Russian energy companies. However, many of Russian President Vladimir Putin’s former European admirers, such as French far-right leader Marine Le Pen, have become critics of Russia since the full-scale invasion in 2022. Ironically, as parts of the European right have tempered their support for the Kremlin, Americans are now supplanting them.

Podcaster and right-wing influencer Candace Owens attends the St. Petersburg International Economic Forum in St. Petersburg, Russia, on June 4.Olga Maltseva/AFP via Getty Images

At the annual St. Petersburg Economic Forum in June, Putin’s American fellow travelers were highly visible, with right-wing podcaster Candace Owens the star of the show. Sachs—who is now a frequent guest on the TV show hosted by Vladimir Solovyov, the most virulent Russian cheerleader for annihilating Ukrainians and bombing Western cities—participated by video from New York. After attending the forum, Andrew Tate and his brother Tristan, who are wanted in Britain on charges of rape and human trafficking, visited a Russian military training ground. They broadcast their positive views of Russia and its military to their predominantly young male global following. Since then, University of Chicago professor John Mearsheimer, who blames the West for Putin’s desire to crush Ukraine, also visited Moscow. Unsurprisingly, the Russian government has used Mearsheimer’s statements to justify its war on Ukraine.

These are not the only prominent Americans in thrall to the Kremlin and its narratives. Others include former Fox News host Tucker Carlson, film director Oliver Stone, Republican Rep. Anna Paulina Luna, right-wing influencer Steve Bannon, and former U.S. National Security Advisor Lt. Gen. Michael Flynn. During her trip to Russia, Owens was interviewed by another fellow traveler, former CNN anchor Rick Sanchez. He is now based in Moscow, where he hosts the Kremlin’s flagship English-language news program on RT, a propaganda channel banned in the United States and Europe since 2022.

Like Carlson before her, Owens praised Moscow’s supposed cleanliness and safety while insisting that any distrust of Russia is an unnecessary “Cold War hangover.” Both Carlson and Owens unwittingly echo the words of fellow traveler Lion Feuchtwanger, an exiled German novelist and playwright who visited the Soviet Union in the 1930s. In the immediate aftermath of Stalin’s mass terror and genocidal famine, Feuchtwanger mused that “everywhere in that great city of Moscow there was an atmosphere of harmony and contentment, even of happiness.”

A view of Red Square in Moscow on Jan. 1, 1935.AFP via Getty Images

To be sure, today’s Russia supporters are a pale shadow of the Western luminaries who sympathized with Moscow in the past, including artists such as Picasso and Fernand Leger, philosopher Jean-Paul Sartre, and writers such as André Gide, Theodore Dreiser, John Dos Passos, Ernest Hemingway, George Bernard Shaw, and even (briefly) W. H. Auden. Today’s Russia-aligned voices may lack artistic and literary greatness, but they make up for it with their vast social media audiences and wide-ranging influence on the MAGA movement.

How, then, should we understand what motivates these contemporary allies of a militaristic, repressive society? One place to start is by examining the Kremlin’s original Western allies. To that end, I recently dove into David Caute’s The Fellow-Travellers, a comprehensive 1973 history of the phenomenon during the Soviet era.

Caute’s book explains that the main impulse of the original fellow travelers stemmed from a belief in progress and the idea that society could be engineered from the top down. Caute also shows that their admiration for the Soviet project stemmed from their deep disillusionment with their home countries and the West in general. Many of these Western intellectuals were concerned about the rise of fascist movements in their countries and believed that communism could serve as a countervailing force.

Consequently, the fellow travelers often overlooked or even justified the regime’s heinous crimes, including the famine-induced genocide that the Stalin regime perpetrated in Ukraine in the early 1930s. At the height of the murderous wave of cultural and political repression known as the Great Terror, many Western intellectuals proudly stood with Stalin’s Soviet Union. In 1937, for instance, a conference on “Peace and Friendship with the Soviet Union” in London was addressed by Dean of Canterbury Hewlett Johnson, book publisher Victor Gollancz, members of the British aristocracy, and Labour Party parliamentarians. Influential Western journalists played an especially pernicious role; chief among them was New York Times correspondent Walter Duranty, who became an enthusiastic supporter of Stalin and whitewashed Soviet crimes.

Just as it did then, today’s Kremlin propaganda seeks to win Western supporters by exploiting their discontents and anxieties. To the Western cultural right, the Kremlin emphasizes Russia’s supposed support for traditional values. To global altruists seeking to avert conflict with a nuclear power, the Kremlin offers Ukraine’s capitulation as the price of peace.

Pro-Russian activists march to commemorate the Soviet victory over Germany in World War II in Washington, D.C., on May 3, 2025.Amid Farah/AFP via Getty Images

As Caute documents, the Soviet Union’s outreach was carried out by the Society of Cultural Relations with Foreign Countries, which collaborated with Western communist parties, as well as by various Soviet friendship societies that targeted non-communists. Following the collapse of the Soviet Union in 1991, Moscow’s foreign influence mechanisms disintegrated, only to be rebuilt under Russian President Vladimir Putin with fresh resources. When Russia first invaded Ukraine in 2014, the Kremlin used these influence networks to spread its narrative that the invasion was actually an intra-Ukrainian civil war, cultivate critics of Ukraine, and denigrate its Western supporters. RT, as the Kremlin’s global, multi-language TV network, played a significant propaganda role.

Following the shutdown of RT in the West, Russia shifted its influence operations to social media. The Kremlin boosted influential voices opposed to the “woke agenda” of LBGTQ rights, secularism, and immigration, also targeting those hoping to avert a larger conflict with Russia and thus also susceptible to Moscow’s narratives. To support these aims, the Kremlin devoted extensive resources to a wide range of initiatives, including the Club of National Unity.

As part of the effort to win over the cultural right, Putin issued Decree 809 in November 2022. The document, titled “On the Approval of the Fundamentals of State Policy for the Preservation and Strengthening of Traditional Russian Spiritual and Moral Values,” is a broad attack on Western decadence and liberalism, counterposing Russia as a traditional, faith-based patriarchal society. The decree closely aligns with the Russian Orthodox Church, whose purged and infiltrated remnants became a major propagandist for the Soviet war effort under Stalin during World War II—and which has been controlled by the Kremlin ever since.

Empires inevitably decline, and Putin’s project will be no exception.

Enhanced propaganda efforts were also facilitated by the “Foundation for the Support and Protection of the Rights of Compatriots Living Abroad,” which secretly disbursed millions of dollars to organizations and individuals, primarily in Europe.

The Kremlin’s efforts to influence the U.S. right are well resourced. It has spent millions of dollars on U.S. influencers and podcasters, not to mention the social media visibility boost that Russia-friendly voices get from a vast network of Russian internet bots. In turn, American allies provide a media platform Russian propagandists such as Alexander Dugin, a far-right nationalist who has long been tasked by the Russian intelligence services with wooing right-wing ideologues in the West. American influencers who have platformed him include Tucker Carlson and Alex Jones, helping them to propagate the view of Russia as a safe haven for Westerners who reject what they regard as the destructive liberal ideological agenda.

The phenomenon of otherwise reputable Western opinion leaders aligning with and fawning over murderous tyrants has a long pedigree on both the left and right. In the 1930s, the former British king and then Duke of Windsor; the industrialist Henry Ford; the aviator Charles Lindbergh, IBM CEO Thomas Watson, and French writer Louis-Ferdinand Céline praised Hitler and the Nazi regime. Similar to Moscow, Berlin built an international network called Auslandsorganisation that sought to win Western public support through various propaganda activities. Fans of Mussolini’s fascism included Columbia University President Nicholas Butler Murray and the poet Ezra Pound. In the 1960s and early 1970s, the North Vietnamese dictatorship could count Mary McCarthy, Bertrand Russell, Sartre, and Simone de Beauvoir among its sympathizers. Noam Chomsky and Jane Fonda visited North Vietnam and expressed support for what they characterized as its anti-imperial agenda. Chomsky would later question the crimes of one of the most vicious 20th century murderers, Cambodia’s Pol Pot.

Actor Jane Fonda meets with North Vietnamese Deputy Prime Minister Nguyen Duy Trinh in Hanoi on July 21, 1972.TF/AFP Files/AFP via Getty Images

The through line that connects many of these fellow travelers is that they seem to be consumed by their domestic political or intellectual hatreds—including contempt for political opponents and various aspects of the societies they live in—to the point that they will embrace the most brutal, genocidal foreign regime: The enemy of my domestic enemy is my friend.

As Caute’s book explains, some of the original fellow travelers became disillusioned with Moscow after the 1939 Molotov-Ribbentrop Pact, which allied Nazi Germany and the Soviet Union during the first two years of World War II, facilitating their joint invasion of Poland. Others only acknowledged their disaffection in the war’s aftermath as they witnessed Central Europe fall to communist tyranny. In 1949, several former sympathizers—including Gide, Stephen Spender, and Arthur Koestler—contributed to an anthology, The God that Failed, in which they reckoned with the roots of their support for Soviet rule, described their gradual disaffection, and acknowledged their moral failings. More fellow travelers fell away after Stalin’s death and the revelations of his crimes by his successor, Nikita Khrushchev; still others after Soviet tanks crushed the 1956 Hungarian Revolution and 1968 Prague Spring.

Will today’s generation of fellow travelers face a similar reckoning someday? There is little reason to hope for a change of heart. Past generations were aided in their reckoning by the fact that most of Stalin’s mass crimes occurred in darkness. Later revelations led many to reject him. Today’s Putin cheerleaders, by contrast, live at a time when virtually everything is known about the nature of Putin’s regime. There is abundant digital and eyewitness evidence of Russia’s systematic war crimes in Ukraine, including unspeakable horrors inflicted in the regions it occupies. Modern-day fellow travelers ignore these copiously documented facts or dismiss them as irrelevant.

Tucker Carlson interviews Russian President Vladimir Putin at the Kremlin in Moscow on Feb. 6, 2024.Gavriil Grigorov/Pool/AFP via Getty Image

Equally disturbing is their indifference to Putin’s system of censorship and political repression, which stifles free discussion and criminalizes any disapproval of his “special military operation.” U.S. podcasters and pundits who endlessly rail against the alleged suppression of free speech in the United States and present themselves as rigorous truth-seekers have no interest in how this issue is treated by Putin in Russia.

Stalin’s crimes were an order of magnitude greater than what Putin has managed so far. But the current crop of fellow travelers is more reprehensible and dangerous than those of the last century. While support for Stalin by prominent Westerners mattered morally, it had little practical effect—there was no credible way to stop him or avert his crimes. In contrast, the West today has the means to effectively counter Putin’s aggression. Today’s apologists for Putin, by fanning controversy and skewing the public debate, have the power to delay, slow down, or cut off needed aid to Ukraine. In doing so, they are actively contributing to Putin’s war. History will likely judge them far more harshly than their misguided predecessors.

Yet no one will be judged more harshly than the newest member of Putin’s circle of fellow travelers: U.S. President Donald Trump. For years, Trump has glorified Putin and expressed admiration for him while frequently showering Ukraine’s democratically elected president, Volodymyr Zelensky, with contempt. In recent days, however, Trump has gone far beyond expressing personal sympathy for Putin and crossed a geopolitical Rubicon. After initially shifting the United States from an outright ally of Ukraine to a neutral intermediary, Trump has both weakened Ukraine’s ability to defend itself and, by lifting energy sanctions, provided the Kremlin with financial resources that will be deployed against Ukraine. It is disgraceful that he has chosen to do so while Russia is trying to render Ukraine’s major cities uninhabitable with escalating waves of attacks.

Near-universal condemnation of Trump’s alliance with Putin by other Western leaders, as well as substantial support for Ukraine in the Republican Party and its voter ranks, may yet force him to change course. But the cynical amorality of Trump’s latest actions will leave a permanent mark on his already controversial, disruptive, and disgraceful presidency.

Adrian Karatnycky is a senior fellow at the Atlantic Council, the founder of Myrmidon Group, and the author of Battleground Ukraine: From Independence to the War with Russia.

#5705. The Real Danger of Big Tech

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: The Real Danger of Big Tech

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

For at least a year now, newspapers have been increasingly filled with urgent and anxious reports about the quickening pace of artificial intelligence development, warning ominously that the technology will eventually defy human control and take over the world.

It is a threat worth taking seriously, and no mere hype. But with all the attention devoted to AI’s growing power, an even more imminent danger has become clear: With their prodigious fortunes, a small cast of men who lead the world’s most powerful AI-related companies have come to wield extraordinary and perhaps already-unstoppable power over U.S. government and society—and, thereby, unprecedented influence over world affairs.

For at least a year now, newspapers have been increasingly filled with urgent and anxious reports about the quickening pace of artificial intelligence development, warning ominously that the technology will eventually defy human control and take over the world.

It is a threat worth taking seriously, and no mere hype. But with all the attention devoted to AI’s growing power, an even more imminent danger has become clear: With their prodigious fortunes, a small cast of men who lead the world’s most powerful AI-related companies have come to wield extraordinary and perhaps already-unstoppable power over U.S. government and society—and, thereby, unprecedented influence over world affairs.

The richest among them, Elon Musk, is the most spectacular example. He has exhibited no modesty about his ambitions as he has used his Midas-like wealth and tentacular corporations to become an irresistible force in contemporary affairs. The U.S. government now openly depends on Musk, who became a trillionaire in June, for its space program, for global communications, and for holding the country’s own in competition with China in frontier industries such as electric vehicles and batteries. But that is far from all.

Early in U.S. President Donald Trump’s second term, the White House accorded a leading role to Musk in shaping—and ostensibly streamlining—the country’s bureaucracy. It gave him not only unprecedented say over large-scale firings and the shuttering of long-standing government entities but also access to the personal data of millions of citizens.

A parallel and equally shocking example of never-before-seen personal influence over government came to light in recent days with the news that Ukraine’s access to the military-grade version of Musk’s internet services company, Starlink, had been interrupted or seriously degraded. Clarity about how this happened or who was responsible has been hard to come by, but the Wall Street Journal reported that Musk ordered the slowdown, badly hindering Ukraine’s offensive and defensive abilities in its ongoing war with Russia. Reuters, moreover, reported that the decision came from the White House. (Trump has denied involvement.)

No other company, or even country, has a system nearly as capable as Starlink, meaning that even the Pentagon, with a budget larger than the military spending of the next 10 defense powers combined, is reliant on Musk for these sorts of capabilities. And the lack of transparency about the chain of command—or the involvement or lack thereof of the U.S. government—in ordering changes that temporarily crippled another country’s defenses is ominous.

Musk, meanwhile, has begun to stealthily play an influential role in what are likely to be two of the most important public policy debates of the coming decades. The first is immigration. Although far from alone in this, Musk has advocated for sharply limiting migration to the West from nonwhite areas of the world. He has helped make post-apartheid South Africa a kind of poster child for this, pushing Trump to ease entry to the United States for members of South Africa’s white minority, whom Washington now pretends are the victims of gross persecution.

Musk’s other big, stealthy public policy play involves the future of Social Security and taxation. Many projections suggest that at current payout rates, the United States’ Social Security system will soon run out of funds, requiring some combination of postponed retirement for millions, greatly reduced benefits, or increased taxation. This is where the ultrarich would step in, creating further government dependence on them.

Musk recently announced that he would give one share of his SpaceX company stock to every U.S. child, a gesture whose value is estimated to be worth as much as $11 billion. Analysts have pointed out that, far from altruism, this would save Musk billions in tax. Other AI-related giants such as Anthropic and Dell, increasingly an AI services provider, have also begun to offer stock or cash donation programs.

Although Musk has not said as much, this also seems likely to be part of a bid by the ultra-wealthy to secure a powerful and potentially lucrative position for themselves in the looming struggle over the U.S. retirement system, via a kind of creeping privatization—a long-standing objective in conservative politics. By donating shares or providing child allowances, these individuals and their companies can profit not only from write-offs, but also perhaps through the management of financial services and fostering government reliance on their mega-corporations, much as the U.S. military depends on Musk’s Starlink.

A historical consensus has already begun to form around the idea that the increasing concentration of wealth, along with the rise of historically unprecedented fortunes stemming from AI and the broader internet and computer economy, is driving a decisive shift in the politics and economy of the United States, along with its democracy. On the country’s present path, the risk this presents is what political scientists and economists call state capture: a blurring of the lines between private and public interests, in which the former gradually predominates.

If the diagnosis is correct, notions about what to do about this have been much slower to coalesce, and that is no accident. Just this week, news emerged that in response to a proposed new tax on the ultrarich in California, Meta founder Mark Zuckerberg was considering leaving the state, where his company is based, and then hastily arranged changes in his corporate compensation. Despite being one of the world’s richest men, Zuckerberg has long received a mere $1 in annual pay from Meta, with the bulk of his fortune coming in the form of company shares. With California considering taxing assets like these instead of income alone, Zuckerberg got his board to create a new class of nonvoting shares that he could sell to pay any new state tax bill without diluting his controlling interest in the company. Other AI-related business leaders in California, where the industry is largely centered, have contributed to a campaign against the new tax measure, Proposition 40, which residents will vote on in November.

Jeff Bezos, the founder of Amazon, has found other ways to wield his influence. Like Zuckerberg and other mega-billionaires, Bezos has contributed to some of Trump’s pet Washington construction projects. Bezos also owns the Washington Post. Once considered a close rival to the New York Times in terms of the breadth and aggressiveness of its government accountability journalism, the Post has undergone a transformation under Bezos, shedding staff and running far softer features than before.

As this has happened, the newspaper’s editorial pages have become openly pro-business, including the AI industry, and thereby much less concerned with speaking truth to power. Bezos himself announced the change in February 2025: “We are going to be writing every day in support and defense of two pillars: personal liberties and free markets. We’ll cover other topics too of course, but viewpoints opposing those pillars will be left to be published by others.”

It is hard to see Bezos’s decisions as separate from his interest in currying favor with the White House, given his company’s huge business dealings with the U.S. government and exposure to state regulation.

It is Musk, though, who has been the least inhibited about flexing his influence over government. In the run-up to next month’s midterm elections, in which Democrats are widely expected to take control of the House of Representatives, and possibly win the Senate, Musk recently issued a threat to anyone in Congress with ideas of investigating him. “Those who pick fights with me should understand that I will find out who is really pulling the strings and come for them,” he wrote on X.

This is a brazen statement, but Musk may feel he has reason enough to believe in his impunity. The man who has become the largest campaign financier in U.S. politics, to the tune of hundreds of millions of dollars in campaign donations to Republican candidates, likely sees the struggle for influence over the government as a long game in which his wealth and that of an allied class of new AI billionaires can be decisive. The question—and it is a fateful one—is whether the country’s civil society, and along with it, its democracy, can hold its own.

Howard W. French is a columnist at Foreign Policy, a professor at the Columbia University Graduate School of Journalism, and a longtime foreign correspondent. His latest book is The Second Emancipation: Nkrumah, Pan-Africanism, and Global Blackness at High Tide. Bluesky: @hofrench.bsky.social X: @hofrench

#5704. Kamala Harris calls Trump administration 'most callous and corrupt' in American history

While stumping for Wisconsin's Democratic gubernatorial nominee David Crowley, former Vice President Kamala Harris cast the Trump administration as a brutal government that is threatening key democratic norms.

While on the campaign trail Monday for Democrats ahead of the midterms, former Vice President Kamala Harris offered some of her harshest criticism of the Trump White House yet, calling it the "most callous and corrupt and incompetent" presidential administration in American history.

Twice during her speech at an event for David Crowley, the Democratic nominee for governor in Wisconsin, Harris accused the Trump administration of wielding an agenda of "brutality" against Americans.

Though in her lambasting of President Donald Trump's character and leadership style, she didn't mention him by name once.

"We in this room, I know, understand that the measure of the strength of the leader is based on who you lift up, not who you beat down," Harris said. "Unlike some other people who are constantly looking in the mirror, David is looking at the people. That's the kind of leader we want."

#5703. BMW’s first three-row electric SUV is almost here: Here’s an early look at the iX7

BMW is preparing to introduce its first three-row electric SUV. With its official debut coming up, the iX7 was spotted testing, offering a sneak peek at the full-size luxury SUV.

The iX7 is the electric version of BMW’s largest and most luxurious SUV. Although BMW has yet to announce it officially, disguised prototypes were recently caught testing at the Nürburgring in Germany.

After the iX3 and iX5, the iX7 is the next BMW SUV in line to debut with its Neue Klasse technology and design.

Like the iX5, BMW’s three-row electric SUV will retain a more traditional style than the full-on Neue Klasse EVs, such as the iX3.

While it’s still covered, the image posted on Reddit is from SHProshots. You can see the grille appears much wider and chunkier than the slim, upright updated Kidney Grille design on the iX3. It also features split headlights, similar to the current X7, rather than the wider headlights on Neue Klasse models.

The iX7 also drops the conventional pull-style door handles in favor of small handles located on the vehicle’s pillar.

BMW will reveal details closer to its debut, expected in mid-2027. Like the smaller iX5 60 xDrive, the iX7 is expected to use a dual-motor all-wheel-drive powertrain. The iX5’s AWD setup delivers a combined 578 hp (425 kW) and 593 lb-ft of torque, which BMW said enables it to hit 0 to 60 mph in 4.4 seconds.

Powered by a 144 kWh battery, BMW said the iX5 will offer up to 435 miles of range. It will also use an 800V electrical system for fast charging.

During a media event in Southern California this week, BMW, IONNA, and Alpitronic showcased the iX5 60 xDrive hitting a peak of 464 kW. BMW said that with up to 460 kW peak charging, the iX5 60 xDrive can add up to 170 miles of range in 10 minutes.

The iX7 is expected to go into production at BMW’s Spartanburg plant in the US in late 2027, joining the new iX5.
