#5701. Ukraine Can Turn Imminent Peril to Its Advantage

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ERIC CIARAMELLA is a Senior Fellow at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace and previously served at the National Security Council, National Intelligence Council, and Central Intelligence Agency.

How a True “Energy Cease-Fire” Can Curb Putin

Ukraine is facing its most precarious moment since the early days of the war. Since late July, Russia has used its growing stockpile of ballistic missiles and a new generation of jet-powered drones to dramatically intensify its long-range attacks. It is now targeting Ukrainian population centers all over the country, with a focus on logistics, transportation, and commercial infrastructure. According to Ukrainian intelligence reports, the Russian government is planning an even more intense campaign against energy and water supplies this winter in an attempt to decisively break Ukraine’s economy and society.

On October 9, U.S. President Donald Trump seemed to make Ukraine’s situation even more perilous when he announced a deal with Moscow to unilaterally lift sanctions on Russian diesel and called on Ukraine to suspend strikes on Russian oil refineries. Those strikes have been one of Ukraine’s most potent forms of counterpressure in recent months. But Trump’s unexpected announcement is not necessarily a major setback for Ukraine. It comes amid a broader shift by the Trump administration in recent weeks to move away from pursuing an elusive comprehensive peace agreement in favor of a mutual cease-fire on strikes against energy infrastructure—a shift that could ultimately be beneficial to Kyiv. Moreover, in his angry retort to Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky’s criticism of the diesel deal, Trump revealed a potential way forward: as long as Ukraine stopped hitting Russian refineries, the president said, “you can do whatever you want to Russia.”

Ukraine should take Trump at his word. If upheld by Moscow as well as Kyiv, an energy cease-fire would be a crucial step toward containing the war. Framed in the right way, it would also leave open other forms of pressure by Ukraine that could help compel Russian compliance. Ideally, a reciprocal halt in long-range air and missile attacks could then be replicated across other sectors—maritime, commercial, and transportation—thus forming the basis of a broader aerial cease-fire.

Thus far, Russian President Vladimir Putin has given no indication that he is prepared to halt or even curtail his strike campaign. He sees the widespread destruction of Ukrainian cities and infrastructure as his most direct path to victory. But Putin is also under pressure from the widening war. In part, his accelerating air campaign reflects weakness on the frontlines, where Russia has achieved negligible progress throughout 2026 and Ukraine has even begun to make some gains in the Donbas. Russia also faces growing costs at home from Ukraine’s long-range strikes, which have disrupted energy and commercial infrastructure and heightened public anxiety over a prolonged war.

The United States and Europe can capitalize on Russia’s mounting challenges if they allow Ukraine to apply targeted pressure to convince Moscow to agree to pause or significantly limit its reckless air campaign. To be effective, this approach needs to be combined with focused diplomacy aimed at that specific objective. In fact, there are tools available for this purpose, and the barriers to putting them quickly into play are far more surmountable than has been commonly recognized.

The key will be credibly signaling that critical Russian military and economic assets will be at risk if Russian forces continue pummeling Ukraine’s civilian economy and society. Only then might Putin conclude that his current approach has reached the point of diminishing returns—and then pivot, as he has done at other junctures in the war. At the same time, this diplomatic play must be hitched to a broader effort by Kyiv’s Western partners to help it urgently shore up its air defenses and finances ahead of winter. Such a strategy may not immediately succeed, but failing to try would all but ensure that Ukraine suffers an extraordinary degree of damage that will be much harder to reverse.

The Trump administration’s repeated failure to advance its stated goal of brokering peace between Russia and Ukraine has led many observers to write off diplomacy as a waste of time. Over the past 21 months, the Trump administration has gotten nowhere in its attempts to mediate a comprehensive peace settlement. Russia’s war aims and Ukraine’s requirements—on such thorny issues as territory, Ukrainian security arrangements, and Russia’s future relations with the West—are simply too far apart. Washington also missed opportunities to build meaningful leverage over Russia, instead lavishly courting it with potential commercial deals and other incentives while pressuring Kyiv to accept Moscow’s conditions.

Yet that experience has helped drive the administration’s current efforts to achieve more plausible and narrowly defined goals. Following their visits to Moscow and Kyiv in early September, Trump’s envoys, Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff, pivoted toward a more limited energy cease-fire. Since then, the president has also reiterated his support for such a deal. Trump’s interest in an energy cease-fire is largely self-serving—he has attributed spikes in fuel prices to Ukraine’s strikes on Russian oil refineries—but this shift could serve Kyiv well, especially if it becomes the basis for a coordinated drive to secure broader reciprocal limits on air and missile attacks.

The core challenge is convincing Russia to embrace such an agreement. To date, Putin has rejected calls for restrictions on aerial strikes of any sort. He views the damage he is inflicting on Ukraine as outstripping the pain Russia is sustaining from Kyiv’s attacks. But the United States and European countries have several ways to change this calculus. Crucially, the United States and Europe must enable Ukraine to strike missile launchers and other military targets inside Russia—or at least allow it to credibly threaten to do so. Thus far, Ukraine has largely focused its long-range attacks on energy infrastructure, because targeting Russia’s hardened military installations and mobile launch systems is technically much more complicated. But it is exactly these harder-to-replace assets that Putin is most concerned about losing.

In fact, it would not be difficult for the United States to facilitate strikes on military assets. For months, Ukraine has pressed the Trump administration and the SpaceX CEO Elon Musk to enable Starlink access over a swath of Russian territory opposite the Ukrainian border from which most ballistic missiles are launched. Kyiv needs such access to allow its drone teams to target mobile launchers. As soon as Ukraine acquires this ability, Russia would likely be compelled to pull the rest of these systems farther away from the border, thereby considerably reducing the ballistic threat to Ukraine. Thus far, Trump and Musk have both deflected Ukraine’s request—each one claiming the decision rests with the other. But neither has foreclosed greenlighting such access.

Kyiv’s case for acquiring Starlink access over Russia might be more persuasive if Ukrainian officials focused on how it could be deployed as negotiating leverage, as part of a wider U.S.-led diplomatic strategy to secure an aerial cease-fire—especially if it were an element of an agreement with Washington to refrain from striking Russian oil refineries. Specifically, Trump could authorize access without public fanfare while signaling to Moscow that he is doing so only until an aerial cease-fire is in place. There is reason to think such a tactic would succeed. Earlier this year, Musk worked with Ukraine to create a registration system that effectively limited Starlink use to Ukrainian drone operators, a step that for a short period sent Russian forces into operational disarray along the front. Washington missed a chance to exploit that moment of Russian weakness to push Moscow toward a tactical de-escalation, however, because U.S. attention shifted just weeks later to the war against Iran.

The German government has a similar card to play. Kyiv has long sought to get Germany’s Taurus air-launched cruise missile, which would allow it to target fixed sites hundreds of miles into Russia, such as defense plants that produce jet-powered drones and ballistic missiles. Thus far, German Chancellor Friedrich Merz has resisted sending the Taurus system to Ukraine, arguing that it would be escalatory and would require a level of on-site technical assistance that would make German forces party to the conflict. But Merz might be persuaded to relent—or at least to authorize Ukrainian troops to start training on the system—if he believed that doing so would create momentum toward an aerial cease-fire, ideally in lockstep with a move from Trump on Starlink. In turn, other European partners could also contribute to such a plan by leaning in more with intelligence and technical expertise to enable Ukraine to target launchers inside Russia.

Any such leveraged diplomacy, of course, will depend on Ukraine’s partners showing that they remain committed to addressing Ukraine’s immediate war needs and that Russia will not succeed in its stepped-up campaign to break the country this winter. And this means having a coherent immediate strategy for shoring up both Kyiv’s air defenses and its economy, irrespective of U.S. involvement or support.

Above all is the question of Patriot interceptors. For Ukraine, the Patriot’s PAC-3 variant is by far the most effective protection against Russian ballistic missile attacks. Yet Kyiv’s stockpile has dried up as the United States husbands an arsenal that has been significantly depleted by the Iran war. Kyiv’s European partners have been scouring the world for Patriot missiles but have so far been unable to meet Ukraine’s modest request of 300 interceptors to get through the winter. Countries with robust Patriot stocks, such as Greece, Japan, South Korea, and Spain, have been reluctant to transfer them to Ukraine for a variety of security and legal reasons.

Here, Europe should take a page from an earlier U.S. playbook. During the Biden administration, U.S. officials cajoled recalcitrant partners and engineered creative multiparty swaps and backfill agreements—many of them subsidized by the United States—to free up critical munitions for Ukraine. For example, the United States bought 155-millimeter artillery shells from South Korea and PAC-3 interceptors from Japan to backfill American munitions for Ukraine. This time around, Ukraine can finance the purchases itself through the loan package it is receiving from the EU.

On the economic front, Ukraine’s most acute needs could be met if the EU agreed to frontload the disbursement of the tranches of its 90 billion euro loan that are scheduled for next year. EU leaders have made clear that Kyiv must first meet Brussels’ reform conditions to secure the tranches of the loan already promised this year. But the EU will also most likely need to find additional resources because the loan itself, expected to last through 2027, will fall short of meeting Ukraine’s needs.

In August, the Netherlands, Poland, Spain, and Sweden again proposed the option of tapping into Russia’s frozen sovereign assets, an estimated $240 billion of which reside in the EU, mostly in the Belgium-based financial services company Euroclear. The legal and political obstacles to taking custody of those assets for onward transfer to Ukraine may be too formidable to resolve in time to help Kyiv in the coming year. But Ukraine’s partners have come up with creative solutions in the past. In 2024, the G-7 designed a $50 billion loan to be serviced by the future windfall profits generated by the frozen Russian assets. The EU’s 90 billion euro loan is another creative example: it is financed from EU borrowing on capital markets, to be repaid by Ukraine only if Russia pays reparations; otherwise, the EU has reserved the right to reimburse itself with the frozen Russian assets. The EU and likeminded partners—notably Canada, Japan, and the United Kingdom—can revisit and expand both mechanisms to generate additional funding for Ukraine while steering clear of political poison pills.

Ultimately, each of these steps needs to be coordinated to have maximum influence on Putin’s thinking. The fact that the Trump administration invited top European officials to join last week’s talks with Ukrainian negotiators in Miami is a sign that such transatlantic coordination is possible, even if Washington’s ties with Europe are otherwise strained. On the other hand, reports that the Trump administration has threatened to cut off intelligence support and supplies of air defense missiles and other critical munitions for Ukraine underscore the reality that Washington is at best a capricious interlocutor. In any case, European leaders who have constructive working relations with the White House, such as Finnish President Alexander Stubb, should continue arguing that granting Starlink access, among other steps to pressure Moscow, would advance Trump’s stated aim of de-escalating the conflict.

Putin is committed to his overall war aims, and no amount of external pressure is likely to force him to abandon those. But he is an opportunist and has shown himself adept at making tactical adjustments in pursuit of his broader ambitions. In early 2022, he gave up his ground attack on Kyiv when it became evident that it would fail. Later, he pulled the Black Sea Fleet away from Ukraine’s shores after Kyiv proved it could sink valuable Russian naval assets; in 2022, he also entered the Black Sea Grain Initiative, which for nearly a year allowed the safe passage of grain shipments from Ukrainian ports and was brokered by the United Nations and Turkey, under pressure from African nations suffering from grain shortages.

For Putin, these were not goodwill gestures but measures of self-preservation designed to advance his broader aim of subjugating Ukraine. With the right kind of leverage applied by the United States and Europe, there is no reason to assume he would rule out a similar pullback from attacks on Ukrainian civilian populations and economic infrastructure. He would be even more inclined to view an aerial cease-fire as advantageous if he saw an expansion of Ukraine’s ability to upend life in Russia’s major population centers—a goal Kyiv no doubt is intent on pursuing, as shown by its effective recent strikes on Russian data centers.

Of course, there is the possibility that, even faced with these new costs, Putin will still refuse to budge. But in that scenario, enabling Ukraine to target Russian missile launchers and defense production sites—combined with continued economic pressure—will be even more essential to helping Ukraine counter Russian escalation. In addition to relieving immediate pressure on Ukrainian cities by making it harder for Russia to launch strikes, it will also undercut Russia’s military capacity. That would be beneficial even if it falls short of containing the air war.

Paradoxically, the adaptability that has enabled many of Putin’s successes also gives Kyiv and the West more space to maneuver than they realize. Trump, Zelensky, and European leaders need to organize their actions in a way that relieves pressure on the Ukrainian economy while showing growing willingness to exploit critical Russian vulnerabilities. Only then might Putin consider that he is better off making a tactical adjustment—in this case, agreeing to an aerial cease-fire—than maintaining the present course. He would do so not to concede but to find a more effective way to achieve his broader aims. In that adjustment period, Kyiv and its partners will gain urgently needed breathing space to regroup, fortify, and prepare for the next round.

#5700. Netanyahu Orders Large-Scale Military Operation in West Bank

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: Netanyahu Orders Large-Scale Military Operation in West Bank

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at Israeli military action in the West Bank, Taiwan bracing for Typhoon Champi, and a powerful earthquake in Tibet.

Two Palestinian gunmen opened fire on an Israeli military checkpoint near the occupied West Bank city of Hebron on Friday, killing two soldiers and injuring three others. Israeli forces said that the militants were linked to Hamas, which praised the attack. In response, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu vowed to carry out an extensive military operation in the West Bank “unlike any seen since the Six-Day War” in 1967.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at Israeli military action in the West Bank, Taiwan bracing for Typhoon Champi, and a powerful earthquake in Tibet.

Two Palestinian gunmen opened fire on an Israeli military checkpoint near the occupied West Bank city of Hebron on Friday, killing two soldiers and injuring three others. Israeli forces said that the militants were linked to Hamas, which praised the attack. In response, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu vowed to carry out an extensive military operation in the West Bank “unlike any seen since the Six-Day War” in 1967.

“We will act to destroy this place, to change it completely,” Netanyahu said, ordering a full siege on Hebron and the complete closure of the West Bank starting Friday. Within hours, Netanyahu appeared to scale back such plans for the territory by saying that he would consider “a cordon” around the cities of Hebron and Halhul. However, he stopped short of rescinding the campaign completely, instead vowing to reprise a strategy used in the West Bank city of Jenin.

In early 2025, Israeli troops launched a broad campaign against militant groups in three refugee camps around Jenin, demolishing hundreds of homes and displacing thousands of Palestinians.

“Many viewed the timing as deliberate—a political lifeline for Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu, whose grip on power relies on appeasing his far-right coalition, delaying his corruption trial, and quelling public anger over the unresolved hostage crisis in Gaza,” Dalia Hatuqa wrote in Foreign Policy at the time.

After Friday’s shooting, Netanyahu characterized the refugee camps around Hebron as “essentially terrorist strongholds.”

“We will evacuate the residents, go house to house, remove weapons found there, and address and destroy all terrorist infrastructure,” he said.

Netanyahu is facing one of his biggest political challenges to date. On Tuesday, Israel will hold parliamentary elections that many analysts have called a referendum on Netanyahu’s leadership, including his handling of the wars in Gaza, Lebanon, and Iran.

To win, Netanyahu will likely need the support of the hard right, including voters who want to see Israel annex the West Bank outright.

Netanyahu vowed on Friday to bolster Israeli settlements in the West Bank, which are illegal under international law. “You are a role model for the people of Israel, you are a role model for the State of Israel,” Netanyahu told Israeli settlers in the West Bank after the attack. “We are going to destroy the terror infrastructures, and we are going to build the settlement infrastructures.”

Typhoon Champi makes landfall. Much of Taiwan’s public services shut down on Friday in preparation for one of the strongest storms to hit the Pacific Ocean this year. Typhoon Champi skirted along northeastern Taiwan late Friday local time and is expected to move toward China and southern Japan in the coming days.

Already, Champi has killed at least one person, injured around 160 others, forced some 2,600 residents to evacuate, and plunged more than 77,000 homes into darkness. Meteorologists have recorded wind surges of upward of 119 miles per hour and rainfall measuring as much as 3 feet in some places. Taiwan’s Transportation Ministry canceled nearly 1,000 flights on Friday in response.

Scientists have argued that this year’s supercharged El Niño fueled Champi’s ferocity. According to a new United Nations climate report published on Wednesday, Earth is on track to warm an average of 2.6 degrees Celsius by the end of this century compared to preindustrial times if all countries meet their climate pledges under the Paris Agreement. This is considered unlikely and still falls far above the temperature that experts argue is needed to avoid the most detrimental impacts of climate change.

“Every additional fraction of a degree and additional year of warming above 1.5°C increases climate impacts and risks, such as the deadly floods, heatwaves and wildfires that have plagued many nations this year,” said Inger Andersen, the executive director of the U.N. Environment Program.

Tremors in Tibet. A 7.0 magnitude earthquake hit the Chinese region of Tibet on Thursday, killing at least 16 people and injuring more than 70 others. Local authorities deployed hundreds of police officers and other volunteers to the quake’s epicenter on Friday to oversee rescue and recovery efforts, including the provision of shelter, food, and clothing. Nearly 340 homes were destroyed in the city of Nagqu, and more than 12,000 people have already been forced to evacuate.

According to Chinese state media, Thursday’s quake was the strongest in the area in the past five years. Although Tibet is sparsely populated compared to other Chinese regions, Beijing has already warned locals of further damage from aftershocks, rockfalls, and intermittent snowfall in the coming days. Communication lines were damaged across some parts of Tibet following the earthquake, and local railway authorities have issued emergency actions to stop trains from passing through the affected area.

Tibet is considered particularly vulnerable to earthquakes and other natural disasters. The region is one of the poorest in China, hindering its infrastructure development. And Tibet is still reeling from a devastating flash flood along its border with Nepal in August that killed more than 40 people in Tibet and left some 500 others missing. It remains unclear precisely how badly the floods hurt Tibet, as the Chinese government censored independent coverage of the incident.

Lula vs. Flávio. Brazil’s presidential election runoff is just two days away. Left-wing incumbent President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva is up against far-right Sen. Flávio Bolsonaro, whose father is serving a more than 27-year prison sentence for orchestrating a failed coup against Lula in 2022. Flávio himself has come under recent scrutiny over a major corruption scandal tying him to the man behind the biggest banking fraud in the country’s history.

According to Brazilian pollster Datafolha, Lula and Bolsonaro are tied at 50 percent of valid votes ahead of this weekend’s election, with the senator up by just 1 percentage point in total votes. Bolsonaro narrowly beat out Lula in the election’s first round earlier this month—securing 47.03 percent of the vote versus the president’s 45.16 percent. The two men square off Friday evening in a final televised debate.

A Bolsonaro win would add Brazil to the long list of far-right governments across Latin America. It would also amount to a win for U.S. President Donald Trump, who has backed both Bolsonaro and his father over the years.

Mauritius ended a curfew on Monday that was put in place last week due to escalating demonstrations protesting the rising cost of living and what other issue?

A. Water shortages B. Dilapidated public school buildings C. A recent policy to ration energy D. A series of high-profile food recalls

#5699. Illinois man accused of threatening Congress with white substances in letters marked with his return address

A letter sent to a congressperson in Illinois containing an unknown white substance (U.S. District Court in the Central District of Illinois)

An Illinois man was charged with two felonies Monday after allegedly sending letters containing an unknown white powder substance to members of Congress, according to a criminal complaint.

Matthew G. Unglesbee was charged with false information and hoaxes and mailing threatening communications after four members of Congress received the letters, which were marked with his return address, according to the complaint.

Unglesbee told the FBI the substance in the letters was salt and that he mailed them as a joke, the complaint read.

The FBI received a tip that Unglesbee had sent the letters on Oct. 16. The tipster had overheard Unglesbee at a restaurant saying he had mailed suspicious substances to members of Congress, the complaint stated.

The tipster allegedly provided authorities with Unglesbee’s full name, address and date of birth.

Members of the FBI’s field office in Chicago responded to the offices of three members of Congress in the northern Illinois area after receiving the tip and a 911 call from one of the congressional offices.

FBI Chicago field office. (AFP PHOTO/Stan HONDA via Getty Images)

All three letters contained "a white granular substance" and a handwritten letter which read "HA HA," the complaint stated.

The FBI interviewed Unglesbee at his home Saturday when he allegedly confessed to sending the letters, telling law enforcement that he heard someone in a restaurant talking about similar letters Congress members had received.

#5697. Tesla (TSLA) releases Q3 2026 financial results: margins rebound, profit still falls

Tesla (TSLA) released its financial results and shareholders’ letter for the third quarter (Q3) 2026 after market close today. We’re updating this post with all the details from the financial results, shareholders’ letter, and the conference call later tonight.

The earnings call with management is scheduled for 5:30 p.m. ET.

Wall Street came into the report expecting a flat quarter. The FactSet consensus, as displayed on Estimize, called for $28.06 billion in revenue, essentially unchanged from the $28.1 billion Tesla posted in Q3 2025 and a bit below the $28.24 billion it reported last quarter.

Profit expectations were lower. Analysts were looking for non-GAAP earnings of $0.45 per share, down 10% from $0.50 a year ago. The Estimize crowd was slightly more bullish at $28.36 billion and $0.48.

We already had the volume numbers. Tesla delivered 486,532 vehicles in Q3, down 2.1% year-over-year but well ahead of the 461,974 consensus, while producing only 464,391. It also deployed 13.7 GWh of energy storage, up from 12.5 GWh a year ago but short of the roughly 15.9 GWh analysts expected. For the full setup, see our Q3 2026 earnings preview.

So the question was never volume. It was whether Tesla could stop the profit slide after Q2, when operating margin fell to 1.4% and EPS missed the consensus by 20 cents.

After the market closed today, Tesla released its financial results for the third quarter and reported $28.05 billion in revenue, flat from a year ago and in line with the $28.06 billion Wall Street expected. Non-GAAP earnings came in at $0.48 per share, beating the $0.45 consensus but down 4% from $0.50 a year ago.

It’s a better quarter than Q2, but the trend hasn’t changed. Margins recovered, with auto gross margin excluding credits back above 18%, yet operating income still fell 56% year-over-year as spending on AI and Optimus grew faster than gross profit. On a GAAP basis, net income dropped 43% to $787 million, or $0.22 per share.

Total gross margin was 19.6%, up 160 basis points from 18.0% a year ago and well above the 16.8% Tesla posted in Q2. Operating income fell 56% to $718 million, for an operating margin of 2.6%, compared with 5.8% a year ago and 1.4% last quarter.

Operating expenses came in at $4.78 billion, up 39% year-over-year. Research and development alone jumped 69% to $2.76 billion as Tesla spends on what it now calls “Super Intelligence” (SI), Optimus, and the Cybercab. Stock-based compensation, which includes the 2025 CEO pay package, hit $1.17 billion, up from $663 million a year ago.

Capital spending was $4.73 billion, more than double the $2.25 billion of a year ago, and free cash flow came in at $1.53 billion, compared with $3.99 billion a year ago and negative $1.09 billion in Q2. Tesla ended the quarter with $45.4 billion in cash and investments. GAAP net income included a $288 million unrealized gain on Tesla’s bitcoin holdings, partly offset by a $142 million unrealized loss on its SpaceX equity investment.

That puts Tesla’s capex at $13.01 billion through the first nine months of 2026, only about half of the “more than $25 billion” management guided for the year. To hit that number, Tesla would need to spend roughly $12 billion in Q4 alone, more than double its biggest quarter so far. The Q3 letter doesn’t update the guidance, and capex actually fell sequentially from $5.79 billion in Q2. So either Q4 spending explodes, or the 2026 plan is quietly slipping into 2027. The lower spending also flatters this quarter’s positive free cash flow.

The number we watch first is automotive gross margin excluding regulatory credits, now that the credit market has mostly dried up. It came in at 18.1% this quarter, up from 16.3% in Q2 and 15.4% a year ago, though still below the 19.2% of Q1, when Tesla got help from warranty and tariff one-time benefits. Tesla credits a better regional and product mix and more FSD subscription revenue, partly offset by lower average selling prices.

Regulatory credits brought in just $113 million, down 73% from $417 million a year ago and below the $146 million in Q2. That line isn’t coming back now that automakers no longer pay federal fuel-economy penalties.

Energy was the weak spot on revenue. Energy generation and storage revenue fell 16% to $2.88 billion, which Tesla partly blames on tariff-related customer refunds, even though deployments rose 10% to 13.7 GWh. The segment’s gross margin jumped to 33% from 20% in Q2, but energy gross profit still slipped to $954 million from $1.07 billion a year ago.

Services and other was the bright spot. Revenue grew 33% to $4.61 billion, and Tesla says gross profit hit a record $734 million on charging, insurance and parts sales.

Tesla says “battery availability continued to be the limiting factor in increasing auto volumes in the quarter,” and that it ended Q3 with “the largest backlog for our passenger vehicles in four years.”

That’s a new explanation for flat sales. Deliveries were still down 2% year-over-year, “other models” (Cybertruck, Semi, Model S and X) fell 48%, and Tesla delivered 22,141 more cars than it built, bringing inventory down to 11 days of supply from 15 in Q2. But average selling prices fell again, which isn’t usually what a supply-constrained automaker does.

On autonomy, Tesla says active FSD subscriptions reached 1.74 million, up 67%, though that count includes upfront purchases, as defined in Musk’s 2025 pay package. Its Robotaxi service reached nearly 2 million cumulative unsupervised miles by the end of the quarter, a month after Cybercab joined the Austin fleet.

I am still waiting for the 50% YOY growth rate that Musk touted a few years ago to justify the TSLA valuation. Then I reminded myself that TSLA is just the largest MEME stock in the market day.

Tesla’s full Q3 2026 shareholder deck is available on the company’s investor relations site, and management will discuss the results on the earnings call at 5:30 p.m. ET.

This is a developing story. We’ll add the most important updates from Tesla’s shareholder letter and earnings call here as they come.

#5696. A Democratic Reckoning in India

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

PRATAP BHANU MEHTA is University Lecturer at Princeton University and a Senior Fellow at the Centre for Policy Research. He is a co-editor of The Oxford Handbook of the Indian Constitution.

The Battle on the Streets Over the Foundations of the State

India is in rebellion. At stake is the very future of the country’s democracy and the potential of its renewal. The mass protests that have unfolded in New Delhi since July and flared again this month are nothing less than a contest over the foundations of the Indian state and whether it lives up to its constitutional commitments.

Demonstrations began in the summer as students decried perceived unfairness in the country’s examination system. They have now widened to a struggle over the integrity of India’s elections, after authorities began to purge over 130 million people from draft voting lists over the last year. Many of those stripped from the electoral rolls are appealing their removal—an often laborious process—but the possibility of mass disenfranchisement is real. Tens of thousands of mostly young people have taken to the streets in New Delhi in the past week, with protests now spreading to other major cities. The demonstrators are demanding the resignation of India’s chief election commissioner and questioning the credibility of India’s ostensibly independent institutions. And they are holding Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi directly responsible, accusing him of “stealing democracy.” In their rejection of the country’s current trajectory, the protests pose the most significant challenge to Modi’s authority since the prime minister came to power in 2014.

The main opposition parties, which have never come close to dislodging Modi in national parliamentary elections, put up a united front under Rahul Gandhi, the leader of the Congress party, and an impressive show of force at a rally in New Delhi on October 12. But the real heart of the protest is the intense political energy and inventiveness shown by Gen Z students. These young people have defied prohibitions on protest, breached draconian security clampdowns, and endured detentions to relentlessly question the government’s authority.

The government, for its part, also sees this confrontation as an existential battle. This is evident in its determination to contain the protests, deploying over 40,000 security personnel, closing down metro and train stations, barricading central New Delhi, suspending mobile Internet, and detaining over 7,000 people. It has doggedly defended the chief of the Election Commission and it has refused to talk to the opposition or the youthful protesters about their demands.

Modi is in no danger of falling; this will not be a rerun of recent Gen Z protests in neighboring Bangladesh and Nepal that successfully toppled governments. But the Indian government faces a subtler danger: its writ no longer carries unequivocal sway. A strongman such as Modi likes to project unquestioned personal authority. Since 2014, his ruling Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) has consolidated and centralized power to an unprecedented degree. For the government, the open defiance of young people—who come from all class, religious, and caste backgrounds and cannot easily be dismissed or stigmatized as belonging to an “antinational” group—is an unprecedented challenge. It makes the Modi government’s once adamantine grip on power seem more fragile. The future of Indian democracy hinges on whether this contest opens up more democratic space or leads to greater repression.

Modern India is founded on two legitimizing institutions: elections and exams. Since its independence in 1947, India has maintained a reputation for conducting free and fair elections. The Election Commission of India, an independent constitutional body, has sweeping powers to ensure that the polls remain beyond reproach. The credibility of the commission has been central to the credibility of Indian democracy.

India also conducts recruitment for public-sector jobs and admissions to public and several private colleges through competitive national exams. For millions of young people, the stakes in these exams are high. Success can send candidates scrambling up the ladder of opportunity, but failure can consign them to never climbing it at all. It is important, therefore, that these exams be conducted fairly and have the imprimatur of honesty and impartiality.

India remains a doggedly unequal society in which money, kinship, and power distort almost all institutions. In this context, elections and exams are the only two processes that make the state’s promise of equal treatment tangible. Elections give even the poorest Indians the prospect of dignity and agency, and as a result, voter turnout rates are routinely high. Exams sustain a parallel promise: they keep alive the idea that academic diligence and performance can trump other forms of social capital. To be sure, the staging of both elections and exams has never been perfect, but the overall integrity of the processes has never been in doubt.

Over the last year, however, the actions of the Indian government have cast doubt on elections and exams. In 2024, the main nationwide entrance examination for medical schools was engulfed in controversy over a slew of irregularities, including the advance leak of the exam’s questions. Protests ensued, and students raised serious questions about the reliability of the examination system. The episode also put the National Testing Agency, the central body that under Modi had become responsible for conducting many important exams, under intense scrutiny. Centralization was meant to ensure greater efficiency and regularity. Instead, it made the crisis deeper by concentrating responsibility at the level of the federal government. In July, antigovernment protests forced the resignation of the education minister over frustrations with the examination system.

Similarly, over the last year or so, the Election Commission decided to undertake what is known as a Special Intensive Revision, or SIR, an assessment of India’s voter lists to ensure their accuracy. The exercise is well within the powers of the election commission; the last such revision was conducted between 2002 and 2004. But the process has become quite controversial this time because of concerns that it places excessive burdens on citizens to prove their eligibility to vote; the lack of transparency about why names are being added or deleted from the lists; and the use of new software that has given the Election Commission the ability to strip citizens from the voter rolls on trivial grounds, often overriding established procedures and the recommendations of those local officials who do the actual verification.

According to his critics, Gyanesh Kumar, the chief election commissioner, acted unilaterally and recklessly in pursuing the revision of voter rolls in ways that violated normal procedures. Since June 2025, the SIR has tagged roughly 130 million names for removal from the voter rolls. Although many of the deletions were duplicates and the names of people who had died or left the country, millions of Indians have found themselves stripped of the right to vote and now burdened with the difficult task of appealing to a tribunal to prove their citizenship. In this light, the protests have already had one constructive effect: the Election Commission is trying to placate its critics by making it easier for those excluded to lodge appeals.

Gandhi has alleged that the SIR is an instrument for manipulating elections. Already, in the state of West Bengal, where nine million names were taken off, millions of Indians found themselves unable to participate in state elections in March because their appeals were yet to be heard. The elections brought Modi’s BJP to power in the state for the first time. But even if the Election Commission is not systematically targeting voters based on their identity or their political preferences, the exercise still risks disenfranchising millions through sheer ineptitude.

To make matters worse, India’s Supreme Court, another vital democratic institution, has also played an unfortunate role in this train of events. The Supreme Court effectively collaborated in the disenfranchisement of voters in West Bengal by blithely claiming that those whose petitions were under appeal could just vote in the next election after their appeals had been adjudicated; it didn’t matter if they missed voting in the current election.

The sense of the Supreme Court’s callousness was compounded in May after Surya Kant, the chief justice, compared a group of young lawyers pressing for change to “cockroaches.” That stray remark precipitated a social movement, with the creation—initially as a joke but then in earnest—of the “Cockroach Janata Party,” a group that has galvanized young Indians both on social media and in the streets. It played a key role in forcing the resignation of Dharmendra Pradhan, the education minister, in July, after mass protests in New Delhi and across the country. It is now driving young Indians to call for the resignation of Kumar, the chief election commissioner.

Most significantly, it has linked the credibility crisis directly to Modi, encouraging a veritable explosion of public mockery of the prime minister on social media and the Internet. That has punctured Modi’s carefully cultivated image of an invincible leader who delivers on every promise. He has prided himself on his dominance of social media, but the loss of control over the political narrative, especially on the image-sharing platform Instagram, was palpable. The prime minister has feebly tried to make his own reels in response, but that failed to stem the tide of public irreverence. The ridicule was noteworthy not simply as political satire. It lifted the pall of fear that had hung over Indian democracy since Modi became prime minister, as critics have been silenced through intimidation and opposition leaders targeted through legal proceedings. In mocking power, young people found their voice.

The threat they pose to Modi is significant. These young Indians are not saddled with the historical baggage of ties to conventional opposition parties. The issues they focus on—exams and elections—cut across social divides and even partisan political affiliations. And the protesters’ grit, wit, inventiveness, and fearlessness have dented Modi’s authority like nothing else has in the past 12 years.

The movement must still contend with the brute reality of state power, which has been evident in the streets of New Delhi this past week. Authorities tried to physically suppress and contain dissent by prohibiting it and preventing protesters from entering certain public spaces. That in turn has given young Indians another reason to go to the streets, to protest the right to protest and reclaim public spaces. The government has deployed thousands of security forces in the national capital, as if it were facing some invading foreign army, not young people making reasonable demands, and all signs are that Modi will continue to maintain a hard line on the protests.

For the government, the movement’s principal demand, the resignation of Kumar, is not simply a question of one official’s tenure. Modi has secured extraordinary loyalty within his BJP through an implicit assurance that those who act at the government’s behest will be protected from the consequences of their actions. If that assurance begins to fray, ministers and party functionaries may worry about what might happen when they can no longer take for granted the protection of the leadership. Modi’s defense of Kumar, then, may be less about the SIR than about preserving loyalty to the government within the ranks of his party.

Moreover, Modi has built much of his political persona around turning perceived slights into demonstrations of strength. To yield in the face of public mockery would be especially difficult and out of character. Kumar’s removal could be read as an admission of guilt. Such a retreat could then embolden Modi’s rivals and encourage further protests—and raise the prospect that a galvanized opposition might finally unseat the prime minister in the next parliamentary elections scheduled for 2029.

To be sure, the future electoral consequences of the protests remain uncertain. Public anger does not automatically translate into votes. The Election Commission may yet blunt the controversy by admitting voters whose appeals are pending or by making procedural concessions that reduce the immediate grievances. The BJP may still benefit from a fractious opposition and from the fact that voters in a country as large as India are rarely united in their motivations and perceptions of the political stakes. The next major electoral test, in the state of Uttar Pradesh, is still some months away, in March 2027. A victory there for the BJP would allow the party to claim that its authority remains intact; a defeat would make it much harder to contain the political implications of the present controversy. The government is likely to double down on repression against dissenters nationwide, trusting that it can succeed in limiting public expressions of anger.

The BJP is hoping that the more disruptions there are in New Delhi, the more it can cast the current upheaval as the work of rabble-rousers and anarchists and rally Indians behind the prime minister. The government and its supporters are already seeking to discredit the movement as the work of foreign hands, invoking conspiracy theories and examples of alleged malign foreign intervention in the youth demonstrations in Bangladesh and Nepal that ousted sitting governments.

Yet the greater danger facing the Indian government is that it comes to believe its own propaganda and ignores genuine discontent. In both Bangladesh and Nepal, the depth of popular grievance was obvious for anyone willing to look—as it is in India. Despite India’s headline growth rates, young people worry about their economic future. Graduate unemployment is at 23 percent, white-collar job growth has slowed to a creeping one percent this year, and nearly a quarter of Indians between the ages of 15 and 29 are not in employment, education, or training programs. The very institutions that were meant to secure their future—elections, courts, and independent agencies—are losing their trust. The strongman is failing, even as the opposition struggles to inspire a great deal of conviction and enthusiasm. What is powerful about this moment, then, is the spectacle of young people who, lacking many good options, are still choosing to fight for their democracy the only way citizens can when it is in peril: with energy, aspiration, and the belief that they can craft their own collective future, wherever that may lead.

How Modi’s Supremacy Will Hinder His Country’s Rise

But Trump’s Coercion Could Force Modi’s Hand

How Indian Voters Constrained Modi—and Saved Their Democracy

China Gains From New Delhi’s Support for South Asian Autocrats

Why Politicians Choose Statist Solutions Over Economic Reforms

The Pressures Facing Washington and New Delhi

The Narrow but Feasible Path to Collective Good

Alex Imas, Mohamed A. El-Erian, and James Manyika

Niall Ferguson, Lisa Klaassen, and Eric Schmidt

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

#5694. Schumer, Dems are quietly betting big on one sleeper race in the Deep South

With the midterm elections approaching, a Mississippi rally emphasizes the high stakes of the Senate race. President Trump championed Cindy Hyde-Smith for the United States Senate while accusing opponent Scott Colom of being a "shameless phony" funded by George Soros.

National Democrats are getting aggressive in the homestretch of this election cycle, sending money and party figureheads to a state that, for decades, has been out of reach.

Mississippi has been a Republican stronghold for the GOP in the Senate since the 1980s, but in a political environment that is proving tough for incumbents of the party in power, Democrats appear to smell blood in the water.

But Republicans aren’t buying the last-minute play in the Deep South, arguing that it’s a reach, at best, to make the race competitive.

DEMOCRATS SEE NEW CHANCES TO FLIP RED STATES — BUT A $400M GOP SPENDING ADVANTAGE COULD DERAIL THEIR PLANS

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer attends a press conference on China AI export controls at the U.S. Capitol on Sept. 23, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Getty Images)

"Largely, I think they’re just throwing s--- at the wall at this point," a GOP political operative told Fox News Digital.

The Senate Majority PAC, a political action committee with close ties to Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., reserved $1.2 million in advertising in the state to boost Scott Colom, a district attorney mounting a bid against Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith.

And major figures in the Democratic Party, like former Vice President Kamala Harris and former Transportation Secretary Pete Buttigieg, have also made the trip or are set to appear in the state — Buttigieg is scheduled to hold a rally on Mississippi’s Gulf Coast on Tuesday.

Jake Monssen, Hyde-Smith’s campaign manager, told Fox News Digital in a statement, "This is simply Washington insiders making a last-ditch effort to give life to Scott Colom's failing campaign."

"Scott is bankrolled by the radical left because he is in lockstep with their agenda," Monssen said. "He supports tax increases, open borders, taxpayer benefits for illegal immigrants, and sex change surgeries for minors."

Like in other races across the Senate map, Colom has a fight with history to contend with. Mississippi has not elected a Democrat to the Senate since 1982, and the Magnolia State has consistently supported President Donald Trump.

TRUMP-BACKED SENATOR ADMITS GOP FACES NIGHTMARE SCENARIO IN STATE REPUBLICANS HAVEN'T LOST IN 90 YEARS

But Colom believes that the ground operation he’s developed while national Democrats stayed away from the state is what would put him over the top against Hyde-Smith, not the last-minute support.

"It could be enough," Colom told Fox News Digital. "I mean, the thing is, the people of Mississippi built this campaign."

Colom went on the offensive against Hyde-Smith, arguing that she "refuses to debate, she doesn’t campaign."

"Because of that, I was able to build momentum on the ground, which means now that it's getting attention, now that it's getting resources, I'm able to take advantage of it in a way that other candidates couldn't," he said.

Republicans aren’t resting on their laurels in the state, however. The Senate Leadership Fund, a political action committee closely aligned with Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., teed up a seven-figure ad buy there in response to Democrats’ spending.

And Trump appeared in Corinth, Mississippi, on Monday to rally for Hyde-Smith. He made a similar pitch to voters that he’s done at every rally stop this midterm cycle: pretend he’s on the ballot.

"In this election, if you would, I'm asking you to pretend that I'm on the ballot," Trump said. "And the reason is that when I'm on the ballot, exactly 49% more people show up."

Jonathan Klingler, a professor of political science at the University of Mississippi, told Fox News Digital that the increased spending and national attention to a once-sleepy race have generated the "talking point of Mississippi as evidence for momentum and building a Democratic wave."

THUNE MAKES BOLD PREDICTION ABOUT REPUBLICANS' SENATE CHANCES AS GOP PULLS MILLIONS FROM KEY RACES

District Attorney Scott Colom, center, is running for Senate against Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith, R-Miss., and has backers with deep pockets in the Soros family. (Courtesy of Colom Senate campaign)

"So at least I'm not — I can't say whether that's the intent of the funding here or visits, but that is the effect," he said.

There are also voter demographics that both Hyde-Smith and Colom have to contend with. Mississippi has the highest share of Black voters in the country at nearly 40% of the electorate. In order for Colom and "Democrats to be successful, they simply have to peel off a larger share of White voters," Klingler said.

There’s another storyline underneath the headlines of this race, too.

Hyde-Smith and Colom have a history dating back to the Biden administration, when the lawmaker blocked his nomination to serve as a district court judge in Mississippi.

"Instead of saving himself the embarrassment of being denied a job twice by Hyde-Smith, Colom is running a campaign backed by Chuck Schumer, funded by George Soros, and built on pro-transgender and other far-left policies," National Republican Senatorial Committee spokeswoman Bernadette Breslin told Fox News Digital.

That’s not Colom’s motivating factor, however. He had just left Trump's northeast Mississippi rally where he spoke to voters about their main concerns, "everybody I talked to, gas prices, grocery prices," and tariffs, he said.

But Colom said his personal dispute with Hyde-Smith was not his primary motivation.

"I'll tell you, as a Christian, I forgive her for that. That's not a motivating factor for me," Colom said. "I mean, as a district attorney, I have to prosecute cases where people lost their loved ones to gun violence. So, you know, and I’ve seen them forgive that person."

"So that's not the motivating factor," he continued. "For me, it's really the betrayal of the America First movement, right? Like she was supposed to help President Trump get lower grocery prices. Well, she betrayed us. We're not getting lower grocery prices."

Alex Miller is a writer for Fox News Digital covering the U.S. Senate.

#5693. 5 Foreign-Policy Priorities for Democrats After the Midterms

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: 5 Foreign-Policy Priorities for Democrats After the Midterms

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Foreign policy could be unusually pivotal in the U.S. midterm elections on Nov. 3. Voters are telling pollsters they are angry about prices at the pump, which have increased by more than a third since the start of the year. Many blame U.S. President Donald Trump’s decision to go to war with Iran, as well as his trade war with Canada, for hurting their wallets. And while Trump himself isn’t on the ballot, voters seem increasingly likely to turn against his party.

Most major election forecasters now predict that the Democratic Party will win control of the House. Should that happen, Democrats would oversee the writing of the chamber’s annual military and foreign aid spending bills. They’d also have the power to convene high-profile hearings to examine the executive branch’s foreign-policy moves.

Foreign policy could be unusually pivotal in the U.S. midterm elections on Nov. 3. Voters are telling pollsters they are angry about prices at the pump, which have increased by more than a third since the start of the year. Many blame U.S. President Donald Trump’s decision to go to war with Iran, as well as his trade war with Canada, for hurting their wallets. And while Trump himself isn’t on the ballot, voters seem increasingly likely to turn against his party.

Most major election forecasters now predict that the Democratic Party will win control of the House. Should that happen, Democrats would oversee the writing of the chamber’s annual military and foreign aid spending bills. They’d also have the power to convene high-profile hearings to examine the executive branch’s foreign-policy moves.

The outlook for control of the Senate is murkier, though forecasters say Democrats’ chances of winning it have improved in recent weeks. If Democrats win the Senate, they will gain the additional power of blocking votes on all of Trump’s national security nominees, which could be important leverage for looming policy fights over Iran, China, artificial intelligence, and troop movements.

Here are the top five foreign-policy priorities that Democrats are likely to focus on if they win control of one or both chambers of Congress in early November:

U.S. President Donald Trump speaks in front of the new Air Force One, gifted to him by Qatar, at Joint Base Andrews in Maryland, United States, on June 19.Brendan Smialowski/AFP via Getty Images

Democrats see alleged corruption committed by members of the Trump administration as a potent campaign message, connecting voter anger over the high cost of living with reports of self-dealing by Trump’s friends and family. And they’re pledging to investigate these allegations if they win the House.

“Trump has used national security and foreign policy as one of his primary tools for monetizing the government,” Rep. Jason Crow, who is leading House Democrats’ efforts to win in battleground districts around the country, told Foreign Policy. He accused the president of carrying out “with astonishing disregard” a “racketeering shake-down operation to earn money and promote his businesses.”

One very visible target for Democratic inquiries could include Trump’s acceptance of a $400 million luxury jet from Qatar. Another could be the alleged links between Abu Dhabi’s investments in the Trump family’s crypto business and the White House’s decision to approve the export of 500,000 advanced chips to the United Arab Emirates. That happened despite fears that the sensitive technology would be shared with China.

“The blatant corruption you’re seeing at all levels, in particular foreign corruption, foreign money flowing to the president’s family and his top associates, and the ways that they have not only blurred the lines but clearly stepped over them in clear violation of the law and ethical standards” is also likely to be a top focus in the Senate if Democrats win control there, said a Democratic Senate staffer, speaking on condition of anonymity because they were not authorized to be quoted.

Sen. Chris Murphy told reporters in October that he wanted to see Democrats use any new oversight powers to probe Trump’s decision to rely on family members and personal friends—including Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff—to conduct high-stakes diplomatic negotiations.

“The White House and Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff want to keep diplomacy with the Gulf to themselves because it’s a grift,” Murphy said. “They don’t want powerful ambassadors to ring alarm bells about their corruption, and so there are not going to be any qualified ambassadors serving the Gulf, because if there were, those people would likely stand in the way of Trump’s corruption.”—Rachel Oswald

The casket of U.S. Army Sgt. Angel Rampersad, who was killed in action at Muwaffaq Salti Air Base in Jordan, is brought out during a funeral service in New York City on July 31. Spencer Platt/Getty Images

The Iran war is deeply unpopular with U.S. voters and could hurt Republicans on Election Day perhaps more than any other issue. The war has been frustrating to the U.S. public for an array of reasons—from driving up gas prices and inflation to the Trump administration’s shifting goals and opaque strategy. Trump has also repeatedly downplayed the economic fallout from the conflict. Meanwhile, there’s no end in sight to the war.

Though some Republicans on Capitol Hill have also been critical of the conflict and the administration’s handling of it, Democratic efforts in Congress to end the war have so far fallen short.

But if Democrats win back one or both chambers in November, they will have more avenues to make continuing the war difficult for Trump—including by moving to cut off funding and ramping up scrutiny of the administration’s approach via hearings and investigations.

“Priority one is taking control of committees and issuing subpoenas and going after the information we’ve been seeking: How much does the Iran war cost? How many casualties? What is the damage to facilities? What is the current state of the weapon stockpile?” Crow said.

Crow, who sits on the House Armed Services Committee, said one of his most urgent questions is learning what the U.S. military is doing to protect the roughly 55,000 U.S. service members deployed to the Middle East from drone attacks.

Murphy, who sits on the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, accused U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth of covering up the “cost and consequences of this war from the American public.” He said ending the war should be a priority for Democrats.

“It is absolutely outrageous that I and Republican senators are being denied the ability to see the damage for ourselves,” Murphy said. “This is a really dangerous moment, when the administration is giving its middle finger to Congress, pretending as if we are totally irrelevant.”

Murphy said that if Democrats win in the Senate, they will have “an ability to decide whether we’re going to fund this war and whether we’re going to pass a defense authorization bill that allows this war to continue.” The senator, who is among the most prominent Democratic voices on foreign policy in the upper chamber, said that if the war is still ongoing in early 2027, then Congress should not “appropriate funding for the Department of Defense without a requirement that this war end … and we should not pass” any new funding without that stipulation.—John Haltiwanger

Volunteers of the U.S.-based food charity World Central Kitchen distribute hot meal to residents in Kyiv on Jan. 22. Sergei Gapon/AFP via Getty Images

The Trump administration has rattled European partners by threatening to annex Greenland, mooting a major drawdown of U.S. troops, and pursuing policies favorable to Russia.

A Democratic win would challenge some of those plans.

“We know they are trying to change the footprint in Europe. We know this plan to review the footprint in Europe is designed to eventually reduce it, … and we’ll resist those things because we have broad policy disagreements with the administration,” Crow said.

Ukraine, long starved of new supplies of U.S. aid, could see Congress seek to rush more weapons to the country. “There’s pretty robust agreement that we should support Ukraine, so we are going to figure out how we bolster the [Ukraine Security Assistance Initiative] money and how we put that into the NDAA and how we compel the administration to procure those weapons,” Crow said, referring to the National Defense Authorization Act.

Aid packages for Ukraine could also move faster, said Rep. Jim Himes, the ranking member of the House Intelligence Committee, speaking last week in Warsaw.

“It means that aid packages move not in nine months but in nine days or nine weeks on the outside and that the message [of support to Ukraine] is very different,” he said.

However, there is also now a broader consensus that Europe should do more for its defense, even among Democrats. “There will be a continuation on the part of Americans to keep pushing the Europeans to assume more of a role,” Himes said. “We may simply not have the money to play the role that we have played in the past.”—Sam Skove

Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro arrives in New York on Jan. 5, following his capture from Caracas during a U.S. military raid. XNY/Star Max via Getty Images

The Trump administration has been conducting strikes on alleged drug boats in the Caribbean and Eastern Pacific for more than a year. The strikes, which experts have decried as illegal under both domestic and international law, have killed at least 246 people. The Trump administration has said the strikes are targeting dangerous “narco-terrorists,” but it has not provided evidence to back up its assertions.

Congressional Democrats have been critical of the strikes and the Trump administration’s broader goals in Latin America, as the United States maintains a sizable military presence in the region and urges more governments to participate in joint operations against drug cartels. But Democratic efforts to rein in Trump in Latin America and stop continued operations via war powers resolutions have so far failed.

Much of what the Trump administration has done in Latin America has occurred quietly or been overshadowed by other developments and events, such as the Iran war. But a Democratic win in November could significantly increase oversight and put more of a spotlight on the administration’s activities in the region via hearings and investigations. If they gain a majority in either chamber, Democrats could also potentially move to curb military operations in Latin America by leveraging or restricting funding.

The boat strikes began last September as a prelude to the operation that captured Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro, whom the Trump administration has accused of drug trafficking. (Experts have said this is difficult to prove.) And the White House’s actions in Venezuela since have also been controversial. Trump effectively embraced the existing regime, minus Maduro, rather than pushing for a democratic process or supporting exiled opposition leader María Corina Machado.

The White House has justified many of its actions in the country by pointing to the potential for the United States to tap into Venezuela’s vast oil reserves. The administration in late August announced an agreement with the Venezuelan government that would give the United States “control” over more than 65 billion barrels of Venezuela’s oil. But there are many reasons to doubt that the United States will benefit from the deal to the extent that Trump has claimed.

Democrats will be poised to press Trump officials for more answers on these issues if they win big in November. Top Democrats in both the House and Senate have repeatedly called for more transparency and accountability regarding both the boat strikes and U.S. involvement in Venezuelan oil production.—John Haltiwanger

Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg speaks to the press with Trump in Washington on Sept. 29. Kent Nishimura/AFP via Getty Images

The leaps in AI capabilities this year alone have alarmed policymakers in Washington nearly as much as the broader geopolitical conflicts the United States is engaged in.

AI models are going rogue and hacking websites, bad actors are allegedly using them to try to fine-tune ballistic missile technology, and companies such as OpenAI and Anthropic are warning that their technology could end humanity and imploring the government to regulate them.

But Trump doesn’t appear interested in doing so and instead has urged companies to keep developing—with only a “morally binding” self-policing pledge that Trump had top AI companies sign.

Democrats feel differently. “Donald Trump thinks he is the smartest man in the room and he can handle it, and he is wrong,” Crow told Foreign Policy. “We know that the government has a role. We know that we need to pump the brakes on AI development so we have time to put appropriate guardrails on.”

That view appears to be bicameral (and possibly bipartisan), according to Murphy. “The biggest issue of trust is the lack of trust the people have that we’re going to actually protect them from the poison, protect them from the potential enormous downside of unregulated, unchecked AI,” the senator told Foreign Policy onstage at the FP Tech & AI Forum in September. “It might be the end of our democracy if the people beg, on both sides of the aisle, for us to do what we’ve done with every complicated new technology in the history of this country since the Industrial Revolution—regulate and control it to make sure we get the good but not the bad—[and] we don’t step up and do the work.”

Murphy outlined three areas that lawmakers can focus on: outlawing the use of deepfakes in politics or mandating watermarks for AI-generated content; banning so-called AI companions or “friendship bots” that he described as “poison for our kids”; and instituting an approval process for new advanced AI models before they are released.

“Those three things are achievable, not terribly political, and could foreseeably get done and then give the public some faith that we have the political capacity to handle some of the more nuanced threats down the line,” he said.—Rishi Iyengar

Netanyahu’s opponents are ahead but will struggle to form a coalition.

#5692. Tesla blames battery supply for flat sales, but its prices keep falling

Tesla says battery supply, not demand, is what’s holding back its car sales. In its Q3 2026 shareholder letter, the automaker wrote that “battery availability continued to be the limiting factor in increasing auto volumes in the quarter.”

It also claims it ended Q3 with “the largest backlog for our passenger vehicles in four years.” But the numbers in the same letter don’t line up with a company that has more buyers than cars.

The battery line has been building for a while. In Q2, Tesla called battery pack capacity “the main limiting factor to near-term vehicle production volume increase.” Now it says batteries limited what it could sell during the quarter itself.

Tesla didn’t put a number on the backlog. It did list record deliveries in Canada, Australia, Japan and Taiwan, and record production at Gigafactory Berlin. Elsewhere in the Q3 2026 financial results, Tesla says it is focused on “increasing production across our factories” because of “strong demand.”

Tesla delivered 486,532 vehicles in Q3, down 2% from 497,099 a year ago. Model 3 and Model Y were down 1%. “Other models,” which covers Cybertruck, Semi and what’s left of Model S and X, fell 48% to 8,295 units.

It built 464,391 vehicles, so it delivered 22,141 more than it produced. That’s the second quarter in a row Tesla has worked down inventory, after building about 50,000 more cars than it sold in Q1. Global inventory fell to 11 days of supply, from 27 days in Q1 and 15 in Q2.

Model 3 and Model Y production ran at about 1.83 million units annualized. Tesla lists more than 2.25 million units of installed capacity for those two models across Fremont, Shanghai, Berlin and Texas. So the factories ran at roughly 81%.

And here’s the part that doesn’t fit. In its list of reasons revenue didn’t grow, Tesla cited “lower vehicle average selling price (ASP)” year-over-year, even after excluding currency effects. Automakers that can’t build cars fast enough usually raise prices, or at least stop discounting.

Tesla has more places to put battery cells than it used to. The same letter says energy storage had its “highest-ever order rate and backlog,” Megafactory Texas started production with 50 GWh of planned capacity, and Tesla is increasing 4680 cell output “to support the ramps of both Cybercab and Tesla Semi along with higher Model Y volumes.”

Tesla’s outlook even says deliveries depend on “allocation decisions between sale to customers and use for our owned and operated fleet.” So part of the battery constraint is Tesla deciding where its cells go.

Battery supply is a real issue, and tariffs on Chinese cells make it harder in the US. Tesla also did set records in a few markets, but overall deliveries are down, and Tesla is selling cars for lower than it used to. Those are not signs of a company that is supply constrained.

That is the excuses to spin. It doesn't matter what Musk says, his supporters will believe it.

In fact, today, in 2026, Tesla has more battery suppliers than ever, and it has itself ramped up production of its own battery cells.

But “we’re supply constrained” is one of Tesla’s oldest lines, and this letter doesn’t prove it. Deliveries fell year-over-year. Prices fell. Two quarters ago, Tesla built 50,000 cars it couldn’t sell. If batteries were really the bottleneck, Tesla would be raising prices and keeping the extra margin, not lowering ASPs.

The more likely answer is that it’s a bit of both. Demand recovered somewhat in some markets, and Tesla is choosing to send more cells to Megapack, Cybercab and Semi because it thinks those businesses are worth more. That’s a reasonable strategy. It’s just not the same as having more customers than it can serve.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer.
