#5691. The Nuclear Deal That Could Save NATO

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

IVO H. DAALDER is Senior Fellow at Harvard’s Belfer Center for Science and International Affairs. He served as U.S. Ambassador to NATO from 2009 to 2013.

How Europe Can Take More Responsibility—Without Starting an Arms Race

At a meeting earlier this year, top foreign ministry officials from several European countries were asked who among them still believed that the United States would come to their defense if they were attacked. Not a single hand went up. It was a stark indication of the extent to which European governments have concluded that the United States—for so long the backbone of European and NATO security—is no longer a reliable ally. Over the past 18 months, conversations in Brussels and in other NATO capitals have revolved around how to boost the continent’s conventional defense to make up for Washington’s retrenchment. But a quieter and more urgent discussion is also taking place, focused on the status of the ultimate deterrent: the U.S. nuclear guarantee.

More than half a century ago, the Great Debate, as the French strategist Raymond Aron termed it, broke out between European countries and the United States over how to preserve European nuclear deterrence in the face of the Soviet Union’s expanding nuclear capabilities. Then, European leaders feared that Washington’s growing emphasis on the conventional defense of the continent reflected an increasing reluctance to protect Europe with nuclear weapons—especially as Moscow developed missiles that could reach the U.S. homeland. Would the United States be willing to risk Boston to defend Berlin? That debate was ultimately resolved through a new grand bargain: in return for a commitment from European countries to forgo developing nuclear weapons, the United States would invite more European participation in nuclear decision-making. That essential bargain stuck for 60 years, helping secure both the United States and Europe and preventing nuclear proliferation.

Now, however, that bargain has come undone. Washington says that it wants a new division of labor: the Europeans need to take on primary responsibility for their conventional defense while the United States continues to ensure their nuclear defense. But that hardly addresses the problem. “If you don’t believe in the United States’ Article 5 commitment,” one of the officials attending the meeting told me, “then extended deterrence is effectively dead.” Already, alternative options are being considered. In some European capitals, officials are quietly raising the possibility of acquiring their own nuclear weapons. France, meanwhile, has offered to adapt its nuclear policy to help defend its European allies.Each of these options has real logic. But none of them adequately addresses the problem of ensuring European security without risking further proliferation. A better approach would be a fourth alternative: a renewed U.S.-European nuclear bargain, this time with a real European core. Led by France and the United Kingdom, NATO’s European members need to take on a larger role in their nuclear deterrence while working to restore the transatlantic defense link that has been the foundation of U.S. and European security for so many decades.

The Soviet Union’s 1957 launch of the Sputnik satellite posed a profound dilemma for NATO. Until then, the alliance had counted on U.S. nuclear weapons deployed in Europe to deter an attack by a much larger Soviet conventional force. But once Moscow demonstrated its ability to launch intercontinental rockets, Europeans feared that the United States would no longer be willing to use nuclear weapons to prevent a Soviet invasion of Europe.

These concerns prompted a fierce debate within NATO about how the continent could ensure its nuclear defense. Some, led by France, were convinced that European countries needed their own nuclear weapons. Others sought less costly ways to restore some control over nuclear deterrence. One American idea that gained substantial traction was to create a so-called multilateral force—a fleet of vessels carrying some 200 missiles. Such a multilaterally operated and funded force could give Europeans a sense of ownership and control over the use of nuclear weapons, the thinking went, while preserving the U.S. president’s final authority over their launch.

The debate exposed a fundamental problem with extending U.S. nuclear deterrence to allies, especially if an ocean separates those allies from Washington. An adversary might be deterred from attacking an American ally by even the small possibility of a U.S. nuclear retaliation; the consequences of a miscalculation would be catastrophic. But that same small possibility would offer little comfort to the ally, who needs to believe that Washington would actually risk nuclear devastation on its behalf. Denis Healey, the United Kingdom’s defense secretary throughout much of the 1960s, postulated what became known as the Healey theorem: “It takes only a five percent credibility of American retaliation to deter an attack, but it takes a 95 percent credibility to reassure the allies.”

The debate was ultimately resolved through a bargain. Washington’s European allies agreed to forgo their right to acquire nuclear weapons in return for a greater say in U.S. nuclear weapons policy and strategy. Although the idea of a costly multilateral force was abandoned, the creation of the NATO Nuclear Planning Group (NPG) provided a vehicle for Europeans to give input into U.S. nuclear planning and for Washington to demonstrate that European security interests were integral to its nuclear strategy and force posture. Reassured, European countries (aside from France and the United Kingdom) agreed to codify their nonnuclear status in the 1968 Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty.

Many European countries retained ground- and air-based systems that could deliver U.S. nuclear weapons deployed on their territory. Today, about half a dozen European countries still deploy dual-capable aircraft that can carry U.S. B-61 nuclear bombs. These nuclear consulting and sharing arrangements proved far more crucial to reassuring allies than the hard-power measures (such as counterforce targeting, low-yield weapons, and greater precision) that U.S. strategists typically believe were essential. The latter measures focused on resolving the five-percent problem; the former addressed the 95-percent questions.

France was the only country to reject this nuclear bargain. (The United Kingdom had become a nuclear power in the 1950s, but it committed all its weapons to NATO and participated in the NPG and the dual-capable aircraft missions.) Under President Charles de Gaulle, France came to believe that it could not count on another country for its nuclear security. Paris embarked on its own nuclear program, refused to participate in the NPG or the dual-capable aircraft mission, and, in 1966, withdrew from NATO’s military command structure. The overall bargain, however, laid the foundation for what has become a highly successful deterrence, reassurance, and nonproliferation regime.

Unlike in the 1960s, today’s nuclear debate has not been prompted by a change in the global balance of power. It has been precipitated by a unilateral change in the United States’ attitude toward Europe. As I explained in Foreign Affairs last year, in Trump’s second term, the United States has rejected the two core principles of NATO’s collective defense commitments: that there is a shared understanding of the threat to NATO members and that members’ security is indivisible.

European members of NATO can no longer assume that the U.S. commitment to their security—derived from the fundamental idea, born of two devastating world wars, that American security is inextricably linked to the security of Europe—is ironclad. Instead, as the Pentagon’s policy chief, Elbridge Colby, has repeatedly noted, “under President Trump’s leadership, the simple fact is that countries can no longer take America for granted.” European countries have never taken America for granted. They have assumed that it shares their belief in the indivisibility of U.S. and European security—and they proved their own commitment to this belief when NATO invoked Article 5’s collective-defense obligation for the first and only time, in response to the September 11, 2001, attacks on the United States.

The American president and other senior officials have made it clear that the United States’ security commitment to Europe is based not on a shared understanding of the threat that both entities face but on secondary considerations, such as European countries’ level of defense spending and their support for U.S. foreign policy. In August, the Pentagon sent its 31 NATO allies a long questionnaire stressing the conditionality of Washington’s security commitment: it asked them to profess their loyalty to Trump’s foreign policy, commit to procuring defense equipment from U.S. firms, and prioritize funding the restoration of the U.S. industrial base by opposing any “buy European” initiatives.

Some European NATO leaders still refuse to admit in public that anything fundamental has changed. Privately, though, most have reached the same conclusion as senior European political officials: that the United States can no longer be counted on to come to Europe’s defense. And if that is true when it comes to conventional defense, it is even more true with respect to the nuclear guarantee. That leaves Europe facing questions it has never had to ask before. What if Russia attacked NATO territory and Washington told Europe it was on its own? How would nonnuclear allies stand up to a nuclear-armed Russia? What if Moscow used nuclear threats, implicit or explicit, to try to force Europe to end its support for Ukraine? None of these scenarios are far-fetched. Russia has issued veiled nuclear threats against Europe repeatedly since 2022, most recently in September, after NATO conducted air exercises above northeastern Poland, near Kaliningrad.

Three options have begun to emerge to fill the gap created by the change in U.S. policy. Washington favors reestablishing the nuclear bargain with a new distribution of responsibilities, which it calls NATO 3.0. “We will continue to provide the U.S. extended nuclear deterrent,” Colby told NATO defense ministers earlier this year, “with Europe taking the lead for its conventional defense.” The United States has even suggested that it might be willing to expand its nuclear umbrella by extending its nuclear sharing arrangements to other countries (Poland, for instance) and developing a next-generation nuclear bomb for potential deployment in Europe.

But this option ignores the core problem. The Trump administration has done nothing to convince its allies that the United States remains committed to NATO other than saying so. No additional weapons deployments can compensate for the central implication that U.S. security commitments to European allies are now conditional on political loyalty.

A second option would end the nuclear bargain altogether. In its place, European countries would acquire their own nuclear weapons. The clearest statement of this idea came in March 2025, when Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk told the country’s parliament that “we would be safer if we had our own nuclear arsenal, that is beyond doubt.” A year later, he told his cabinet that Poland would “strive to prepare . . . for the most autonomous actions possible” on nuclear security. And Poland isn’t the only country thinking seriously about this option. In Sweden, some politicians have invoked the country’s secret nuclear program during the 1960s to indicate their interest in pursuing a national option. Even in Germany, where discussions of defense have historically been constrained by its own history, a Bundeswehr general argued publicly earlier this year that Germany “needs its own tactical nuclear weapons,” an assertion that would have been unthinkable just a few years ago. Similar ideas are being voiced in Asia, especially in South Korea, raising the prospect of a cascade of allied proliferation.

In theory, if more European countries acquired nuclear weapons, it could resolve the extended deterrence dilemma. In practice, it isn’t a workable solution. It would take many years for Poland, Sweden, or even Germany to develop independent nuclear deterrents. European nuclearization would break the Nonproliferation Treaty architecture that the original 1960s U.S.-European nuclear bargain was built to protect, and it could set off a nuclear arms race that could make Europe less, not more, secure. A strategy designed to bolster deterrence would end up weakening the very alliance structure it aimed to strengthen.

As an alternative to these two options, France has spearheaded a third approach that would aim to replace, or at least augment, the U.S. umbrella. French President Emmanuel Macron proposed this solution in a speech in March 2026, announcing a “forward deterrence” initiative that would include European allies in nuclear training exercises, temporarily forward-deploy French nuclear-capable aircraft on allied soil, and host extensive nuclear discussions with participating countries. So far, ten European countries have joined the initiative by establishing bilateral nuclear steering groups to discuss French nuclear strategy and how its evolution could enhance their security.

The Macron proposal represents a major evolution in French thinking by explicitly extending France’s vital interests to include the security of its European partners. Paris’s willingness to engage European neighbors in direct talks about nuclear issues is welcome. But there are real drawbacks to such an initiative. In his announcement, Macron reiterated the long-standing “four nos” that underpin France’s defense doctrine: no joint decisions on nuclear use, no joint nuclear planning, no shared definition of French vital interests, and no nuclear guarantee “in the strict sense of the term.” The framework’s reliance on bilateral discussions also weakens the idea that Europe’s defense ought to be a collective NATO effort.

And crucially, France’s nuclear doctrine—which allows for a warning shot, followed by a devastating response if the warning fails to deter the adversary—gives it fewer options for managing conflict than does NATO’s more flexible strategy, which allows for gradual escalation. This narrower range of options could make its nuclear threats less credible in a conflict over an ally’s security. In the end, the French proposal would add a measure of reassurance and deterrence, but not enough to overcome the voids created by the change in U.S. policy.

None of the three options now being discussed resolve Europe’s extended nuclear deterrence dilemma. But elements of all three point to a possible solution. NATO needs to bolster both the European and the U.S. legs of its nuclear deterrence posture, bring France into its nuclear consultation and planning structures, and extend the existing nuclear sharing arrangements to incorporate additional members. These tasks will require NATO members, especially its three nuclear powers, to make significant adjustments to their existing beliefs and policies. The alternative is not the status quo—which is already unsustainable, given the nature of the Russian threat and the changes in U.S. policy—but rapid nuclear proliferation.

NATO countries will need to agree to three major adjustments in their nuclear posture: in the number and kind of weapons deployed, in how nuclear and nonnuclear NATO members consult one another, and in the alliance’s doctrine for using nuclear weapons. First, NATO nuclear powers need to modernize their nuclear forces. The United States should replace the B-61 gravity bomb, which will be difficult to use effectively against heavily defended targets, with a stand-off missile that can reach deep into Russian territory from NATO airspace. Such a modernization is long overdue. In addition, European nations should agree to develop a long-range missile that could be outfitted with a nuclear warhead developed jointly by France and the United Kingdom. The missile should be financed by European countries and be deployable on aircraft owned and operated by nonnuclear allies. Finally, these new U.S. and European nuclear capabilities need to be based in a wider range of NATO countries, expanding European participation in the nuclear deterrent mission.

Changes to hardware are necessary to restore the credibility of Europe’s nuclear deterrence. But the software of consultation needs to be at the core of the effort, as it was in the 1960s. Back then, France refused to join the NATO consensus and withdrew from the integrated military command structure. Even when it returned, in 2009, its nuclear deterrent remained fully independent, and it did not join the Nuclear Planning Group. That insistence on independence is what led Paris to pursue bilateral nuclear arrangements this year. But these parallel bilateral discussions not only create confusion and distrust about Paris’s true intentions among dialogue partners; they also fail to foster the cohesion that could come from a collective European nuclear deterrence dialogue.

Put simply, Paris needs to join the NPG. Doing so would not automatically place its nuclear capabilities under NATO command and control. But it would give European partners a better understanding of French thinking and afford France more knowledge of European requirements for deterrence and reassurance. European NPG members could agree to meet regularly without the United States to discuss how to integrate the new European nuclear missile forces into NATO’s nuclear strategy. For many years, countries hosting U.S. nuclear forces have met separately from other NATO members in the NPG; a similar, European-only caucus would build on that precedent.

The third necessary adjustment needs to be doctrinal. One element of NATO’s nuclear doctrine cannot change: that the ultimate decision to use nuclear weapons will always reside with the nuclear powers, France, the United Kingdom, and the United States. These decisions cannot be delegated. And national nuclear deterrents will continue to be guided by national doctrine, not subject to NATO control. If France wants to retain its one-warning-shot escalatory doctrine, it can do so, even after it joins the NPG. But it would need to explain how this doctrine would reassure allies and enhance deterrence. At the same time, France’s participation in the NPG and the addition of the new European nuclear missile force would expand Europe’s influence over the development of NATO nuclear strategy. And it would confront Moscow with the reality that the decision to use nuclear weapons in Europe’s defense no longer rests in Washington’s hands alone.

These are major posture adjustments that will require significant changes in policy and perspective. Washington will have to accept that the deployment of additional European nuclear forces means it can no longer dictate NATO’s nuclear policy and strategy—including, ultimately, the decision to use nuclear weapons. Paris, meanwhile, must accept more integration into NATO. London will need to extend its nuclear cooperation with the United States to France, develop a second nuclear system (in addition to its submarine force), and find the resources necessary to design and build a new warhead in collaboration with France. Nonnuclear European countries will have to agree to finance the development and procurement of new missile systems and possibly airplanes (although not nuclear warheads, which would violate their treaty commitments). They must also collectively finance security, communications, and other upgrades to enable the expanded deployment of dual-capable aircraft. Most important, these nonnuclear allies would need to reiterate their commitment to forgoing nuclear weapons of their own, reaffirming the essential bargain that was cemented 60 years ago.

None of this will happen quickly or easily. It will require London, Paris, and Washington to give up an element of control over their nuclear arsenals that each has jealously guarded for more than 60 years. But the alternatives are worse. The security of Europe and the United States is, in fact, indivisible, no matter what the current administration in Washington may believe. If Europe were left to fend for itself and nuclear weapons spread from capital to capital, Americans would be less safe, not more. Yet U.S. retrenchment is also real, and no amount of reassurance from Washington will restore the credibility of a nuclear guarantee that Europeans have come to doubt. With the United States pulling back, a new bargain in which Europe carries more of the nuclear burden and has more say in the choice to use these weapons is the only way to ensure transatlantic security.

Police arrested a Libertarian North Carolina state Senate candidate Monday after an hours-long armed standoff near a private school, ending a tense confrontation that unfolded as the candidate posted threatening messages on social media.

Jonathan Miller, a Libertarian challenging a Democratic incumbent for a North Carolina state Senate seat, raised widespread concern Monday when he began making cryptic and viral posts on X suggesting that he was in the process of an armed pursuit with law enforcement.

"The whole police department is chasing me with guns drawn. This should be fun," he wrote around 9:38 a.m. "I've got six shots and the high ground. I hope they don't find me, but if they do it's going to be a hell of a show."

LIBERTARIAN TECH MOGUL TO HIT DSA WITH AN EIGHT-FIGURE SPENDING NUKE

Police stand armed outside Jonathan D. Miller’s apartment; Miller's mugshot  (WRAL; Cary Police Department)

A number of individuals pleaded with Miller to lay down his arms and surrender, including 2024 Libertarian presidential nominee Chase Oliver. Responding to Oliver at 10:04 a.m., Miller claimed that he had "reached the tree line," indicating that he was attempting to hide in the woods.

Responding to another user offering to send him money, Miller claimed that he "gave away all [his] money to charity" and that he didn’t "want or need yours."

Police said they received calls from concerned members of the public after Miller posted messages Sunday night suggesting he was considering suicide. Officers attempted to check on his welfare Monday morning, and during a subsequent encounter, Miller was carrying a firearm. Police said he fled after being ordered not to touch the weapon.

'MODERATE' AG'S OFFICE TURNED TO GROUPS THAT SPENT YEARS SUING PRISONS FOR RECRUITING HELP, EMAILS REVEAL

A large law enforcement presence was reported at the scene. (WGMB)

WRAL, a local NBC affiliate, managed to get Miller on the phone during his standoff with the police, where he gave his side of the story.

"Last night I was pretty depressed and suicidal, and I sort of put a suicide note on X, and it has gone quite viral," he told WRAL during one such call. "Actually, millions of people have seen it. I usually get about 50 views on my post, but this one has gone super viral on me. I spent all night crying, and angry and upset, and depressed, and all the emotions. And this morning I was tired, but I had things I needed to do. And I didn't kill myself.

"So I got up and made coffee," Miller continued, "and I had two sips of coffee, and I went outside to have a cigarette, and while I was having a cigarette, a police officer approached me, knew my name, saw that I had a holstered revolver on my hip, so he drew his weapon and told me to keep my hands away from mine. I told him I was going to refuse that order, and I backed away slowly until I was far enough away from him to turn and run, and then I turned and ran into the woods."

DOJ OPENS NEW PATHWAY TO RESTORE FEDERAL GUN RIGHTS FOR CERTAIN BARRED INDIVIDUALS

The North Carolina state Capitol building in Raleigh. (Getty Images)

Cary Police Chief Terry Sult stated that Miller had a warrant for being a felon in possession of a firearm.

Police expressed fear that the public could be in harm's way as a result of the standoff, urging pedestrians and motorists to avoid the surrounding area. The armed confrontation unfolded near Resurrection Lutheran School, a private K-8 school that was placed under a code yellow lockdown for part of Monday as police searched for Miller, according to local reports.

"They had some hostage negotiator type dude call me. While I was distracted talking with him on the phone they were moving in around me. Luckily I spotted one of them and took off," Miller posted to X around 1:46 p.m.

Around 3 p.m., law enforcement officers closed in on Miller and arrested him without incident. He has been charged with communicating threats, resisting a public officer, going armed to the terror of the people and possession of a firearm by a felon. No shots were fired during the confrontation, according to police.

The Libertarian Party of North Carolina told the New York Post that it was "aware of the situation," thanking law enforcement for "pursuing a solution without involving violence."

Miller is running for office on a relatively standard Libertarian platform of weakening legal protections for police, repealing drug laws and reducing economic regulations.

#5688. How the 1966 Midterm Elections Presage 2026

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: How the 1966 Midterm Elections Presage 2026

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Conventional wisdom in U.S. politics holds that foreign policy rarely matters in midterm elections. When it comes to the composition of Congress, voters care about many issues, but ordinarily, what the United States does overseas is usually not among them. Bread-and-butter concerns (or, in today’s parlance, the issue of affordability) are much more likely to drive voters’ decisions about who should represent them. As Thomas E. Ricks wrote for this magazine in 2012, “Americans frankly don’t give a damn about foreign policy, Scarlett. By a 81 to 9 percent margin, they care more about the economy.”

There have, however, been notable exceptions where politics does not stop at the water’s edge, and 2026 is shaping up to be one of those years. It typically takes extraordinary circumstances for foreign policy to influence voting behavior, whether that concerns a full-scale mobilization, as in the 1942 and 1944 elections, or the deployment of large numbers of ground troops, as in the 1970 or 2006 elections. Yet the connection between military operations and economic conditions can also become politically potent.

Conventional wisdom in U.S. politics holds that foreign policy rarely matters in midterm elections. When it comes to the composition of Congress, voters care about many issues, but ordinarily, what the United States does overseas is usually not among them. Bread-and-butter concerns (or, in today’s parlance, the issue of affordability) are much more likely to drive voters’ decisions about who should represent them. As Thomas E. Ricks wrote for this magazine in 2012, “Americans frankly don’t give a damn about foreign policy, Scarlett. By a 81 to 9 percent margin, they care more about the economy.”

There have, however, been notable exceptions where politics does not stop at the water’s edge, and 2026 is shaping up to be one of those years. It typically takes extraordinary circumstances for foreign policy to influence voting behavior, whether that concerns a full-scale mobilization, as in the 1942 and 1944 elections, or the deployment of large numbers of ground troops, as in the 1970 or 2006 elections. Yet the connection between military operations and economic conditions can also become politically potent.

When voters can readily perceive a direct link between a president’s national security decisions and the state of the economy, the conditions are ripe for the opposition party to connect the dots in its argument for rebalancing power on Capitol Hill.

This dynamic was an important part of the story in the 1966 midterm elections.

President Lyndon Johnson knew that the 1966 midterms would not be easy. From the moment he took over the presidency following John F. Kennedy’s assassination in November 1963, he urged his advisors to move quickly and decisively, recognizing that the window for major legislation would begin to close once lawmakers turned their attention from governing to reelection.

The strategy worked. Between 1963 and 1966, Johnson and the Democratic Congress enacted a sweeping array of Great Society programs, including the War on Poverty, the Civil Rights and Voting Rights Acts, Medicare and Medicaid, federal aid to elementary and secondary education as well as higher education, immigration reform, and much more.

Johnson’s decisive defeat of the conservative Republican Barry Goldwater in 1964 greatly strengthened his position. The landslide victory was widely viewed as a mandate for his agenda, and left Democrats with enormous congressional majorities: 295 seats in the House and 68 in the Senate. It also helped discredit conservative extremism within the GOP; within the Democratic Party, the balance of power shifted decisively away from the conservative southern committee chairs and toward liberal northerners.

By 1966, however, Johnson was struggling to hold his coalition together. Beyond the normal rhythms of the electoral cycle, the civil rights agenda was becoming increasingly contentious within the party’s northern wing, which had long supported civil rights when the issue centered on the Deep South. The administration’s proposed open-housing legislation, which would have prohibited racial discrimination in the sale or rental of housing, provoked fierce resistance in cities such as Chicago. Of the violent white mobs he encountered there, Martin Luther King Jr. said, “I’ve been in many demonstrations all across the South, but I can say that I have never seen—even in Mississippi and Alabama—mobs as hostile and as hate-filled as I’m seeing in Chicago.” In Nixonland, the historian Rick Perlstein recounted how Republican candidates in the Midwest spread all sorts of rumors about possible racial violence to stoke the fears of ethnic urban white voters who had long been part of the New Deal coalition.

Although there was a brewing backlash to some domestic initiatives, namely the War on Poverty, much of the growing discontent with the administration centered on Vietnam. Despite the presence of more than 300,000 U.S. troops in Vietnam by the fall 1966, the deployment itself was not yet the central political issue. The anti-war movement, largely concentrated on the left, was still in its formative stages and on the margins of politics. The angle Republicans focused on in their effort to reclaim seats was the economic cost of the war, as well as the Great Society—that is, rising federal deficits and inflationary pressures. While Republicans argued that Johnson was an ambivalent warrior, unwilling to unleash the United States’ full air power against North Vietnam, the economic consequences of the conflict became a centerpiece of their campaign. Republicans remembered how successfully they had used the rising cost of living during the Korean War to regain control of Congress and the White House in 1952.

Americans in 1966 still cared deeply about balanced budgets. The issue carried powerful symbolic weight because voters viewed government finances through the same lens as household finances. Republicans warned that Johnson’s war, combined with his ambitious domestic agenda, was producing large deficits. Those deficits, they argued, were fueling inflation. The federal deficit remained relatively modest by later standards—$2.3 billion in 1966—but it was larger than what Americans had become accustomed to during the 1950s.

Is the United States destined for a repeat of Jan. 6 every election now?

In 1983, the realization the program could implode propelled last-ditch compromises.

Forget hearts and minds, this path to power revolves around appealing to hate and anger.

For most of the post-World War II period, the nation had enjoyed extraordinarily low levels of inflation. In 1966, however, the Consumer Price Index registered the largest increase since 1957. Economists have generally agreed that wartime spending was the central driver, more so than the new domestic programs. At a Republican Party dinner in October 1966, House Minority Leader Gerald Ford warned that Johnson was “planting the seeds of a future depression.”

Worse still, Republicans argued, the increases were concentrated on basic goods such as pork chops, beef, transportation, medical care, and other household items. One internal White House poll found that 76 percent of respondents in California disapproved of the president’s handling of the cost of living. Johnson himself remarked that, whereas politicians in the 1950s could hardly meet a voter without being asked, “What do you think about McCarthy?” (a reference to Cold Warrior Sen. Joseph McCarthy), in 1966 the question had become: “What do you think about inflation?” In October, housewives in many states staged boycotts of local supermarkets to protest higher food prices.

Johnson knew that there was little political support for imposing price controls. He and congressional leaders understood that instead they would have to raise taxes or cut spending. Great Society programs remained popular, but Republicans used the mounting costs of Vietnam to deride “Great Society funny money,” arguing that domestic spending was worsening the problem. Republican campaign pamphlets warned voters that “progress is a shrinking dollar.” GOP candidates staged supermarket photo opportunities to dramatize the issue. “Every housewife who shops in a grocery store knows this,” Senate Minority Leader Everett Dirksen lamented. “They are the living, breathing signs of this destructive burglarizing force.”

Richard Nixon, whose political career had appeared finished after his loss to Kennedy in 1960 and his subsequent defeat in the 1962 California gubernatorial race, reinvented himself by traveling the country on behalf of Republican candidates, speaking about inflation and deficits, and raising over $6 million for their war chests.

Although Johnson delayed consideration of a tax increase, Federal Reserve Chairman William McChesney Martin raised the discount rate from 4 percent to 4.5 percent in December 1965. The decision infuriated the president and contributed to rising interest rates and a credit crunch during the summer of 1966. Housing starts declined, as did homebuilding and commercial construction.

The elections were disappointing for liberal Democrats. Although the party retained control of Congress, its majority fell to 64 seats in the Senate and 248 in the House. Republicans gained 47 seats in the House and three in the Senate. The conservative coalition of southern Democrats and Republicans expanded, recovering much of the ground it had lost in the Democratic landslide of 1964. At the state level, Republicans captured eight governorships and 557 state legislative seats.  The GOP rebounded throughout the Midwest, where Goldwater’s candidacy had cost the party heavily two years earlier.

Surveying the results, pollster George Gallup concluded that Vietnam was the “great underlying issue and probably the prime reason why the G.O.P did so well.” Yet voters had not rewarded Republicans because they opposed the war. Rather, they punished Democrats for a failure to be aggressive enough, and due to the sense that the administration was failing to manage economic strain. California’s newly elected governor, former actor Ronald Reagan, demanded that Johnson authorize the full use of military force to bring the conflict to a decisive conclusion. House Majority Leader Carl Albert later reflected that the election “broke the back of the Great Society right there.”

The predictions proved correct. In 1967 and 1968, as the opposition to the war intensified on the left, Johnson’s coalition began to unravel. Conservatives in Congress forced reductions in domestic spending and blocked further expansive social programs. Faced with the growing costs of war, the administration increasingly chose guns over butter. By the next presidential election, when Johnson withdrew from the race, the Democratic Party was also deeply divided over the war. In 1968, Vice President Hubert Humphrey lost the election to Nixon. A new, more conservative era was underway.

Democrats are currently facing an opportunity similar to the one Republicans confronted in 1966. Although the war in Iran has not approached the scale of Vietnam and probably never will, its economic consequences have been substantial, and in some ways more transparent. Moreover, it has been unpopular from the start.

By destabilizing global oil and trade markets, the operations have directly contributed to higher prices for consumers—who are already struggling to keep up with the cost of living. Whereas Republicans are benefitting from the hundreds of millions of dollars Elon Musk has poured into his super PAC, Democrats may possess an even more powerful campaign message, through the price of gasoline that Americans see at the pump and the price of food they see at the grocery store register. And whenever they turn on the news, they see again that the president who promised to avoid so-called forever wars has entangled the nation in one himself, with working Americans, red and blue, paying the prices at home.

Julian E. Zelizer is a columnist at Foreign Policy and a professor of history and public affairs at Princeton University. He is the author of The Long View, a newsletter putting the news in perspective. In 2027, Norton will publish his new book, Rumble on the Promenade: The Clash of Justice and Power at the 1964 Convention. X: @julianzelizer

#5687. Tesla FSD ‘subscriptions’ hit 1.74M, counted the way Musk’s pay package wants

Tesla says it now has 1.74 million active “Full Self-Driving” subscriptions, up 67% from a year ago. A footnote in its Q3 2026 shareholder letter explains how it gets there: the metric follows “our 2025 CEO Performance Award” and “includes both upfront payment and monthly subscriptions.”

Here’s what Tesla has reported under that definition:

Tesla stopped selling FSD as a one-time purchase on February 14, 2026. New customers can only get it as a subscription, at $99 a month in the US. But everyone who paid upfront before that, at prices that reached $15,000, stays in the count as long as the car is active. The only exclusion is free trials.

We flagged this in April when Tesla reported 1.28 million. Six months later, Tesla still doesn’t say how many of the 1.74 million actually pay every month.

The same approach shows up elsewhere in the letter. Tesla’s “cumulative deliveries” figure, now 10.2 million, also counts “any unsupervised Robotaxis placed into commercial operation,” again to match the pay package, which has a 20 million vehicle milestone.

Tesla also says more than 75% of new deliveries in North America included FSD subscriptions in Q3, “a nearly twenty percentage point sequential increase.” It says it believes “FSD is driving demand in markets where it is available.”

That’s a big number, and Tesla doesn’t define it. The letter doesn’t say whether it counts subscriptions started at delivery with an introductory offer, or how many of those buyers are still paying after a few months. The active-subscription metric excludes free trials, but Tesla doesn’t say the same about the attach rate.

Musk’s 2025 award has 12 tranches. Each requires a market cap milestone and one operational milestone. The operational list includes 20 million vehicles delivered, 10 million active FSD subscriptions, 1 million Optimus robots, 1 million robotaxis in commercial operation, and adjusted EBITDA targets. As of June 30, Tesla considered only one of them probable: 20 million deliveries.

Tesla added 260,000 net FSD subscriptions in Q3, its best quarter yet. At that pace, it would take about eight years to reach 10 million. At the pace of the last 12 months, about 700,000 a year, it would take closer to 12. The award runs until 2035.

Counting people who bought FSD outright isn’t wrong on its own. Those cars do have the software. The problem is calling them “subscriptions” and presenting the total as proof of momentum, when the definition was written for Musk’s pay package and not for investors trying to understand the business.

The metrics that are far off in Musk's 2025 comp plan don't matter because if there is a change of control (i.e., SpaceX buys Tesla), all the comp plan metrics vest and count as achieved, and good old Elon gets paid. And you wonder why he wants this merger?

This is now Tesla’s headline software metric. The board wrote the definition, Musk’s compensation depends on it, and the company won’t say how many people pay $99 a month. Tesla obviously knows that number.

The attach rate is the more interesting claim, if it holds up. A 75% take rate on a $99-a-month product would be a real business. So show us the retention: how many North American buyers who started a subscription in Q2 are still paying today?

With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #5685. The Axis of Upheaval Goes to War

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ANDREA KENDALL-TAYLOR is Senior Fellow and Director of the Transatlantic Security Program at the Center for a New American Security. From 2015 to 2018, she was Deputy National Intelligence Officer for Russia and Eurasia at the National Intelligence Council.

RICHARD FONTAINE is CEO of the Center for a New American Security. He has worked at the U.S. Department of State, on the National Security Council, and as a foreign policy adviser to U.S. Senator John McCain.

How Battlefield Cooperation Is Binding Together America’s Adversaries

Andrea Kendall-Taylor and Richard Fontaine

On July 17, an Iranian missile struck Muwaffaq Salti Air Base in Jordan, killing three American soldiers and wounding four others. The strike was unusually precise. In the weeks that followed, U.S. officials discovered that before the attack, Tehran had received high-resolution satellite images of the base that could aid its targeting. Afterward, Iran obtained more imagery, potentially helping it assess the damage and hone future attacks. The images came from Chinese entities.

China is not alone in assisting Iran. Russia, too, has helped Tehran find and hit American and allied targets. Two days before the March 27 attack on Prince Sultan Air Base in Saudi Arabia that wounded 12 American personnel, 14 Russian spy satellites passed overhead. U.S. officials later concluded that Moscow had fed Iran satellite intelligence that aided Iranian precision strikes. Moscow has also provided Iran with weapons and military expertise, including upgraded versions of Iranian drones refined on the battlefield in Ukraine, and Russian engineers are helping Iran develop supersonic cruise missiles that could evade U.S. and Israeli air defenses.

Neither Russia nor China has directly entered Iran’s war with the United States. They haven’t needed to. By providing intelligence on American targets, military technology and components, and economic and diplomatic support, they can give Iran what it needs without joining its war.

The United States entered the conflict with advantages its adversaries lacked: a vast network of formal allies, military bases, intelligence-sharing arrangements, defense partnerships, and interoperable forces spanning Europe, the Middle East, and Asia. Yet many of Washington’s traditional partners were reluctant to associate themselves with the campaign, and some denied the United States specific forms of military access, at least for a time. Others publicly resisted U.S. calls to help secure the Strait of Hormuz.

The contrast illustrates a striking shift since we first described Russia, China, Iran, and North Korea as an axis of upheaval in Foreign Affairs two years ago. The United States’ adversaries began this war with this weaker network, and Washington with the far stronger one. But after helping Russia fight its war in Ukraine over the past few years, members of the axis are now finding ways to make their network work in a new theater. They are fine-tuning their cooperation to make their partners more resilient—and more dangerous to the United States—while limiting costs and risks. Washington, for its part, is creating friction in many of its partnerships instead of deepening them, and risks squandering its network just as its adversaries are learning to make more of theirs.

Much has changed since 2024. Most obviously, the United States itself has become a source of upheaval in the international system. The Trump administration has challenged international institutions, strained traditional alliances, and adopted a more transactional approach to foreign policy. It has upended trade agreements, threatened the sovereignty of allies such as Canada and Denmark, and embraced unpredictability. It has also pursued improved relations with China, North Korea, and Russia while seeking to weaken Iran. None of this has loosened the ties among the United States’ adversaries.

Early in the Trump administration, U.S. officials expressed hope that normalizing relations with Moscow might peel Russia away from the group. Yet President Donald Trump’s oft-expressed bonhomie toward the Kremlin has opened no gaps with the other three. As seen from Moscow, the administration has been unable to deliver a deal on Ukraine, and any Trump-driven rapprochement with Washington is unlikely to endure: Congress continues to pressure Russia, and a future administration may well adopt a tougher approach.

More fundamentally, attempts to weaken the axis misunderstand what holds it together. Its members object to an international order that they see as enshrining Western dominance at their expense. But more pointedly, they oppose the United States’ extraordinary power to constrain, isolate, and punish them, and potentially to threaten their regimes. Recent U.S. actions have only rekindled those concerns. The United States’ capture of Venezuelan leader Nicolás Maduro and the killing of Iranian Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei reinforce long-standing fears in Beijing, Moscow, Pyongyang, and Tehran about U.S. intervention and regime security.

The axis countries may moderate their anti-American rhetoric when doing so is tactically useful, but their motivation to reduce their vulnerability to American power remains. The Trump administration’s own skepticism about international order will not ease minds in axis capitals. On the contrary, Trump’s efforts to create a more transactional and coercive world may increase the value of their partnerships. As rules and institutions constrain less and brute power matters more, countries will seek partners to help them blunt sanctions, access critical technologies, compensate for military weaknesses, expand their room for maneuver, and resist isolation. Axis cooperation is evolving to meet the moment.

When Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, cooperation among the four countries was relatively easy to map. Iran provided Russia with drones, and North Korea sent ammunition, ballistic missiles, and later, troops. China became Moscow’s most critical lifeline, buying Russian oil and supplying the machine tools, microelectronics, and other components it needed first to sustain and then to expand its military production. Russia, in turn, transferred increasingly sophisticated military technology to its partners.

These exchanges were consequential, but they could still largely be understood as a series of bilateral transactions focused on Russia’s war. That is becoming less true. Cooperation is increasingly networked and geared toward solving new problems in new regions.

Russia’s drone program illustrates the change. Iran initially sold Russia its Shahed drones and then helped Moscow establish production at home. Russia did not simply replicate what it received; it incorporated Chinese components and continually modified the drones’ navigation, communications, payloads, propulsion, and tactics in response to Ukrainian defenses. New military units fed successful modifications back into production, generating an unusually rapid cycle of battlefield adaptation. Russia’s Unmanned Systems Forces, a drone-and-robotics branch of the Russian armed forces, was established last year to help institutionalize this cycle. The combination of domestic adaptation and axis partnership has proved effective. Russia’s latest jet-powered Geran drones, for instance, are faster, carry larger payloads, and are significantly harder for Ukrainian forces to intercept than earlier variants.

Russian improvements do not stay in Russia, either. They circulate through the axis network. For Iran, the technology that originally flowed to Russia is now returning, along with tactical advice drawn from Russia’s experience in Ukraine. If Russia’s jet-powered drones also show up in the Middle East, as they likely will, Iranian attacks on U.S. forces and regional energy infrastructure will become harder and costlier to defend against.

North Korea and China are also benefiting. According to Ukrainian reports, Russia struck a deal to help North Korea expand production of its Shahed-type drones. North Korean engineers have learned from time spent working in Russia’s massive Yelabuga drone factory, and North Korean forces fighting alongside Russia have gained firsthand experience in integrating drones into modern combat. Chinese military personnel have received training in drone warfare from Russian forces, and Chinese suppliers are gaining valuable information about how their technologies perform in combat. Beijing is thus seeing gains from the evolution of drone warfare that began with Iranian technology and has been refined in Russia’s war in Ukraine.

Two years ago, it was not clear whether partnerships among the four members of the axis could outlast the war that accelerated their formation. If the Ukraine war ended and Russia no longer needed Iranian drones, North Korean ammunition, or Chinese economic and technological support, the relationships might lose momentum.

Yet the war did not only create an immediate need for cooperation. It has also provided proof of concept for what these partnerships can accomplish, beyond what any country might attain on its own. Ukrainian defenses, Western sanctions, U.S. military supplies, and diplomatic isolation have all been less effective than would be the case if Russia were operating alone. By helping one another withstand pressure, the axis countries make it harder for the United States and its allies to compel them to change course.

Iran is now showing that the model can travel. Russia’s support for Tehran has been operationally meaningful but carefully bounded. Even as it fights its own war in Ukraine, Moscow has found ways to help Iran strike American targets and sustain the fight through diplomatic cover and material support while stopping short of actions that could draw Russia into direct conflict with the United States. China has been even more cautious, but its sharing of dual-use electronics and purchases of Iranian oil have similarly enhanced Iran’s ability to withstand U.S. pressure.

Such restraint is not evidence that the axis is weakening but rather a defining feature of how these partnerships work. With the striking exception of North Korean soldiers deployed on Russia’s behalf, the axis countries have generally avoided fighting one another’s wars. Instead, they provide political backing, economic support, intelligence, technology, and limited military assistance when doing so advances their own interests at manageable risk.

The wars in Ukraine and Iran have taught the axis another lesson: supporting a fellow member does not merely strengthen the recipient. Conflict in one theater can also create opportunities for partners elsewhere. Iran’s closure of the Strait of Hormuz, for example, sent oil prices soaring, delivering a windfall to Russia and prompting Washington to temporarily ease restrictions on Russian oil exports. Iran’s war thus generated resources Russia could use to sustain its own.

Before attacking Iran, the United States redeployed key military assets, including air and missile defenses, from the Indo-Pacific to the Middle East. As the fighting continued, U.S. forces put two aircraft carriers in the region, leaving none in the Indo-Pacific. China gains operational freedom from a thinner U.S. military presence in the western Pacific and is less vulnerable when U.S. munitions stocks are depleted. If Washington settles into a prolonged strategy of containing Iran, as it did with Iraq in the 1990s, Beijing could enjoy the advantages of those diversions for years.

The experience of the axis of upheaval shows that the size and strength of a network matter less if its members cannot turn their individual capabilities into collective power. The United States and its allies start from a position of considerable advantage: greater economic and technological resources, more capable militaries, and a far larger network of allies and partners. The question is which network can make better use of its assets.

That starts with leadership. For decades, the United States favored an international order that forbade wars of conquest, upheld freedom of the seas, maintained a generally open global economy, and allowed populations to exercise democratic rights, even if autocracies remained. Washington coupled this vision with an implicit bargain: any country that wished to join the order could do so. Upholding the order’s basic principles conferred benefits, including from the United States: trade relations, diplomatic preferences, sometimes military alliances.

For countries that rejected the open international order, as the axis countries do today, the benefits were far fewer and the penalties considerable. The United States sanctioned, pressured, and sometimes went to war against rogue states, and it resisted the establishment of competing orders such as the Cold War communist bloc. U.S. foreign policy started from the seemingly uncontroversial premise that Washington should befriend like-minded countries and treat them better than rejectionists.

The Trump administration demonstrates, at least at times, something like the opposite inclination. The U.S. ambassador to China recently said that Trump offered to sell Beijing weapons, while stalling arms sales to Taiwan. The president has expressed greater interest in and affection for North Korea’s dictator, Kim Jong Un, than for South Korea’s democratically elected president, Lee Jae-myung. The administration has also intermittently pressured Ukraine to cave in its war of survival against Russian aggression. And Trump has threatened to bomb Oman, a longtime partner. Such moves both strengthen the axis countries and weaken the cohesion of the network best positioned to confront them.

Doing no further harm would be a good start in dealing with the axis. Beyond that, Washington should help its partners grow stronger. Just as the axis countries are helping one another work around sanctions, preserve access to critical technologies and components, and sustain their economies under pressure, the United States should aid its own network. That means strengthening supply chains that run through trusted partners, sharing technology, and encouraging allies to take on greater security burdens. Washington must take care, however, not to transfer defense responsibilities faster than allies can develop the capabilities to assume them—and not to do so in ways that erode trust, interoperability, and coordination. Otherwise, the United States and its allies could become individually more capable but collectively less effective.

The United States should also reverse policies that undercut its own network. Tariffs and other protectionist measures harm allied economies and their industrial bases. Expressing a desire to seize allies’ territory and attempting to undermine their political leaders do not make for a more capable international network. Washington should project vision and leadership to its friends, not jarring uncertainty and bullying transactionalism.

Even if the United States changes course, American and allied policymakers should prepare for a long struggle. As the unsuccessful regime-change effort in Iran demonstrated, there are no shortcuts to besting the axis countries, together or individually. The contest of networks will be nearly global, waged in multiple domains, with huge stakes riding on the outcome. The United States and its allies have the capacity to prevail. They should demonstrate the will.

#5684. Could Victory for Netanyahu Mean Mass Emigration?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Could Victory for Netanyahu Mean Mass Emigration?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The week before Israel’s parliamentary elections, Amir, an Israeli technology freelancer, traveled to the Greek island of Evia to view properties for sale. Amir had planned to purchase a holiday home for himself and his wife. (He asked to be identified only by his first name to discuss private matters.) However, the property could become their permanent residence if Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s far-right coalition stays in power after the Oct. 27 vote, he said.

“We’ve been talking about [leaving] for a long time,” Amir said. “Most people I know are waiting for the results of the elections to have that discussion on a more practical and serious level.”

The week before Israel’s parliamentary elections, Amir, an Israeli technology freelancer, traveled to the Greek island of Evia to view properties for sale. Amir had planned to purchase a holiday home for himself and his wife. (He asked to be identified only by his first name to discuss private matters.) However, the property could become their permanent residence if Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s far-right coalition stays in power after the Oct. 27 vote, he said.

“We’ve been talking about [leaving] for a long time,” Amir said. “Most people I know are waiting for the results of the elections to have that discussion on a more practical and serious level.”

Nearly one-fifth of Israeli voters are considering emigrating because of the country’s security and social situation, according to an Oct. 4 survey from the Hebrew University of Jerusalem. Other polls from this past summer showed similar results. The recent survey found that Israelis are deeply uncertain about the country’s future, with fewer than one-quarter of residents believing their children will live in a better reality.

Israelis have expressed feeling increasingly unsafe since the Oct. 7, 2023, Hamas attacks and amid Israel’s wars with Gaza, Lebanon, and Iran. The cost of living has skyrocketed, with food prices increasing by 20 percent in the last four years. Israeli society is also deeply polarized, with the secular liberal and religious-nationalist factions at odds. Netanyahu’s critics fear that his coalition, if victorious, will advance policies that favor the religious nationalists at the expense of other Israelis.

The latest polls show that it is highly possible that neither Netanyahu’s coalition nor the main opposition bloc will secure a parliamentary majority in the elections, which would yield a hung parliament and set the stage for coalition negotiations. If those efforts to secure a majority ultimately failed, Netanyahu—Israel’s longest-serving prime minister—would stay on as caretaker until a new vote can be held.

If thousands of people decide to emigrate based on the election’s outcome and aftermath, Israel could lose highly skilled workers in key sectors who are sustaining the economy and tend to belong to the country’s more secular factions. Such departures would unsettle the political balance among the remaining population, which would in turn lead to weaker internal opposition.

Many Israelis consider the upcoming parliamentary election—the first held since the Oct. 7 attacks, in which around 1,200 people were killed and 251 were taken hostage—the most important in the country’s history. Israel faces growing international isolation over its war in Gaza and rising settler violence in the West Bank. More than 74,000 people have been killed in Gaza, and much of the enclave has been destroyed since the war began.

Israeli society was polarized before the Hamas attacks in 2023. Earlier that year, Netanyahu’s government proposed a judicial reform bill that would have limited Supreme Court oversight of lawmaking and policy and, according to critics, destabilized Israeli democracy by undermining the balance of power. Thousands of Israelis took to the streets to protest the measure, creating deep rifts within the population. Ultimately, the court struck down the bill.

Such fissures only deepened after Oct. 7. Current and former Israeli military and defense officials accused Netanyahu of pursuing flawed strategies to address the hostage crisis, while the families of the hostages accused the government of inaction. Some pro-war Israelis supported extreme military pressure in Gaza, even as swaths of the population regularly protested the war.

Thousands of Israel Defense Forces (IDF) reservists have served hundreds of days in Gaza and Lebanon. Meanwhile, members of the ultra-Orthodox Jewish population—the country’s fastest-growing population group—have rarely completed military service in any capacity. In 2024, the Supreme Court ruled that Haredi men could no longer be exempted from conscription and ordered the military to begin drafting them. Yet ultra-Orthodox military service and workforce participation remain low, fueling resentment among other Israelis.

The cost of living has continued to rise, in part due to unaddressed structural issues such as high market concentration. Increased tax and utility fees have also led to cost increases, while other wartime costs have deepened the country’s debt. Israel’s tourism and construction sectors have been hit especially hard by the ongoing conflict and labor shortages resulting from conscription, as well as the suspension of work permits for Palestinians from the West Bank.

Taken together, these trends have worsened divisions between the nationalist and religious factions that support Netanyahu and his far-right allies and the more liberal, secular factions that want to see that coalition out.

The country’s coming election has become focused on the status of the country’s ultra-Orthodox at wartime.

Netanyahu’s opponents are ahead but will struggle to form a coalition.

The real action starts after polls close and the coalition wrangling begins.

Netanyahu has served as Israel’s prime minister for nearly 19 years. His Likud party is polling neck-and-neck with Yashar, the party founded last year by Gadi Eisenkot, his former military chief of staff. Eisenkot has formed an alliance with three smaller parties, but this opposition bloc is likely to fall short of an outright majority. After the election, he could seek an agreement with Israel’s two predominantly Arab opposition parties to form a minority government, though the political viability of such a deal remains uncertain.

The opposition parties blame Netanyahu for the security failures that led to the Oct. 7 attacks and for failing to eliminate threats from Hamas, Hezbollah, and Iran. They agree on the need for an independent commission to investigate the Israeli leadership’s responsibility for the attacks and have pledged to enforce mandatory conscription for ultra-Orthodox men.

Israeli journalist Amir Tibon recently wrote in The Atlantic that a victory for Netanyahu could prompt an immediate “exodus of educated, highly skilled Israelis.” Tibon moved back to his home in the Nahal Oz kibbutz this month, three years after surviving the Hamas attacks. But he said that he and his wife “struggle to imagine any kind of future here if this government stays in power” and that many of his friends and relatives have already left Israel.

Indeed, after years of relatively stable migration patterns, nearly 270,000 Israelis left the country for at least three consecutive months between 2023 and 2025, according to an August study by Tel Aviv University. That amounts to roughly 90,000 departures annually—around 50 percent above the annual average for the preceding decade.

Researchers identified increased emigration among physicians, Ph.D. holders, engineers, and individuals with relatively high incomes. In the last three years, Israel experienced a “medical exodus,” with almost 1,400 doctors leaving for a period longer than 12 months—a 94 percent surge compared with a similar period between 2013 and 2015.

Lilach Lev Ari, a professor of sociology at Oranim College, surveyed Israelis who emigrated between 2023 and 2025. She said that 20 percent of respondents reported they would never move back to Israel, roughly double what Lev Ari found in studies from previous years. “What is unique about this cohort are the security considerations, the war, and particularly the socio-political situation in Israel,” Lev Ari told Foreign Policy.

The emigration of highly skilled workers isn’t unique to Israel, but its economy may be particularly sensitive to such trends because it relies heavily on high-quality human capital, especially in tech, health care, academia, and other knowledge-intensive sectors. Further, just 20 percent of Israel’s workers fund 93 percent of all income-tax revenue, according to the Shoresh Institution; half of the country’s earners are so poor that they do not pay any income tax.

“Risk is particularly acute in Israel, where a relatively small group of a few hundred thousand highly skilled people plays a disproportionate role in sustaining the country’s economy and essential institutions,” said Itai Ater, a business economics professor at Tel Aviv University. “The departure of even a fraction of this group could trigger a self-reinforcing dynamic: As more people leave, the country’s economic prospects and essential services deteriorate, giving others stronger incentives to leave and potentially setting off a much broader wave of emigration.”

Mass emigration among this largely liberal and secular cohort could shift Israel’s demographic and political balance toward religious-nationalist factions. Over time, that shift could influence policy on the Israeli-Palestinian conflict, civil liberties, judicial authority, and the relationship between religion and state. The erosion of a counterweight to hard-line forces could induce a perpetual feedback loop of polarization and further emigration.

Israeli activist Hagai Agmon-Snir and his wife left Israel for Italy this summer after decades of promoting inclusivity and equality in his home country. As a co-founder of the Jerusalem Intercultural Center, Agmon-Snir helped initiate talks between the LGBTQ and ultra-Orthodox communities, train thousands of police officers in nonviolent methods, and assist an initiative that taught tens of thousands of Israelis to speak Arabic.

Even though his quality of life in Israel was “excellent,” Agmon-Snir said he couldn’t ignore politics. “We left because Israel has become hopeless, and we felt the need to distance ourselves from that evil,” he said. He described his decision as “ideological emigration,” which he said is relatively rare compared with departures driven by fears “that Israel is going to collapse or … that their privileges are going to be lost.”

After Agmon-Snir’s story became public in an interview with Haaretz, he said that dozens of people reached out to learn more about his decision and ask for advice. He and his wife are returning to Israel to vote on Oct. 27, believing they can still help mitigate local injustices from abroad. “We chose to use our freedom and our capabilities to keep on fighting from outside the country with integrity,” Agmon-Snir said.

Giovanni Legorano is a journalist based in Italy.

#5683. House report says NYC prosecutors tailored plea deals to shield illegal immigrants from deportation

ICE New York City Field Office Director Ken Genalo reveals the criminal histories of the suspects charged in the stabbing of an NYPD detective. Genalo discusses Dominican Republic nationals D'Angelo Rafael Jean Caraballo, Sneider Mejia Bautista, and Eduardo Paulino, highlighting their prior arrests and immigration status. Genalo calls for cooperation between local law enforcement and federal immigration authorities to protect communities.

New York City prosecutors from all five boroughs have given "preferential treatment" to noncitizens who commit crimes, all to help them avoid deportation, according to a report from the GOP-led House Judiciary Committee.

The committee, chaired by Rep. Jim Jordan, R-Ohio, said nonpublic documents obtained through its oversight work revealed new details about the extent to which the city's district attorney's offices sought to limit the immigration consequences of criminal prosecutions.

Some of the report's most serious allegations came against Bronx District Attorney Darcel Clark. The report claims she "trains prosecutors in her office to conceal certain facts" from defendants' conviction records and pursue charges that would not trigger deportation.

Internal materials from the Bronx DA's office explicitly instruct prosecutors to leave references to firearms out of conviction records to maintain "immigration-conscious plea bargaining," according to the report.

Bronx County District Attorney Darcel Clark attends the 54th Annual Bronx Ball at Bally's Golf Links at Ferry Point on May 16, 2026 in Bronx, New York. (Joy Malone/Getty Images)

SOROS-BACKED DA ACCUSED OF GOING EASY ON ILLEGAL IMMIGRANTS GETS SQUEEZED BY HOUSE GOP

In cases of domestic violence or crimes against children — offenses that are often deportable under the Immigration and Nationality Act — Bronx prosecutors were told to leave out defendants' relationships with their alleged victims and pursue charges that did not specify the ages of child victims.

Clark, who has been the Bronx DA since 2016, was also accused of encouraging prosecutors to charge foreign nationals with "nonexistent, legally impossible" crimes to skirt deportation.

The report's authors offered the example of Clark's staff getting someone to plead guilty to "attempted reckless assault," a crime they described as legally impossible because someone cannot intend to act recklessly. A plea to such a crime, they said, could allow a noncitizen defendant to stay in the country indefinitely.

New York courts nevertheless allow defendants to plead guilty to such hypothetical offenses as part of negotiated plea deals. The Court of Appeals, the state's highest court, upheld the legal concept this year.

The New York State Court of Appeals building on Oct. 2, 2024, in Albany, New York. (Will Waldron/Albany Times Union via Getty Images)

DENVER, BOULDER IGNORED ICE DETAINERS FOR MIGRANTS WITH CHILD ABUSE, ASSAULT ARRESTS, HOUSE GOP REPORT REVEALS

The practice is not limited to the Bronx. State data obtained by the Immigrant Defense Project showed 289 convictions for attempted reckless assault across New York City from 2015 through 2025, including 50 in the Bronx.

The House Judiciary Committee also pointed to similar practices within the Queens DA's office, led by Melinda Katz since 2020.

It cited a May 2026 email from a Queens prosecutor informing an NYPD detective that the DA's office had "basically made a [plea] deal which is ‘immigration safe’ while still having [the defendant] plea to multiple crimes and do some jail time."

Queens prosecutors were also instructed to "exercise extreme caution" when resolving cases involving noncitizens and to consult the office's immigration specialist when considering plea deals for them, the report said.

Queens District Attorney Melinda Katz is pictured during the arraignment of Roman Amatitla in Queens, New York, on April 9, 2026. (Theodore Parisienne / New York Daily News via Getty Images)

WATCH: DEM DEMANDS 'ONE CASE' WHERE SANCTUARY CITIES DIDN'T TURN OVER ILLEGAL IMMIGRANTS; DHS PROVIDES LIST

Over in the Manhattan office, the report said DA Alvin Bragg issued a memo in 2022 telling prosecutors to "seek dispositions that avoid immigration consequences for all misdemeanors, and all felonies" where prosecutors generally did not expect defendants to receive jail time.

Bragg's office responded to the committee by describing its January 2022 policy as "outdated," adding that it had updated it to give prosecutors discretion on individual cases.

The DA's offices in Brooklyn and Staten Island were alleged to have used comparable tactics when dealing with noncitizen defendants, according to the report.

In a statement to Fox News Digital, the Staten Island DA's office called the House Judiciary Committee's report "flagrantly false and patently political."

"[The Richmond County District Attorney's Office] has and will continue to ask ICE to let us prosecute and convict criminals before they deport them because Congress has failed to secure our borders. That is the only way to guarantee our victims justice and to keep our communities safe. If you believe in law and order, that’s the only way to do the job," the office said.

Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg speaks at a press conference as he comments on the outcome in the retrial of former film producer Harvey Weinstein on June 12, 2025 in New York City. (Yuki Iwamura-Pool/Getty Images)

DHS SECRETARY BASHES NEW YORK LEADERSHIP AFTER ANNOUNCING THOUSANDS OF ARRESTS IN SWEEPING OPERATION

The four other offices referenced in the report did not immediately respond to a Fox News Digital request for comment. The mayor's office also did not immediately respond.

Citywide sanctuary policies led the NYPD to decline 20,255 immigration detainers between July 2018 and June 2026, the report said. Detainers are federal requests asking local authorities to hold certain noncitizens briefly so Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) can take them into custody.

The city's Department of Correction also declined all 2,852 ICE detainers it received during the same period, according to the report.

These figures are not necessarily surprising, given that each mayoral administration throughout that period has limited city agencies from cooperating with ICE.

Federal agents walk near the scene where a man was shot by an Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) officer during an operation in the Bronx borough of New York City on Oct. 8, 2026. (Charly Triballeau / AFP via Getty Images)

What stands out, however, is that nearly half of those detainers — almost 10,000 — were lodged between July 2025 and June 2026, an indicator that the second Trump administration has acted far more aggressively in its attempts to scoop up noncitizens arrested by local police than either the Biden administration or the first Trump administration.

The House Judiciary Committee's findings come as House Republicans push legislation that would prevent state and local governments from restricting cooperation with federal immigration authorities.

In March, the committee advanced the Shut Down Sanctuary Policies Act — a bill that has stalled in the House — and the report said lawmakers would continue investigating sanctuary jurisdictions.
