#5661. The Fog of Tech

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Why the Future of War Can’t Escape the Past

At a time when debates about national security tend to be polarized along partisan and ideological lines, there is one thing analysts and policymakers across the political spectrum seem to agree on: the future of warfare will be determined by technology. Hypersonic missiles, quantum computing, drone swarms, and artificial intelligence have all been hailed as transformative technologies that will shape battlefields, remake strategies, and upend established military doctrines.

Without question, these technologies are already having a tremendous impact. In Gaza, Iran, Lebanon, Ukraine, and elsewhere, every party with the capacity to employ artificial intelligence and autonomous systems has done so—in the air, on land, and at sea, and at times in massive numbers. And there is also no question that in order to maintain its status as the world’s preeminent fighting force, the U.S. military must ensure that it has the most sophisticated and cutting-edge capabilities available. To continue developing and operationalizing these technologies, defense planners should prioritize scaling and integrating them and developing innovative operational concepts to employ them. The risk of being overtaken by rivals such as China is real.

But an equally significant risk exists: call it “the technology trap,” in which policymakers develop a misplaced sense of certainty about how innovations will change war and what warfare will look like in the future, while not paying enough attention to the unchanging fundamentals of war. An excessive focus on the role of technology would be shortsighted and lead militaries in dangerous directions, especially if planners are highly confident about how new capabilities will develop and make decisions in line with that confidence—which will almost certainly be misplaced.

For war planners, faith in technological answers can obscure the most fundamental questions: Who kills, who dies, and who decides? In the post–Cold War era, this myopia has contributed to dreadful outcomes in Afghanistan, Iraq, Ukraine, and Gaza. In the decade to come, as U.S.-Chinese tensions persist, the fog of tech could help pave the way for a disastrous great-power war. Policymakers should worry less about getting the right technology at the right time and more about how fixating on technology might make them lose sight of the essential elements of war and find comfort in false certainty.

More than 50 years ago, the military historian Michael Howard famously cautioned that future wars are unknowable. “I am tempted indeed to declare dogmatically that whatever doctrine the Armed Forces are working on now, they have got it wrong,” he wrote. “I am also tempted to declare that it does not matter that they have got it wrong. What does matter is their capacity to get it right quickly when the moment arrives.”

But Howard may have been a bit too sanguine about the costs of getting it wrong. For Americans, the paradigmatic example from recent history is how the U.S. military’s technological prowess in the 1990–91 Gulf War seemed to herald a new, high-tech American way of war that would guarantee victory in the post–Cold War era. Instead, Washington slowly and painfully learned that the technological advantages that beat Saddam Hussein’s regime could not defeat insurgencies in Afghanistan and Iraq during the post-9/11 wars.

Predicting the role of technology in future wars has become even harder today thanks to the speed, scale, and complexity of contemporary technological progress. In the past, transformative innovations tended to be expensive at their outset—in resources or personnel—or required specialized knowledge and skills accessible to only a few. Think of the combined power of the rifle, the railroad, and the telegraph in the nineteenth century, or fighter jets and nuclear weapons in the twentieth century. By contrast, many of today’s technologies have low barriers to entry: they are cheap to produce, and access to the knowledge necessary to make them has been democratized and globalized. Enemies today often face each other on more equal battlefields, making it trickier for war planners to understand when and for how long new tools will confer an outsize advantage.

Focusing too much on procuring the shiniest equipment also obscures the ways that high-end technology works in tandem with more basic tools. In the 2010s, defense planners failed to account for this dynamic when predicting future battlefields, which they believed would be dominated by sophisticated platforms. In 2017, a senior Pentagon official declared that the F-35 stealth fighter would be “a war winner.” The 2018 National Defense Strategy, drafted under the direction of Elbridge Colby, who now serves as undersecretary of defense, anticipated that a few primarily high-end capabilities—including advanced computing, artificial intelligence, robotics, hypersonics, and biotechnology—would “ensure we will be able to fight and win the wars of the future.”

Instead, what emerged were conflicts such as the war in Ukraine, which can be described as World War I meets World War III. Sophisticated Russian ballistic missiles wreak havoc on Ukrainian cities, as do precision-guided glide bombs delivered by fighter aircraft out of the range of air defenses. At the same time, however, highly lethal bombs are dropped by drones carried aloft by simple balloons, and one of the most effective antidrone defenses consists of fishermen’s nets (or similar netting) draped over security checkpoints, critical infrastructure, and, in the case of Izium, an entire small Ukrainian city. A sense of certainty that sophisticated tech would dominate battlefields represented a failure of imagination that has produced real-world consequences: a dozen years ago, few would have predicted that during a U.S.-Israeli war on Iran, cheap Iranian drones would assault soft targets in Gulf Arab states, as they have since February.

An obsession with technology can obscure the precept that war remains a human endeavor. As the military theorist Carl von Clausewitz said almost two centuries ago, “War is not the action of a living force upon a lifeless mass . . . but always the collision of two living forces.” Technology can inadvertently make war appear mechanical and sterile. But the nature of war has not changed. Its language is violence, and technology simply offers new and different ways to communicate. Therefore, the fundamental questions of war remain salient regardless of technological change: What are the ends (particularly with respect to politics), the ways, and the means?

Perhaps the most perilous time for leaders to be distracted by the allure of technology is when considering whether to go to war in the first place. They often mistakenly believe that technology will make doing so less costly in political terms, particularly if they assume innovations will help reduce casualties. A fixation on the tangible and quantifiable advantages technology confers can detract from vital debate and discussion of the broader consequences of resorting to violence. This dynamic likely influenced Russia’s decision to invade Ukraine. Putin and his advisers believed that the Russian military was sufficiently capable thanks to a massive technological reform effort that began in 2009. In the run-up to the 2022 full-scale invasion, Russian Defense Minister Sergei Shoigu proudly claimed that more than 70 percent of Russia’s military had advanced weapons. Within weeks of the invasion, it had become clear this had conferred little advantage on Moscow. The same goes for the U.S.-Israeli attempt to topple the Iranian regime in 2026, a decision shaped in part by the belief that the sophistication of the American military, particularly when compared with its opponent, made success almost inevitable. In the wake of U.S. strikes on Iran’s nuclear sites in 2025, Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth declared that the attack showed Tehran that Washington’s military capabilities are “nearly unlimited.” In fact, the limits of those capabilities became glaringly obvious when the Iranian regime withstood the 2026 onslaught and even managed to close the Strait of Hormuz.

There are ways to avoid the technology trap. The first is to determine a clear goal before going to war. Planners must develop a theory of victory before a single shot is fired and then steadily put that theory into practice. These critical processes cannot be negated by or outsourced to technology. Before launching the 2026 war on Iran, the Trump administration failed to clearly articulate the operation’s goal, instead offering a shifting set of justifications and rationales and various descriptions of the desired end state. The hope seems to have been that technological wizardry would make it possible to achieve everything on a long wish list without having to prioritize or navigate tradeoffs.

To clarify goals, planners must identify what Clausewitz termed “the center of gravity” of a military operation: “the hub of all power and movement, on which everything depends.” This is where an opponent’s power originates, so it needs to be the focus of a military’s attention and resources. During the 1990–91 Gulf War, the commander of U.S. forces in the Middle East, General Norman Schwarzkopf, identified the center of gravity as Saddam Hussein’s command and control of his military. Accordingly, U.S. strategy focused on impeding Saddam’s ability to communicate with his troops and weakening the Iraqi military’s ability to execute orders—a state of affairs that helped convince Saddam to accept a cease-fire. Without this clarity about where to direct efforts and how to measure their impact, a military will struggle to achieve its ends. Early in the 2003 Iraq war, the U.S. military’s inability to identify the Iraqi population as the center of gravity—that is, failing to understand that the political and military leadership no longer wielded control—resulted in years of devastating losses by encouraging tactics that fomented anti-American sentiment, fueled an insurgency, and made it more difficult to establish a credible and functioning government.

Another way to avoid the technology trap is to conduct deliberate, impartial, and regular reviews of lessons learned during and between wars. Iran closely studied the attacks that Israel and the United States carried out on its nuclear sites in 2025. Recognizing that another war would likely pose an existential risk to the Islamic Republic regime, Iranian security officials engaged in a serious and intense effort to prepare. By devolving command and control at both the political and the military levels during the 2026 war, the regime has survived a lengthy onslaught. U.S. allies are, of course, equally capable of such adaptation. In recent years, NATO’s Joint Analysis and Lessons Learned Centre has maintained a team of researchers dedicated to studying the war in Ukraine, a smart way to institutionalize this task and a potential model to apply to other theaters.

The combination of a focus on strategic aims, preparation, and learning remains crucial for effective decision-making in war and cannot be replaced by technology. Similarly, the ways and means of warfare are influenced by technology but have not been fundamentally changed by it. Consider, first, the importance of coalitions and the difficulties of managing them. Going to war exposes the distinct and sometimes even opposing interests, objectives, and perspectives of allies. Planners can devise workarounds when they face disagreements in the heat of conflict, but those may be short-term fixes that mask strategic cleavages.

In early 2026, the United States and Israel launched the war on Iran relying solely on each other. Although the U.S. and Israeli militaries have never operated more closely together in the history of their relationship, their limited success calls into question the partnership’s strategic acumen, despite its remarkable technological capabilities. The two countries notably failed to bring in other partners in the region or in Europe. Early on, several U.S. allies and partners denied the American military access to their territory, bases, and airspace. Although U.S. defense planners have always understood that they might not receive such permissions—Turkey, for example, was unwilling to let the U.S. military invade Iraq from Turkish territory in 2003—the resistance this time was of a different quality and scale. Although most countries ultimately acquiesced, the ones that initially declined threw a wrench into U.S. defense planning, since the American way of war is predicated on an unparalleled global basing network. According to several media reports, Israel found ways around this challenge by setting up a military staging area in Iran during the 2025 war and one in Iraq in 2026. But the ad hoc nature of such creative approaches causes problems for military planning.

As the war has dragged on, the Arab Gulf states have not formally joined the U.S.-Israeli coalition, although at various times during the conflict, Bahrain, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, and the United Arab Emirates have used military force against Iran. Their willingness to use force was significant in and of itself, but it did not influence the battlefield, despite some of the sophisticated capabilities those countries possess. On the other side, Iran masterfully managed its proxy, the Lebanese militia Hezbollah, pulling it into the conflict and enabling Tehran to project power through relatively unsophisticated technologies such as rockets, mortars, and most notably, cheap drones that Hezbollah repeatedly deployed against Israeli soldiers in Lebanon and Israeli civilians inside Israel. Meanwhile, Russia also helped Iran target U.S. military facilities in the Gulf, resulting in devastating (though not determinative) consequences.

The complexities of coalition maintenance are on display in Europe, as well, where defense planners are wrestling with a security trilemma. First, they must figure out how to incorporate a highly capable and modernized Ukrainian military into continental defense planning. As Germany turbocharges investments in its military, Europe must also contend with a shift in its internal balance of military power—one with uneasy historical echoes. And, incredibly, during the past year, Europeans have had to consider the risk of aggression on the part of the United States, as President Donald Trump mused about securing U.S. control of Greenland. These three realities introduce a host of tradeoffs into European strategy, since prioritizing any one of them would likely make it harder to deal with the other two—and no technology offers a way out.

Technological determinism can also lead to faulty assumptions about casualties—both civilian and military. Today, target selection is increasingly shaped by artificial intelligence, and strikes are increasingly carried out by autonomous systems. Many have argued that these technological advances will improve targeting and better protect civilians. To date, however, it is unclear whether autonomous systems have delivered on this promise. During the war in Gaza, for example, Israeli military personnel have been able to rapidly approve targets generated by an artificial intelligence system called Lavender. But such systems reflect the rules of engagement set by decision-makers, and after Hamas’s October 7 attack on Israel in 2023, the Israeli military raised the level of civilian casualties it would accept when striking Hamas militants. Perhaps for that reason, researchers who conducted an experiment testing the decision-making of around 2,000 Israeli military personnel found that in hypothetical scenarios involving high levels of collateral damage, they demonstrated “algorithmic aversion” and a desire to rely more on human decision-making.

Ironically, civilians may become more vulnerable, not less, as new technologies make it harder to attack military personnel and targets, increasing the risk of collateral damage and even providing some parties with an incentive to deliberately attack civilians and soft targets. Many militaries, including the American one, increasingly employ technology to minimize the risks borne by their own forces. This desire for immaculate war is not new. Nearly 700,000 U.S. service members participated in the Gulf War in 1990–91, and contrary to expectations of a bloodbath, only 143 of them died, an outcome that bolstered arguments that technology had made it possible to carry out major military operations with minimal risk to American troops. Two years later, the Battle of Mogadishu undercut that view, as the U.S. military withdrew from Somalia after 18 service members died in a protracted fight with Somali militants armed with cheap and relatively low-tech weapons such as rifles, machine guns, and rocket-propelled grenades. Early in the present century, the rapid American overthrow of the Taliban in Afghanistan rekindled the dream of relatively bloodless warfare made possible by innovations. But the grinding fights that followed there and in Iraq once again poured cold water on the idea.

Technological hopes spring eternal, however, and the U.S. military once again seems to be planning operations that use high-tech tools to reduce risks to its personnel. In an operation carried out in Venezuela this past January, that meant employing special operations forces complemented by space-based and cyber platforms and more than 150 aircraft and drones—all to capture a single person, Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro. In Iran, it has meant waging a war to transform conditions on the ground, but with attacks carried out primarily from the air and by sea.

To be sure, this approach is linked to the fact that the United States is not engaged in existential wars. And when the U.S. military has fought an adversary that perceives the conflict as existential, as in the Iranian case, American officials are often surprised that their adversaries refuse to back down and are willing to absorb staggering losses. Even if technology allows for fewer American casualties, that does not mean it should short-circuit robust political debate about whether to go to war. Casualties, after all, are hardly the only cost that contemporary warfare can impose. Even wars that produce relatively low casualties can destroy economies, bring down governments, and rip apart societies.

Countries that haven’t waged war in many decades, such as China, may be even more susceptible than the United States to the technology trap. Such countries might focus on acquiring innovative tools rather than preparing for adversaries that know how to integrate new technologies at scale. This may present some opportunities for Washington given its substantial recent experience in formulating concepts of operation tied to technological advancements. But countries such as China might be no less confident about their capabilities, even though they are significantly less experienced, which means the risks of miscalculation and escalation could easily increase.

Deep faith in new technologies could result in much smaller militaries, as machines replace human personnel. In the United States, this shift could widen the gap that already exists between ordinary Americans and those who serve, as even fewer Americans will have connections to—and therefore care about—how, when, where, and why Washington resorts to military force. This detachment of the military from the society it serves is corrosive to the institution itself and to democracy more broadly since it undermines the principle that war-making should reflect the will of the people. Countries that rely on conscription won’t face quite the same problem, but technological determinism will affect their relationships with their publics, as well: even if such countries need fewer citizens to serve, the technology trap could still reduce social cohesion by further marginalizing the sectors of society from which conscripts tend to be drawn.

Perhaps the most pernicious effect of the technology trap might be the way it could hollow out deterrence, as policymakers are tempted to adopt a myopic approach to the use of force shaped by deterministic views of technology’s impact on the battlefield. Deterrence requires an adversary to cogently read signals regarding capability and will. If both parties in a conflict have confidence in their own capabilities and in those of the other side, they may face increasing pressure to outdo each other in demonstrating a greater will to fight. Such a dynamic could result in a dangerous escalation spiral, particularly if it is paired with assumptions that technology will minimize the role and responsibilities of humans.

It is possible to escape the fog of tech. The most important thing for strategists and officials to grasp is that even though the means of warfighting will keep changing, the fundamental nature of war will not. There is not, and will never be, a shortcut—technological or otherwise—to answering the hard questions one must confront before sending troops to fight, kill, and die.

MARA KARLIN is Professor of Practice at the School of Advanced International Studies at Johns Hopkins University and a Visiting Fellow at the Brookings Institution. She served as U.S. Assistant Secretary of Defense for Strategy, Plans, and Capabilities during the Biden administration and in policy roles at the Pentagon during the Obama and George W. Bush administrations.

#5659. Tesla wants to sell you an unusable car with no steering wheel this year

Tesla said today it will sell Cybercab to consumers by the end of this year, raising questions of what end users would do with a vehicle with no steering wheel and no self-driving software.

During Tesla’s Q3 conference call today, a question was asked about when Tesla will deliver unsupervised FSD to customer cars.

That question was answered slightly differently than usual. Instead of the standard “end of next year” that we’ve heard every year for a decade, Tesla changed the answer to: “middle of next year.” In other words, Q2 2027.

So, alright, that’s when Tesla says its cars will be able to drive themselves with nobody paying any attention. Great. It would be quite a big step from their current level 2 system to a level 4 or 5 system, which are the only categories that could be deemed as “unsupervised” as level 3 still includes a driver in the driver’s seat who needs to be ready to take control at any time.

But then, in the same question, Tesla made another prediction. VP of Engineering Lars Moravy said that Cybercab will be in customer hands by the end of this year – which means within the next two months. Here’s the answer:

In short we’re trying to get Cybercabs to customers by the end of this year in 2026, obviously we’re producing a lot in Texas but all of those are going to our own internal Robotaxi fleet, and as Tesla grows we’ll mainly deliver fully designed autonomous vehicles to our customers and Cybercab will be the first. And so as we start small this year and we learn about all the things Ashok mentioned about delivery and ownership and fleet management and autonomous vehicles in customer hands, we’ll continue to scale that.

Musk then added on to the answer, highlighting that Cybercabs would mostly be Tesla-owned transport-as-a-service vehicles, which somewhat contradicts Tesla’s call for Cybercab fleet buyers which it put out the same day as its Cybercab launch event.

But did you notice anything? Moravy said that Cybercabs would be in customer hands – that is, not Tesla’s hands – by the end of 2026. And Ashok, in his answer which we reported on here, said unsupervised FSD would be in customer hands in Q2 2027 (if that’s to be believed).

This would leave a half-year where these Cybercabs, which are intended to come with no steering wheel or pedals, are completely inoperable. You couldn’t even push them into your driveway, because you’d have no steering wheel to help you do so.

Unless, of course, these Cybercabs get delivered with steering wheels – which most of the ones in the wild have these days anyway. And if they did, then why not just call it a Model 2 and sell it to people for $25k, like you should have done in the first place?

Either that, or the vehicles will be limited to the same areas that Cybercabs are already limited to. Despite Musk predicting that Robotaxis would cover half the US population by the end of last year, it is currently available only in small portions of 6 cities in 2 states.

The entire fleet of autonomous cabs in Austin, the most built out market by far, is estimated at 300 cars, including Model Y units. So where are the thousands of Cybercabs that are pouring off the line? And isn't that pretty poor planning, to have an assembly line dedicated to production that runs in the tens per week demand?

If Tesla gives customers access to the same software it uses (which it has promised for a long time), then presumably they could operate Cybercabs in the same areas. But Tesla said, in the same call, that it won’t give people access to that software, even in the areas where it’s already operating, until Q2 2027.

So, per Tesla’s statements today, it plans six months of selling a car that is usable by precisely nobody, to do precisely nothing.

Either that, or all the timelines are made up. But Tesla would never do that, would they?

Far-left streamer Hasan Piker dodged his controversial past in an exchange with Fox News Digital on Tuesday, hours after teaming up with embattled Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., on the campaign trail to co-host an event in her home state of Minnesota.

He also dismissed his disagreements with Democratic Party leaders after he called Republicans "terrorists" and Omar said the GOP is the "pedophile protection party" during the stream on Monday.

Omar's decision to embrace Piker on the campaign trail marks a potentially risky political alliance, coming on the back of a high-profile raid on her Washington, D.C., home amid two dueling investigations — one for alleged immigration fraud, which could threaten her political future and citizenship status, and the other for fishy financial disclosure statement discrepancies.

The lawmaker's appearance with Piker comes after Michigan Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed drew backlash from his own party for campaigning with the controversial Twitch streamer earlier this cycle.

Piker refused to comment on one issue though — his past comments claiming America "deserved 9/11."

"You made some comments on 9/11, do you still stand by them?" Fox News Digital asked Piker, at which point he ignored the question and walked through the line for security.

#5657. AI Is a Choice

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

The Narrow but Feasible Path to Collective Good

Alex Imas, Mohamed A. El-Erian, and James Manyika

Few technologies in history have matched the potential of artificial intelligence to transform the economic landscape in which they emerged. Deployed broadly and effectively, AI can make workers more efficient, enable more dynamic entrepreneurship, and lift economic growth, all of which could be necessary bulwarks against the powerful economic headwinds brought on by aging populations and mounting public debt. AI can also make possible revolutionary new advances in science and medicine and democratize access to knowledge once reserved for the select few.

But the acceleration of AI also carries risks. It could be applied in ways that displace workers, disrupt how students learn, create new bioweapons and cyberweapons, and strain current supplies of energy, water, and land. Compounding these challenges is a mismatch: the benefits of this new technology may take a long time to become visible and widespread. As with earlier transformations, such as the Industrial Revolution, people are likely to experience the disruptions that come from adopting AI before they feel its benefits. And whereas users directly feel the effects of tools like chatbots, some benefits that AI is indirectly making possible, including more accurate predictions of natural disasters and improved medical diagnoses, may not be perceived as owing to the technology at all.

Public opinion has already begun to turn against AI, especially in the United States. One of the main targets has been data centers, with polls showing that a significant majority of Americans oppose the construction of data centers in their communities. Opposition to data centers needed for the new AI economy now cuts across party lines and age groups. Numerous surveys have found that residents worry about data centers using too many resources, creating endless noise, and raising their utility bills, all while developers benefit from public subsidies at the communities’ expense.

Although some of these problems are local, they also reflect wider concerns about AI. Some leading figures in AI have no doubt contributed to this anxiety with dramatic warnings of a white-collar-job apocalypse and even the possibility that AI will lead to human extinction. These are real issues that must be understood and addressed, but the growing public pushback against AI also suggests a deeper distributional challenge: individuals, communities, and businesses are being asked to help shepherd a transformative and potentially unsettling new technology at a time when many citizens see its financial gains going to a few people whom they increasingly distrust. AI is now a major issue in American politics: candidates running in competitive elections, no matter which party they belong to, appear increasingly averse to being associated with support for AI.

These anxieties are understandable. AI adoption risks repeating the mistakes of globalization, which generated large and widely spread economic benefits but led to concentrated losses of jobs, growth, and industry in particular communities. Government support in response was insufficient or arrived too late. Although the economy gained overall from free trade, the political bargain failed to give affected workers and communities a credible stake in the gains, even as individuals benefited as consumers from lower prices. The result was a backlash that contributed to the protectionism and fragmentation that continue to plague today’s politics and society.

Turning AI’s capabilities into broadly shared and enduring prosperity requires more than investing in the technology itself. The central challenge of the AI era is coordination and cooperation across groups responsible for the many complementary investments that promote the adoption of AI, including technological diffusion, worker empowerment, firm reorganization, and infrastructure construction. Because these complementary investments must be made across different institutions and at different times, progress requires market mechanisms to encourage cooperation as well as coalitions among organizations, firms, workers, communities, and governments that can turn AI’s technological gains into widespread economic and social benefits. To borrow a concept from game theory, AI is a “cooperative game” in which the largest gains arise only when many groups coordinate, and those gains persist only when every group expects to do better as part of shared coalitions than outside them.

But coalition building requires trust, and public trust—particularly regarding technology and who benefits from it—has eroded. Grand promises of scientific and medical breakthroughs will not overcome deep-seated hostility. Durable cooperation requires giving affected groups a concrete stake in the opportunities and gains that AI generates rather than merely promising future benefits. Without that stake, the individuals and groups whose support is needed to make AI flourish could block its progress. Governments could impose stifling regulations on the industry or stop issuing data center permits, firms could decline to adopt new AI-driven systems, and workers could avoid sharing the tacit knowledge needed to make the technology useful. As the economist and Nobel Prize laureate Michael Spence once observed: “You cannot solve a cooperative game uncooperatively.” The way to ensure that society benefits from AI’s enormous potential while mitigating its risks is to make cooperation the clear choice.

AI is staking a claim to being the next general-purpose technology—one that applies across a wide array of activities, improves over time, and generates complementary innovations. Electricity was an early general-purpose technology. When inventors discovered how to commercially harness electricity in the late nineteenth century, the ability to use it to power many types of machines transformed industrial production. Computing, another general-purpose technology, used electricity to process information itself, revolutionizing sectors as varied as engineering and health care.

But AI works through a more powerful input—intelligence—which makes it possible to interpret information, predict outcomes, diagnose problems, communicate with others, and make decisions. As a result, AI can raise productivity in existing sectors: a nurse can spend less time on paperwork and devote more time to patients, engineers can more quickly develop new designs, and small firms can scale up their analytics capabilities to compete with larger competitors. It can also increase efficiency so significantly that it makes possible wholly new products and services that were otherwise too costly or complicated to deliver.

AI also goes beyond existing general-purpose technologies by transforming the process of research and discovery. By inventing new ways of optimizing scientific processes, AI opens up previously unknown possibilities, similar to what microscopes and telescopes did in earlier eras. AlphaFold, a specialized AI model for which Demis Hassabis and John Jumper shared half the 2024 Nobel Prize in Chemistry, is a clear example. Determining a single protein’s three-dimensional structure from its amino acid sequence used to take months or even years; by using machine learning, AlphaFold has predicted the structures of more than 200 million proteins, fundamentally reshaping what biologists are able to investigate.

The potential of AI is thus a combination of the Industrial Revolution, whose general-purpose technologies transformed production, and the Enlightenment, which brought about inventions for making inventions—the new instruments and new paradigms that transformed the process of discovery. AI holds the promise of providing both the productivity boost and the tools to pave the way for discoveries that can address many of the problems weighing down aging, debt-ridden societies across the world.

The latest wave of AI has diffused at unprecedented speed. Google’s Gemini app and OpenAI’s ChatGPT each now reach more than one billion monthly users. Google’s AI & Economy ATLAS, a study of how people are using the company’s AI products that two of us (Imas and Manyika) worked on, analyzed 15 million interactions in more than 150 countries. It found that AI was used in tasks in 68 percent of occupations, and these occupations made up roughly 90 percent of all U.S. employment. Even automotive technicians and industrial mechanics used AI to interpret test results and diagnose electrical faults. Another study that two of us (Imas and Manyika) ran in collaboration with MIT FutureTech found that nearly half the scientists who were surveyed used some type of AI model every day, saving them time and enabling more interdisciplinary research.

But the gains of diffusion are neither guaranteed nor automatic. AI has been adopted broadly across and within sectors, but thus far its penetration remains fairly shallow. The ATLAS study found that only 21 percent of tasks in a typical occupation have been substantially affected by the technology. According to the Anthropic Economic Index, which measures how the AI model Claude is used in workplace tasks as varied as writing software, analyzing data, and managing administrative workflows, potential use far exceeds actual use. This suggests that access has spread much faster than adoption. Incorporating AI deeply enough to allow it to live up to its potential requires a bigger transformation. A firm does not change how it operates simply by buying access to an AI coding agent. It must also invest in organizational reforms and worker training and often needs to redesign its governance structure or its product lines.

The banking sector, for example, has widely adopted AI but mostly used it to perform superficial tasks. As it is currently deployed, AI gives analysts a faster way to research companies, populate spreadsheets, and identify the sources of investment returns. Deep adoption, on the other hand, requires changing workflows and job responsibilities. A bank must decide what proprietary data to feed a model, how to protect client information, and how to verify the results of the AI models it uses. Junior employees may spend less time collecting information and more time interpreting it, forming ideas, and advising clients. But many tasks that AI can automate are also forms of training. Firms will therefore need to find ways for experienced employees to help younger colleagues acquire the skills the experienced employees once developed by producing the underlying analyses themselves.

The true economic promise of AI lies in deep and transformational adoption. This requires revamping organizational processes, preparing workers to capitalize on the new technology, and applying AI in ways that enhance productivity and innovation and improve consumer welfare. Innovations that increase output and can generate new products and services have not yet appeared at a meaningful scale. But there is enormous latent demand for many new products and services, including specialized services such as individualized tutoring, health care, and legal and financial assistance, and additional demand will emerge from completely new activities that technology will make possible. By fundamentally changing how work is done and what work can be done, AI can lower the cost of providing tailored products, making new opportunities economically viable. Serving demand that previously did not make economic sense adds new value to society rather than merely redistributing a fixed economic pie.

But enlarging the economic pie does not guarantee a smooth transition for every worker. As with globalization, the new demand that AI unlocks will not necessarily arise in the same jobs, sectors, or communities in which existing work is done. This potential mismatch risks major labor market disruption and political backlash. Public anxiety is already growing, especially among younger workers seeking entry-level positions that are the most likely to be automated by AI. One study by the Stanford economists Erik Brynjolfsson, Bharat Chandar, and Ruyu Chen found that the employment of 22-to-25-year-olds in occupations most exposed to AI automation was 19 percent lower than it would have been if employment had grown at the same rate as occupations less exposed to AI disruption. Yet the overall effect of AI on jobs, including entry-level positions, remains uncertain. The Economist has estimated that AI has created around one million jobs in the United States but led to about 200,000 layoffs, and the gap between the unemployment rate among 20-to-24-year-olds and the overall unemployment rate is low compared with recent decades. Most likely, as one of us (Manyika) wrote with Spence in these pages, in 2023, some jobs will be lost, some will be gained, and many more will be permanently changed.

Harnessing AI’s potential to increase prosperity without destabilizing society requires coordination. In addition to AI companies making new models, products, and tools, infrastructure developers must build the physical systems such as data centers that underpin AI. Businesses need to supply data and drive the organizational transformation to deploy models at their full potential—not just for efficiency but also to expand existing products and services or create new ones. Employers must decide how these technologies will be used, how workflows will be transformed, and how to enable workers to be more productive. Workers have to adopt the technology and invest in learning AI-relevant skills. Local communities must provide the land for data centers and consent to the technology’s expansion. And governments need to set rules, fund foundational research, and provide social insurance to support and manage transitions for those whose work or livelihoods are displaced by AI.

Each of these groups bears unique costs, captures different shares of the benefits, answers to its own constituencies, and responds to distinct incentives. This also means that unless they coordinate—whether through market mechanisms or active cooperation—these groups could slow or block AI development. Firms might underinvest in organizational change and use AI only to cut costs rather than to expand revenues, workers might resist or slow down adoption, communities might block infrastructure, and governments might restrict development. Each action might make sense in isolation, but together they lead to a suboptimal outcome for society.

This is a classic cooperative game scenario in game theory: these groups can accomplish much more when they work in concert than when they act individually and without coordination. A coalition with a common goal does not need to be a formal organization; it can be any grouping of actors who operate in ways that allow them to coordinate decisions and contribute resources to a shared project through contracts, policies, standards, investment commitments, or political bargains. Each possible coalition can generate more growth and economic value by promoting the adoption and diffusion of AI than any one actor can by operating separately.

In an ideal world, all these actors would form a single “grand coalition” to capture as much value from AI as possible. Their complementary actions and active coordination to promote AI would kick off a virtuous cycle in which wider adoption would spur advancements that would make AI more useful for society, unlock new demand and productivity growth, and encourage more people, communities, and companies to adopt the technology and support its expansion.

Such a grand coalition, however, is a benchmark rather than a blueprint. Progress is not all or nothing; smaller, more focused coalitions can still create economic and social gains that far outweigh what each actor could achieve alone. What is needed is a stable coalition—one in which no participant or group of participants can do better by leaving and doing something on their own. In the AI economy, exiting the coalition does not mean building a rival AI system. It simply means exercising a veto on increased AI use, which could take the form of imposing a local moratorium on data centers, resisting workplace adoption of AI, or blocking new regulations that encourage AI development. Because the withdrawal of any one participant would destroy more value than that participant could ever capture alone, it is critical that every essential group prefers being inside the coalition to being outside it.

A crucial question, then, is how to share the gains of working together so that cooperation remains stable over time and no coalition member wants to leave. In the case of AI, one factor that can help drive cooperation is that the technology creates conditions for what economists call a “convex game”: because the inputs to AI are complementary, each new participant makes everyone else’s contribution more valuable, causing the gains from joining the coalition to increase at an accelerating rate. Building better computing infrastructure, for instance, makes AI models and tools more capable; more capable models and tools increase the benefits for companies that want to invest in transforming their organizational processes and retraining workers, which increases productivity and spurs innovation; and wider adoption of AI in the workplace drives the need for additional computing power, which creates incentives for more infrastructure providers to build. Once a core group starts working together, the next actor that wants to join has even more to gain.

These characteristics of the AI economy also make it easier to share the gains of growth more fairly. A simple approach is to distribute the benefits based on how much each member contributes to a coalition. Under the framework of the Nobel Prize–winning economist Lloyd Shapley, each participant’s contribution can be measured by the average additional value that possible coalitions create when that participant is included. If a coalition wants to use AI to discover new treatments for chronic or rare diseases, for instance, it needs to include drug developers, AI labs, research hospitals, frontline physicians, patient groups, and the government. AI labs supply specialized models and computing power, research hospitals curate historical clinical data, physicians administer drug trials, and patient groups consent to share biological records (under privacy guardrails set by the government). These contributions are not measured in the same units, and although smaller coalitions can make partial progress, all these groups are needed for the drug discovery pipeline to fully succeed. A Shapley-style arrangement would share the gains based on how much different coalitions benefit from having each member inside them compared with what would result if the member were outside them. As Shapley’s research showed, in a convex game such as AI adoption, compensating each partner in this way produces a stable coalition that incentivizes everyone to join.

Prices, contracts, and incentives are indispensable for building a stable coalition, and market design and competition provide the architecture that enables coordination. But markets alone will not be enough. In the case of AI, the various costs and benefits will not unfold on the same timelines for all participants; some of them will benefit long before others catch up. Public institutions are necessary. Only policymakers, for instance, can create the common rules that will allow frontier labs in the coalition to coordinate on making AI safer, and only policymakers can provide enough support for workers to adapt to the AI economy. The bitter experiences that many workers and communities felt during globalization have made coalition building harder yet even more necessary.

A workable coalition does not need to bring every participant to the same negotiating table. A national bargain can establish common terms and align incentives on issues such as AI safety standards, workforce transition support, and infrastructure permitting, and smaller agreements at workplaces and in local communities can bring needed groups on board to cooperate. At both levels, the principle is the same: each group must receive a credible stake in the upside of the economic gains that the coalition creates through the overall growth of AI.

At the national level, cooperation should focus on the groups whose participation will determine whether AI technology can scale enough to generate massive economic gains. This includes the federal government, leading labs, major infrastructure builders and providers, organized labor, and large firms deploying AI. Big business matters because its use of technology and its investments in the workforce create demand that fuels the entire supply chain. Labor organizations will want to negotiate favorable terms for workforce transition, such as expanded unemployment and wage insurance, as well as for retraining; a lack of participation from organized labor will deprive the coalition of the support needed to facilitate adoption, especially in sectors such as construction in which unions have more power. For critical infrastructure, including power grids, telecommunications networks, and payment systems, the priority needs to be cybersecurity and reliability: before they integrate AI deeply into core operations, infrastructure providers need to believe that more capable models will not expose them to catastrophic risk.

The government can establish a predictable, risk-based regulatory framework to set clear standards in potentially dangerous areas such as biosecurity and to help coordinate the testing of advanced AI systems. A national framework would preempt a fragmented patchwork of idiosyncratic state-by-state bans on low-risk commercial uses of AI. Policymakers can also support AI development by funding research on foundational questions in artificial intelligence and increasing immigration and visa pathways for highly skilled workers to attract top global talent to U.S. labs. AI model and application developers, meanwhile, can participate in safety testing, and companies building out AI capacity can make available more computing power to small businesses, nonprofits, and local governments. Infrastructure providers can construct new data centers and show their support for the communities they work in by implementing ratepayer protections and paying local taxes, and large companies can invest in worker training, organizational transformation, and new business models. Finally, organized labor can supply the skilled employees needed to build the physical infrastructure underlying AI adoption, help redesign organizational workflows within companies, and lend its political weight to promoting AI nationally.

Each group benefits more than it is expected to contribute. AI model and application developers and infrastructure providers gain a predictable development path and a skilled workforce. Large firms have access to the support they need to more deeply adopt AI and improve their productivity. Workers gain new opportunities and job security during the economic transition. Washington also stands to benefit as the economy grows and the United States maintains its global technological leadership.

AI model developers and other companies in the AI ecosystem are already working together to diffuse AI more widely. Leading developers, including Anthropic, Google, and OpenAI, along with partner organizations, have embedded engineers inside large organizations to identify valuable uses of AI, redesign workflows, and integrate new technology systems into production. These efforts recognize that model access is not enough: adoption requires technical assistance, organizational knowledge, and sustained engagement. A coalition can extend this capacity to smaller firms, schools, clinics, and municipalities. For AI model and application developers, supporting diffusion expands the market and provides insurance against potential political backlash. Only if the gains of AI adoption are shared with a broad swath of organizations and the general public will citizens support the expansion of the technology in society.

Frameworks for coalition building with labor groups are also emerging. Rather than working with individual firms to sign bespoke agreements, technology developers, industry associations, and policymakers are supporting worker cooperation through existing unions, apprenticeships, and retraining programs. The International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers, for example, is mobilizing its members to oppose data center moratoriums while calling for better labor agreements. Google is working with the IBEW’s partner organizations to train 100,000 electrical workers and 30,000 apprentices, giving workers access to well-paid jobs and providing developers with a source of skilled labor and the political support needed to build.

At the local level, new data centers may impose concentrated costs on communities even when they create national benefits for AI users. Developers should therefore negotiate agreements that protect ratepayers and provide tangible benefits for communities through increased local revenue, better infrastructure, and more employment opportunities. Templates for how to do this are taking shape in many U.S. states. Leading AI companies and “hyperscalers,” including Amazon, Google, and Microsoft, have signed on to the Ratepayer Protection Pledge, a voluntary commitment to pay the full cost of new power generation and grid upgrades in areas where they are building data centers so that household electricity bills do not rise. These companies are also funding local workforce programs and water-restoration projects and contributing property tax revenue that will allow communities to pay for new schools and roads.

The economic benefits that AI brings will increase the stability of emerging coalitions as long as they are shared. The distribution of benefits does not need to be equal, but for groups providing complementary inputs, benefits must be apportioned according to what each group contributes and also be adjusted as the technology evolves. In practice, determining each group’s contribution is not an exact science but an ongoing structured negotiation: tools such as utility tariffs, labor agreements, and enabling policies can allocate benefits in ways that reflect the role of different groups in supporting AI expansion and adoption. The share of the upside that each coalition member receives must be large enough to make participation more attractive than obstruction. Giving key players a real stake in the economic surplus that is generated is how those who might have been potential impediments to AI become participants in, and perhaps even advocates for, the expanded use of this new technology.

Where AI substitutes for existing tasks rather than complementing them, policymakers can also learn from what went wrong with globalization. Public programs to help displaced workers can be effective, but they must be accessible enough to provide support when technological disruption occurs. Targeted parts of the federal government’s Trade Adjustment Assistance program to compensate and retrain workers affected by globalization succeeded where they were put in place—research by the Federal Reserve Bank of New York found that eligibility for wage insurance under the program increased reemployment, shortened the amount of time out of work, and raised cumulative earnings—but it reached only a small portion of workers. Other research found that most support was provided through disability benefits that required workers to permanently exit the labor force. With AI, policymakers will need to set up expanded wage and unemployment insurance programs and support employer-led retraining programs that help workers transition into in-demand roles before displacement occurs rather than improvise after workers begin to suffer.

A coalition does not need to be built all at once. Cooperation should start in areas in which complementary benefits are clearest and participation constraints are easiest to identify, including promoting AI adoption and worker training in particular sectors such as health care or skilled trades, and encouraging support for new data centers. At the national level, safety cooperation can grow out of partnerships between coalitions and the government. Because AI’s development trajectory remains uncertain, cooperation agreements should center on investments that could pay out across multiple plausible scenarios and can be revised as the trajectory of AI becomes clearer.

International cooperation can grow in the same way. Although AI may well eventually require cooperation among adversaries and allies alike, Washington can start by forming a U.S.-led coalition built around trusted partners willing to accept common safety and security standards. The United States could, for example, set up a coalition among trusted partners that agrees on principles and frameworks in exchange for access to technology and cooperation. This coalition will naturally widen as its terms prove workable and more countries see the benefits of participating. It could eventually include China, which, given its advanced AI capabilities, is critical to addressing AI safety issues; this will require navigating complex questions of alignment and verification, however, including how to set up trusted validation bodies and mechanisms.

The Basel Committee on Banking Supervision provides an instructive example of international cooperation. It began in 1974 as a small forum among national regulators in 12 advanced industrialized countries and over time developed common standards for how to supervise banks around the world without empowering an imposing supranational authority, expanding its scope as those standards proved valuable. The Basel Committee was the seed for what is now the dominant international architecture that identifies and minimizes the risks of financial instability, including setting standards for how much capital banks need to hold and how to classify the riskiness of securities held on banks’ balance sheets. A future coalition for AI standards could grow through the same combination of clear entry conditions and credible commitments that have made the Basel Committee and the International Organization for Standardization, which publishes voluntary technical standards, successful.

Harnessing the potential of AI will not be easy. Critics will immediately jump to the conclusion that there are many reasons why such coalitions will not work in practice. Labs might decide that they can earn more on their own, incumbent firms might try to convert cooperation into tools to protect their market share, and governments might promise support for workers and communities and fail to deliver, as was the case with globalization.

But there is reason to be cautiously optimistic about cooperation in the age of AI. Because the technology is still relatively early in its development, it is possible to identify the groups needed to initiate cooperation, and labs, governments, communities, employers, and labor organizations have already shown interest in different parts of the bargain. Ultimately, society does not need to choose between innovation and shared prosperity. The two can be complements: cooperation will drive growth, and sharing the gains of such growth will sustain the cooperation needed to keep it going.

ALEX IMAS is Director of AGI Economics at Google DeepMind and Roger L. and Rachel M. Goetz Professor of Behavioral Science, Economics, and Applied AI at the University of Chicago’s Booth School of Business.

MOHAMED A. EL-ERIAN is Rene M. Kern Professor of Practice at the Wharton School of the University of Pennsylvania, Chief Economic Adviser at Allianz, and Chair of the Board of Directors of the Center for Global Development.

JAMES MANYIKA is Senior Vice President at Google Alphabet and its President of Research, Labs, Technology, and Society. He is a Distinguished Fellow at Stanford University’s Human-Centered Artificial Intelligence Institute and a Visiting Professor at the University of Oxford’s Blavatnik School of Government.

#5655. Serbia’s Student Protesters Deserve a Nobel Prize

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Serbia’s Student Protesters Deserve a Nobel Prize

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

The stunning majority victory of the Student List in Serbia’s parliamentary elections on Oct. 25 was a vital reprieve for a beleaguered continent. As furious youth protests sweep across Europe, Gen Zers in Serbia have proved that youth can achieve transformative change through nonviolent political engagement. The extraordinary discipline, diligence, and organization exhibited by Serbian students over two pressure-filled years are worthy of a Nobel Peace Prize.

The improbable bid to dislodge the corrupt regime of former President Aleksandar Vucic, which held all instruments of power, also offers a seminal opportunity for the United States and European Union, in and beyond the region. Serbia’s neighbors have often voiced skepticism of the student-led movement, which includes hard-line Serbian nationalists along with pragmatists who grasped the need to blunt Vucic’s charge that the students and their supporters are “anti-Serb.” But Serbian students have created an inspiring, viable model for channeling mass discontent within the political process—provided the new government succeeds.

The stunning majority victory of the Student List in Serbia’s parliamentary elections on Oct. 25 was a vital reprieve for a beleaguered continent. As furious youth protests sweep across Europe, Gen Zers in Serbia have proved that youth can achieve transformative change through nonviolent political engagement. The extraordinary discipline, diligence, and organization exhibited by Serbian students over two pressure-filled years are worthy of a Nobel Peace Prize.

The improbable bid to dislodge the corrupt regime of former President Aleksandar Vucic, which held all instruments of power, also offers a seminal opportunity for the United States and European Union, in and beyond the region. Serbia’s neighbors have often voiced skepticism of the student-led movement, which includes hard-line Serbian nationalists along with pragmatists who grasped the need to blunt Vucic’s charge that the students and their supporters are “anti-Serb.” But Serbian students have created an inspiring, viable model for channeling mass discontent within the political process—provided the new government succeeds.

Fully capitalizing on the dramatic student-led triumph in Serbia means first understanding its ingredients. The sharp contrast between Serbia’s constructive students—operating in a hostile environment—and their attention-hogging counterparts in liberal France is teeming with lessons.

First among them is the commitment to nonviolence. While student unrest featured from the outset of the French school protests, it was the opposite—violence inflicted on peaceful student protesters in Belgrade—that sparked the nationwide student movement in Serbia. A key factor in attracting nationwide support, as opposed to fleeting press attention and opportunistic rallies, was Gandhian student restraint, even as the regime escalated provocations and attacks, notably after a third mass protest in late June 2025.

Unlike the protests that brought down former President Slobodan Milosevic, demonstrators occupied no government buildings, only university faculties. In Novi Sad, the site of the train station tragedy that cost 16 people their lives and ignited nationwide outrage, university students proudly were at pains to show the care they exercised over the facilities. Determination to achieve change—not vitriol—propelled the collaboration and collective decision-making that were central to the success of the movement.

Second, Serbian youth have focused on the essential needs of society, over parochial demands on the cost and conditions for education. Sacrifice—demonstrated in blistering, long-distance marches, marathon bike rides, and tedious meetings—instead of instant gratification is the hallmark of this successful movement. While French youth storm the Bastille, Serbian students have fanned out across the country, creating citizen groups in large towns and small villages, rekindling the belief of ordinary people in participatory democracy.

Third, the movement did not dogmatically reject engagement in the electoral process, as many student activists do across the globe. Anti-corruption protesters in Bosnia and Herzegovina a decade ago, for example, fell for this mistake.

Initially, the Serbian students refrained from any political stance, demanding only that the government discharge its legal and moral duties to the victims of the Novi Sad tragedy. After the resignation of Prime Minister Milos Vucevic in January 2025, the students called for a transitional government to prepare for new elections. It was government intransigence in the wake of the massive rally that March—the largest gathering in Belgrade since the end of World War II—that convinced the students to change course. A clear majority of students understood and accepted their unique role as the only untainted political actor with the standing to oust the regime at the polls.

Unlike many of their youth counterparts in the United States, Serbian students recognized their lack of life experience as a handicap in running for office or running a government office. Instead, they turned to the public, allocating a proportional number of candidate positions to each university faculty across the country. Applicants endured strict vetting, thorough interviews, and several rounds of selection. Only those enjoying a solid reputation in their respective fields cleared the bar.

One absolute criterion dominated the selection process: Nominees could have no political affiliation or background whatsoever. Astutely, the students grasped that the so-called professional Serbian opposition, benefiting from state funding, operating through patronage, and widely seen as ineffectual, would have no credibility with voters. Indeed, the few opposition parties that contested the elections—including the longtime leading opposition party, the Party of Freedom and Justice, headed by former Belgrade Mayor Dragan Djilas—have, as of this writing, failed to clear the threshold. Marking the new political era, even the governing party of former Prime Minister Ivica Dacic—a fixture in Serbian politics and the leader of the Socialist Party of Serbia, founded by Milosevic—also will likely fail to enter parliament.

Now is the moment for the United States and EU to advance political, economic, and people-to-people contact between Serbia and its former Yugoslavia neighbors, restoring trust lost to years of Serbian revisionism under Vucic. Indulged by a pliant EU and both the Biden and Trump administrations, Vucic’s Serbia became the most potent platform for Russian and Chinese influence in all of Europe and the primary source of regional discord. As much as it detests Europe, the Trump administration now needs to actively support the Brussels-led dialogue between Serbia and Kosovo.

And with the Serbian presidential election looming in December, Brussels must move with rare alacrity to support and sustain a successful transition from the entrenched, would-be autocracy of Vucic. Strong economic performance under Vucic led his Serbian Progressive Party to retain more than 100 seats in the Serbian parliament, a far greater seat percentage (some 41 percent) than former Prime Minister Viktor Orban’s Fidesz party now holds in neighboring Hungary (albeit with a similar share of the electorate). Unknown is how many loyalists Vucic will still control in government, particularly in the security services. This makes rationalizing—under clear, accountable authority—the disparate internal security agencies that served the regime an urgent priority.

For its part, the Trump administration should quickly pin the long-delayed ouster of the Russian owners of Serbia’s national oil company on the caretaker government led by Vucic’s party, before it leaves office. When it returns, the Senate should swiftly confirm Michael Young, a rare qualified administration nominee, as ambassador to Serbia, with the admonition that rule of law—not crony capitalism—is the foundation for increased, expedited U.S. investment.

They can take inspiration from Serbia’s students. Although they were widely characterized as “leaderless,” the hundreds (not thousands) of core student activists who engineered this historic election victory actually embodied the essential qualities of leadership: sacrifice; persistence; devotion to the cause, not to personal interest; courage; collaboration; effective communication; sophistication; and, above all, integrity.

Edward P. Joseph teaches conflict management at Johns Hopkins School of Advanced International Studies. He served for a dozen years in the Balkans, including with the U.S. Army, and as Deputy Head of the OSCE Mission in Kosovo.

#5654. Rio Tinto eyes a giant hot-brick battery to cut fossil fuel use

Rio Tinto wants to put Australia’s cheap midday electricity to work at its Yarwun alumina refinery – by storing it in hot bricks and using that heat battery to make steam after the sun sets.

The global mining giant and California-based Rondo Energy have started engineering and design work on a huge heat battery near Gladstone, Queensland. Rondo calls it Australia’s largest planned thermal energy storage project and says it would be a first for the alumina industry.

Alumina is a hard white powder made of aluminum and oxygen that’s used to make aluminum metal, sandpaper, and strong ceramics. Globally, aluminum sector refining accounts for roughly 175 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent annually.

It’s still at the study stage, but the potential payoff is substantial. Rio Tinto estimates the battery could supply around 5% of Yarwun’s steam needs and cut the refinery’s direct emissions by about 67,000 metric tons of carbon dioxide annually. It would replace some of the coal and natural gas used to generate steam.

Rondo’s heat battery uses electricity to heat refractory bricks, which can withstand high temperatures. The bricks hold that energy as heat, which is then used to produce steam for the refinery’s existing steam network.

That matters because industrial heat demand doesn’t stop when solar production drops. Rondo’s heat battery charges during hours when electricity is cheap and plentiful, then delivers heat continuously so factories can keep running.

The proposed Yarwun system would have 360 megawatt-hours (MWh) of thermal storage and deliver up to 30 megawatts MW of heat. The Australian Renewable Energy Agency (ARENA) lists its electrical charging capacity at 78 MW, allowing it to take in electricity faster than it releases the stored energy as heat.

For the refinery, another attraction is that the battery would feed into equipment it already uses. The study will assess whether that integration can deliver reliable high-pressure steam at a cost that makes sense.

“This project shows how Australia’s abundant energy can become a competitive advantage for industry,” said Dr. Kira Rundel, Rondo’s head of Australia. “Heat batteries allow manufacturers to use low-cost electricity to produce the reliable, round-the-clock heat their operations need.”

ARENA is providing an initial A$3.6 million for the engineering and design study. If the project moves ahead, the agency will provide up to A$38 million for deployment. There’s also a further A$33.3 million in contingency support.

Rio Tinto will match ARENA’s A$3.6 million study contribution. The company says its total investment over the project’s life is expected to exceed ARENA’s funding if deployment goes ahead.

“Reducing emissions from alumina refining is complex, particularly when it comes to generating the high-pressure steam needed for our operations,” said Rio Tinto Pacific Operations managing director Armando Torres.

“This study will help us determine whether thermal energy storage can provide a technically and economically viable alternative to fossil fuels for part of Yarwun’s steam requirements.”

The feasibility study is expected to take about 12 months.

Read more: Germany is using heated bricks to replace gas-fired industrial boilers
