#5641. The Global Push to Keep Kids Off Social Media

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: The Global Push to Keep Kids Off Social Media

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

The European Union issued a bold declaration last month targeting one of the world’s wealthiest, most influential industries. “No social media under the age of 13. No personal account under the age of 15,” European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said during her state of the EU address.

The plan puts the 27-nation bloc on track to join a growing number of countries enacting far-reaching bans, hourly restrictions, or other content moderation controls for young people on online platforms. More than 60 countries have imposed or are considering restrictions on minors’ social media access, according to Tech Policy Press, a nonprofit that tracks age restrictions.

The European Union issued a bold declaration last month targeting one of the world’s wealthiest, most influential industries. “No social media under the age of 13. No personal account under the age of 15,” European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said during her state of the EU address.

The plan puts the 27-nation bloc on track to join a growing number of countries enacting far-reaching bans, hourly restrictions, or other content moderation controls for young people on online platforms. More than 60 countries have imposed or are considering restrictions on minors’ social media access, according to Tech Policy Press, a nonprofit that tracks age restrictions.

Policymakers have accused tech companies of using addictive design features, such as endless scroll and autoplay, to lure and keep minors online, while parents worry about megacorporations collecting their children’s data.

“It’s the equivalent of putting our kids in cars with no seat belts, with no airbags, and having them drive on roads with no speed limits and no traffic lights,” former U.S. Surgeon General Vivek Murthy said last year in a warning to Congress about the impact of social media on children’s health.

In December 2025, Australia became the first to implement such legislation, barring children under 16 from creating social media accounts. As of October, 82 percent of Australian parents support such restrictions. This decision started a “domino effect,” said Tommaso Giardini, the associate director of the Digital Policy Alert, a nonprofit focused on online transparency.

Governments from Brazil to Malaysia to the United Arab Emirates have since adopted policies that curb minors’ access to online platforms, with Facebook, Instagram, TikTok, Twitch, X, and YouTube among the most common targets. “Selling the idea of protecting children is very easy,” Giardini said.

Harder is deciding which strategy to pursue. Countries have generally followed three main policy approaches: the Australia model, the China model, and the India model.

Young people look at an iPad in Sydney on Dec. 7, 2025.George Chan/Getty Images

Australia’s model of social media restriction may be the most straightforward—and the most severe. Users must be age 16 or older to create a social media account, period. Parents cannot consent on behalf of their children.

“One of the virtues of age-based age-gating is that everyone knows the law,” said Terry Flew, a professor of digital communication and culture at the University of Sydney, who likened the policy to similar age-based bans on alcohol, smoking, and vaping.

Three months after Australia’s ban took effect, Indonesia implemented a similar under-16 ban, though the Southeast Asian country included one major caveat: Children under age 13 will only have access to age-approved platforms, but those between ages 13 and 15 can go on “low risk” sites with parental consent.

This model has also proved popular across Europe, where Austria, France, Germany, Greece, Spain, Turkey, and others have passed or are discussing age-based systems, each with their own conditions.

“It is more interventionist, but it feels less interventionist,” Giardini said, adding that the approach “will probably cause less skepticism than the government or some government agency going into the details of which types of children can access which types of platforms for how many minutes.”

Yet age-based restrictions have also come under criticism for being ineffective. According to an Australian government report published in March, around 7 out of 10 parents whose children were on social media prior to the ban said that they still had their accounts despite the order for platforms to remove minors’ profiles. Other countries with age-based systems, such as the United Kingdom, have recorded a surge in virtual private network usage as minors attempt to circumvent the prohibitions.

Several governments have responded by increasing their penalties on social media platforms. In June, Australia announced that it would double fines on companies that fail to crack down on minors’ access, going from around $34 million to $68 million. However, experts suggest that the impact is limited. Facebook generated $200.97 billion in annual revenue last year; a fine of $68 million is just 0.03 percent of the company’s yearly earnings.

Meanwhile, children’s rights experts warn that young people, particularly queer youth, will become more isolated without these online resources. Adults fear that a new bout of data collection—when they must prove their identities to access these sites—could violate their own privacy.

Most of all, though, analysts say that simply restricting access does not address social media’s most concerning features. “By implementing this ban, the government is giving up on telling platforms how to regulate their algorithms, which is the essence of the problem,” said Francesco Bailo, the co-director of the University of Sydney’s Center for AI, Trust, and Governance. He warned that vulnerable people over age 16 remain exposed to the platforms’ addictive designs.

Estonia, the only EU member state that opposes such bans, has made a similar argument. “The way to approach this, to me, is not to make kids responsible for that harm [stemming from social media platforms] and start self-regulating,” Estonian Education Minister Kristina Kallas told Politico in April. The “responsibility is on the governments and on the corporation side.”

People play games at an internet cafe in Beijing on Jan. 26, 2024.Greg Baker/AFP via Getty Images

China—with its well-known Great Firewall—is no stranger to restricting online content. But in the realms of social media and gaming, Beijing has been a leader in protecting minors’ exposure. As early as 2002, China banned people under 18 from entering internet cafes, and increasingly tighter regulations have shaped how young people can access the digital world.

That approach reached new heights in April 2025, when China launched its systemwide “minor mode” initiative. Whereas Australia’s age-based policy restricts usage all the time, China’s model offers a multitiered approach with caveats based on age, day of the week, and time of day.

Children under 18 are barred from using nonessential internet applications between 10 p.m. and 6 a.m., and screen time during the day is limited from 40 minutes to two hours depending on one’s age. Devices are required to remind users to rest every 30 minutes, online content is carefully tailored to match one’s age level, and parents can monitor their children’s activity.

“China has built a holistic, systemic machinery for regulating minors’ internet use,” said Fengshu Liu, a professor at the University of Oslo who specializes in sociocultural issues among young people in China. Minor mode works at the device level, though, which gives parents ultimate control over whether to activate the system.

Content from rap videos to protest photos is being removed in the name of “national security.”

Trump has decimated the norms of diplomatic communication, and other countries have followed suit.

Why Washington’s foreign-policy community needs to take its conversation to a new platform.

“In practice, this means implementation varies significantly across households, heavily influenced by the digital literacy, educational level, and socioeconomic status of the parents,” Liu said.

The EU has considered a similar strategy. On July 13, the bloc issued a report recommending that children under 13 have “time-limited” access to social media—and only with parental supervision. In September, von der Leyen outlined a tiered system that includes “mini accounts” for teens aged 13 and 14 that would restrict screen time and minimize contact with strangers.

“It is not about our minors accessing social media,” von der Leyen said during her address. “It is about when and how do we allow social media to access minors.”

Unlike Australia, the EU forecasted in July that it also plans to consider imposing restrictions on other online content deemed harmful to children. Although von der Leyen did not specify exactly what might be targeted, experts suspect that video games and artificial intelligence chatbots could be next.

Chinese authorities have already started such a crackdown. In 2021, China mandated that those under 18 only be allowed to play video games from 8 p.m. to 9 p.m. on Fridays, Saturdays, Sundays, and legal holidays, and only for a total of three hours per week, according to Daniel Ahmad, the director of research and insights at Niko Partners, a video game research firm.

Individual apps use age-verification technology, including enhanced facial recognition run through a nationwide citizen database, to enforce these restrictions. Still, “loopholes exist, such as games distributed via Steam or PlayStation that are not officially distributed in China but can be accessed via a VPN or disc import,” Ahmad wrote in an email.

Lack of parental accountability has also contributed to poor efficacy. Around 29 percent of young gamers in China continued to play outside of the allotted time during the first year these restrictions were in place, a Niko Partners report found in 2022. Of this group, 82 percent did so by using an adult’s account.

Participants attend a gaming festival in Bengaluru, India, on Sept. 28, 2025.Idrees Mohammed/AFP via Getty Images

Despite their differences, Australia and China’s social media policies have their whole-nation approach in common. India, meanwhile, has allowed legislation to be drafted and implemented on a state-by-state basis.

In January, Goa became the first Indian state to weigh age restrictions similar to the Australian model. Two months later, Karnataka—home to India’s tech capital, Bengaluru—announced a social media ban for children under 16. And in April, the state of Andhra Pradesh declared its intention to draft a law limiting social media for those under 13.

The United States has taken a similar approach, even as the federal government has considered a nationwide ban.

In 2022, California—home to Silicon Valley—became the first state to enact a law requiring age-appropriate online design codes for minors. Since then, the state has joined Colorado, Minnesota, and New York in requiring social media platforms to display mental health warnings. Several other states have enacted or are considering legislation that requires age-verification measures.

“I’m getting the sense that every [U.S.] state wants an all-the-above strategy” for addressing who should be responsible for a child’s welfare online, Joel Thayer, president of the Digital Progress Institute think tank, told Tech Policy Press. However, “it really just comes down to what the political will is for the particular legislator and where they see the most acute issues and what they think the most effective mechanism is.”

Many lawmakers in India share the same mindset, arguing that waiting for federal action could hurt a politician’s reputation and that a localized approach is more efficient at responding to parental concerns.

However, a state-by-state approach has its downsides. India is “a large country, and enforcement doesn’t happen,” said Mishi Choudhary, a technology lawyer and online civil liberties activist. “Businesses don’t have the same predictability, and that is why all these identity verification checks are such a patchwork that nobody really understands what the transparency is.”

Around 500 million people in India are active social media users, some 750 million people own a smartphone, and just over 1 billion individuals are internet users. The country also has the largest population of young people in the world, according to a 2024 UNICEF report.

Inconsistent implementation can also be found in the United States. “It is very difficult to actually geolocate particular users,” said Kate Ruane, director of the Free Expression Project at the Center for Democracy and Technology. For instance, if you live just across the border from a state with a different social media policy than your own state’s, then “you may find yourself subject to age verification in order to access a service that didn’t used to require that of you.”

Ruane expects that the legality of social media restrictions is likely to come before the U.S. Supreme Court in the near future. Justice Brett Kavanaugh has already filed a concurrence arguing that age-based restrictions likely violate the First Amendment.

U.S. Ambassador to the United Nations Mike Waltz clashes with Cuban Foreign Minister Bruno Rodríguez Parrilla during a heated exchange on the floor of the U.N. General Assembly.

U.S. Ambassador to the United Nations Mike Waltz told Cuba's foreign minister to "back up" Tuesday after the Cuban diplomat approached the podium and interrupted his remarks on the floor of the U.N. General Assembly.

"Well, we know. We know where it went. And today we are here to share it to the world," Waltz said as he accused Cuba's government of enriching its leaders while its people suffered.

#5639. How Russia Sees the World Now

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

DMITRY ADAMSKY is Professor at Reichman University and an Associate with the Project on Managing the Atom at Harvard University. He is the author of The Russian Way of Deterrence: Strategic Culture, Coercion, and War.

The Kremlin’s New Mental Map Is Changing the War in Ukraine—and the Confrontation With the West

In 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale war in Ukraine, the Kremlin’s strategic thinking was shaped around several core ideas. It viewed the United States as its principal geopolitical rival, an adversary that had encouraged Kyiv to impose strategic defeat on Moscow. It considered Europe and NATO largely as an extension of American power. And it saw nuclear muscle flexing as efficient means to exert coercive pressure.

Today, after nearly five years of war, and 22 months of the second Trump administration, the mental map guiding Russian strategy has changed. First, Russia now sees Europe, instead of the United States, as its principal geopolitical adversary, a shift that has driven Russia’s defense establishment to call for an approach known as “castling” to deal with the main threats. Related to this, Moscow now attributes new strategic agency to Europe itself because of the dramatic military modernization taking place in many European countries and the growing interest among some European leaders in developing or extending their own nuclear deterrent. A third shift concerns Russia’s theory of deterrence: Moscow’s traditional understanding of nuclear coercion is giving way to new thinking about nonnuclear strategic deterrence using advanced conventional weapons systems aimed at Europe.

These new lines of thought, which have gained prominence among influential defense intellectuals and policy experts close to the Kremlin over the past year do not need to be endorsed in formal security doctrine to matter. As the past decade has shown, ideas circulating within Russia’s national security establishment have shaped how officials frame problems, define options, and ultimately make major strategic choices. The three recent shifts in Russian strategic thinking will likely play an important part in how Moscow approaches any negotiation to end the war, as well as how it manages future escalation and competition with Europe and the United States.

As the Trump administration steps up its efforts to engage Moscow, it is now negotiating with a Russian leadership whose strategic mindset has evolved considerably since 2022. For the United States, understanding Russia’s new mental map will not only be crucial to any attempt to find an off-ramp with Moscow. It will also be essential to reducing the risk of inadvertent escalation between Russia and Europe and to ensuring strategic stability on the continent in the years to come.

One of the most notable trends in Russian strategic thought over the past year has been the emergence of the idea of “castling.” A term borrowed from chess, castling in geopolitics describes the repositioning of forces and resources to optimize a state’s strategic posture. Moscow’s defense experts have used the concept to describe a strategy for dealing with a new reality: for the first time since World War II, they have argued, Europe, rather than the United States, has become Russia’s principal geopolitical adversary. According to this view, European countries are now the principal source of Ukraine’s military capacity, political resolve, and strategic resilience; it is Europe and not the United States that has made Ukraine its proxy and is encouraging Kyiv to pursue Moscow’s strategic defeat.

As a consequence, Moscow must direct coercive pressure at Europe—its elites, its decision-making centers, and its critical infrastructure. By doing so, Russia can instill into Europe’s calculus what theorists in Moscow call a “deterring fear”: a European reluctance to antagonize Russia through further support to Ukraine. Such action, they argue, can also rebuild Russian credible deterrence for long-term competition with the West, which is now centered on Europe.

Castling does not imply a change in Russia’s overall objectives, which remain the same as those invoked by Russian President Vladimir Putin at the time of the 2022 invasion. These include banning NATO enlargement to Ukraine and other former Soviet republics; rolling back NATO’s eastern flank; and restricting NATO military exercises, as well as the conventional and nuclear deployments of NATO members, near Russian borders. The difference is that Russian coercion must now be aimed at Europe rather than the United States to achieve these objectives.

Along with this revised assessment of Europe, Russian strategists have interpreted the Trump administration’s own strategic stance as evidence that Asia has replaced Europe as the principal theater of U.S. strategic competition. They point to the administration’s reluctance to back Ukraine, its desire to terminate the war, and its continued overtures to the Kremlin. The administration’s decision in mid-October to provide sanctions relief to Russia and apply new pressure on Ukraine amplify this view in Moscow. Russian strategists also assume Washington seeks to solidify its control of the Western Hemisphere and views China, not Russia, as its primary long-term adversary.

Moscow believes that Washington is thus seeking to reduce its commitments in secondary theaters—particularly Europe and the Middle East—in order to concentrate political, military, and organizational resources on Beijing. As Russia sees it, the new American assertiveness in Venezuela, Cuba, and the Caribbean is unsurprising. By contrast, the current war in Iran—the biggest U.S. military engagement in recent years—has been read in Moscow as an unintended deviation from the new direction of U.S. policy, one that Washington seeks to correct as soon as possible. Whether this reading reflects actual American intentions matters less than that it has become a prevailing understanding in Moscow.

A related trend in Russian discourse is a growing fixation on what defense experts refer to as Europe’s “nuclear curiosity” and new military assertiveness near Russian borders. Specifically, Russian strategists have been concerned by the French and British governments’ modernization of their nuclear programs and apparent readiness to wield them more assertively. Moscow has also noted the prospect of French or British nuclear guarantees extending to European states and nonnuclear European capitals considering the development of nuclear options of their own.

Russian sources identify Germany, Poland, and the Nordic-Baltic states as “nuclear curious,” whether in pursuing nuclear-threshold status or seeking protection under French and British nuclear umbrellas. In this regard, comments by Polish officials in early 2026 expressing support for a Polish nuclear deterrent have been particularly noted. Russian strategists have also closely monitored discussions by Paris and London about offering extended deterrence to the continent as an alternative to the U.S. umbrella.

From Moscow’s perspective, any new attempt by a European state to become a nuclear power would cross a redline and could potentially create a casus belli. Moreover, Europe’s growing assertiveness on nuclear issues is happening in parallel with the deployment of conventional deep-strike precision weapons near Russian borders. For NATO, this more active European posture mitigates the risk of Washington shifting away from its European allies, but to Moscow it signals that Europe could potentially undertake strikes aimed at disarming Russia’s nuclear arsenal or decapitating its leadership.

To Russian analysts, these various developments also suggest Europe’s growing strategic autonomy from the United States. Traditionally, Moscow has viewed Europe largely as a “parking lot” for U.S. forces: a region of the world that is relevant but strategically subordinate to Washington. That assumption is changing. Along with the perception that Europe is now Russia’s principal geopolitical adversary, the continent’s increasing strategic autonomy, including on both nuclear and conventional issues, has become a crucial variable in Russia’s calculus. According to Moscow’s defense intellectuals, Russia may need to change its posture to contain this trend, including by seeking new tools to keep Europe at bay and to coerce it below the nuclear threshold.

To align with Russia’s new mental map, Russian defense intellectuals and military officers have been examining how to make Moscow’s coercive threats more effective. During the first years of the war, Russia’s nuclear muscle flexing and hybrid warfare failed to prevent Ukraine’s allies from funneling financial support, military aid, and targeting intelligence to Kyiv. This approach also failed to deter Ukraine’s land incursions into Russia and strikes on symbolic Russian targets and Russian strategic infrastructure, let alone compel Kyiv to surrender.

Today, NATO is preoccupied with Russia’s escalating cross-domain coercion, or what U.S. and European analysts call hybrid warfare. But that is only one part of the Russian approach. As Moscow concentrates on Europe as the new primary threat, it is also drawing on new tools of coercion that Russian experts call nonnuclear strategic deterrence. For Russian military authors, NNSD involves using advanced conventional capabilities, including precision-guided weapons, drones, ballistic, cruise, and hypersonic missile systems, as well as “weapons based on new physical principles”—a Russian catchall term for electromagnetic-pulse and high-power microwave weapons to deter and compel adversaries.

Conceptually, NNSD belongs to what Russian experts refer to as the “art of strategic gestures,” a euphemism for using, or threatening to use, nuclear and nonnuclear capabilities to deter and coerce an adversary. The concept is not new. Since the early years of this century, Russian strategists have tried to develop conventional alternatives to nuclear deterrence. But until the full-scale war in Ukraine began, in 2022, these ideas remained underdeveloped. NNSD has emerged from the lessons learned during the conflict.

From the outset of the war, Russia used tactical ballistics, cruise missiles, and hypersonic capabilities, which it has paired with drones and maneuver warfare. But even when coupled with the threat of nuclear coercion, this approach underdelivered, in the view of many Russian analysts. As a result, as the war has become entrenched, in particular since 2024, Moscow has shifted its focus to more advanced ballistic salvo waves, including strikes using Russia’s intermediate-range Oreshnik ballistic missile.

Drawing on this experience, Russian military theoreticians center NNSD on using massive, deep, precision strikes against the adversary’s critical military, political, and economic infrastructure. The strategy, they suggest, should be aimed not only at Ukraine but also at its European supporters. Although the exact contours of NNSD are still emerging, the concept appears to be more escalatory than the hybrid warfare and deniable asymmetric campaigns that Russia is currently pursuing in Europe.

This is more than a peripheral theoretical discussion. There are already indications that NNSD has gained traction among Russia’s senior military officers: for example, since 2025, the flagship journal of Russia’s general staff, Military Thought, has published several articles on the subject. All the branches of the Russian military are also developing their own versions of NNSD and are seeking to claim a comparative advantage in its execution. This interservice competition may increasingly normalize what constitutes acceptable coercion below the nuclear threshold and shape targeting priorities, as well as escalation management.

As the Kremlin sees it, the war in Ukraine began as an attempt to impel Europe to reshape its security architecture. Yet the strategic landscape confronting Moscow today may look even more threatening than the one it sought to change in 2022. Washington needs to recognize that the Kremlin regards Ukraine not as a discrete conflict but as part of a broader struggle over Europe’s strategic order. Any U.S.-led effort to broker an off-ramp in Ukraine will need to reckon with Moscow’s emerging castling logic and its mounting anxieties about Europe. A settlement that addresses the immediate battlefield while leaving Moscow’s wider frictions with Europe untouched is likely to provide only a temporary pause in this larger conflict. Washington needs to understand that Russia’s nonnuclear coercion has become a part of its ongoing deterrence strategy against Europe rather than simply a feature of the war itself.

Even if Moscow engages in negotiations to end the war, it is likely to continue using military coercion against Ukraine, as well as Europe. It will do so for several reasons: to align the terms of the settlement with Russian interests, to refine Russia’s emerging theory of nonnuclear strategic deterrence, and to force Europe to adopt a different security architecture. Russia may therefore undertake precision strikes on critical infrastructure, and even possible decapitation attempts, as well as calibrated attacks of varying intensity in Ukraine, alongside diplomatic engagement. By using such tactics, Moscow will be seeking not only to gain leverage in negotiations with Kyiv but also to restore its strategic deterrence with Europe. In response, Europe, doubting the credibility of the Trump administration’s commitments and seeking to demonstrate its own resolve, may decide to escalate.

Washington should thus be prepared for the possibility that stepped-up efforts to terminate the war may coincide with heightened security risks in Europe. Given that the dynamic between Europe and Russia is now characterized by pervasive mutual suspicion, conspiracy theories, and expectations of war, there is a growing danger of inadvertent escalation. Actions by either side that are intended as limited coercion, signaling, or leverage might be misread, thus triggering a self-reinforcing action-reaction cycle in which each side’s effort to restore deterrence instead erodes it. Paradoxically, this dynamic could emerge precisely at the moment when all sides—including the Kremlin—are seeking an off-ramp. Both Europe and Russia risk crossing a “culmination point of coercion,” beyond which any additional threats to use force, or its actual use, could expand the war further.

For Russia and Europe, then, the main challenge will be to bolster deterrence without crossing this tipping point. For Washington, the primary task is not only to mediate the substance of a deal but also to learn how to choreograph negotiations amid ongoing military action. It will be imperative for U.S. officials to anticipate coercive moves by all sides without mistaking them for a rejection of diplomacy—and without allowing them to derail negotiations. Washington will need to help both Europe and Russia contain their use of coercion before the security dilemma locks them into a dangerous escalatory spasm.

Decades of Hard-Won Mediation Experience Point the Way

#5637. A Papal ‘Homecoming’

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: A Papal ‘Homecoming’

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In the early 1990s, conditions in parts of Peru were brutal. Poverty was widespread, and the Shining Path insurgency was terrorizing communities. The authoritarian government tried to crush the far-left militant group, at times committing human rights abuses. Hundreds of thousands of people fled the violence.

After the August 1991 murders of three Catholic priests in the Diocese of Chimbote, many missionaries were among them. Augustinian missionaries who stayed in Peru were told by their superiors in the United States to develop an exit plan.

In the early 1990s, conditions in parts of Peru were brutal. Poverty was widespread, and the Shining Path insurgency was terrorizing communities. The authoritarian government tried to crush the far-left militant group, at times committing human rights abuses. Hundreds of thousands of people fled the violence.

After the August 1991 murders of three Catholic priests in the Diocese of Chimbote, many missionaries were among them. Augustinian missionaries who stayed in Peru were told by their superiors in the United States to develop an exit plan.

Robert Prevost, an American priest in his mid-30s who had arrived in 1985, was among those who refused to leave. He ultimately spent 20 years in Peru across four decades, ministering to the poor, distributing food and clothing to displaced migrants, helping parishioners through personal difficulties, and naturalizing as a Peruvian citizen. Last year, he became Pope Leo XIV.

On Nov. 6, Leo is scheduled to begin an 11-day visit to Latin America that includes a return to Peru along with stops in Uruguay and Argentina. The pope was diagnosed with lung cancer this month and had part of his right lung removed. But the Vatican confirmed on Monday that Leo will proceed with the trip—the longest of his pontificate so far, covering more than 19,000 miles, 12 plane flights, two helicopter rides, and significant changes in altitude.

Dr. Stefano Margaritora, the surgeon who operated on the pope, said on Oct. 17 that Leo’s recovery was proceeding well and that “there are no clinical reasons why [the trip] should not take place.” And Luigi Carbone, the physician responsible for coordinating Leo’s medical care, said in a statement on Monday that “there are no contraindications that would prevent him from undertaking his upcoming Apostolic Journey to Latin America as planned.”

Latin America is a traditional stronghold of the Catholic Church, with around 40 percent of the world’s 1.42 billion Catholics in the region. But the share of people who consider themselves Catholic has fallen by nine percentage points in six of Latin America’s most populous countries in the last decade, according to a 2024 Pew Research Center survey.

The rise of Protestant and evangelical churches has meant a decline in Catholic affiliation in the region, particularly since the 1970s. But over the last 10 years, the Pew study showed a sharper increase in the percentage of people who describe themselves as atheist, agnostic, or “nothing in particular.” Sexual abuse scandals involving Catholic clergy have also alienated members.

The declining percentages of Catholics in Latin America mirror a worldwide trend. A global Pew survey conducted between 2023 and 2024 showed that more adults left the Catholic Church since childhood than joined it in 21 of 24 countries surveyed across all six inhabited continents.

The church’s leadership is aware of this decline. When fellow cardinals elevated Leo to the papacy, they selected a U.S.-born prelate who was known for ministering to communities in Peru, including far-flung ones where transportation was difficult. Leo would take crucifixes and ceremonial wine with him, sometimes traveling by foot or on horseback. He once said that he intended to live in the country for the rest of his life.

“It will be a massive homecoming,” said Rev. John Lydon, a fellow Augustinian who attended Villanova University with Leo in the 1970s and lived and worked alongside him in Peru. The Augustinian order emphasizes the values of truth, unity, and charity. “The Peruvian people are very warm and welcoming. They loved [Leo] because he treated them with dignity and respect,” Lydon added.

Peru is where many of Leo’s social priorities solidified. Central to his outlook are love for the poor, treating migrants with dignity, and addressing income inequality.

Living among the poor “deeply affected him—on a personal, human level,” Lydon said. Leo “would go out to where the people were … They really loved him for that. They were poor, and society treated them badly, and here was someone who was conscious of their human dignity.” Lydon spent nearly 40 years as a missionary in Peru and is now the director of the Mother Cabrini Institute on Immigration at Villanova.

Five soup kitchens that Leo established in Peru are still operating, according to Lydon. During the COVID-19 pandemic, the now-pope helped secure oxygen supplies for hospitals. Leo was so beloved by his parishioners that his birthday celebrations each September couldn’t be confined to a single day and instead were stretched out over a week.

His fellow missionaries and church leaders saw him as someone who listened thoughtfully to parishioners and fellow clergy, including those he had differences with, to get things done.

When Leo’s predecessor, Pope Francis, sent him to lead the Diocese of Chiclayo in 2014, it was heavily influenced by the controversial Opus Dei organization, which Francis did not see eye to eye with. (Opus Dei has faced accusations of secrecy and coercive recruitment.) Francis was confident that Leo would be able to engage in dialogue and be accepted, Lydon said: “I think he was picked because of his personality of working well with people who think differently.”

The cardinals who selected Prevost as pope may have seen him as an American counterweight to U.S. President Donald Trump. Leo has characterized the treatment of immigrants in the United States as “inhuman” and called Trump’s threats against the Iranian people “truly unacceptable.” Trump later lashed out at Leo on Truth Social, calling him “WEAK on Crime” and “terrible” for foreign policy.

Though Leo has avoided attacking Trump personally, the pope has said that he has “no fear of the Trump administration or speaking out loudly.” The public tension between Trump and Leo has done little to dent the pontiff’s popularity in the United States, where around eight in 10 Catholics had a favorable view of Leo in a June Pew survey. Just over half of those polled said that Trump has been too critical of the pope.

Leo’s ability to shape U.S. policy toward Latin America is limited. However, his credibility among U.S. Catholics, moral authority, Latin American background, and access to top U.S. officials—last May, he met with U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio, who is Catholic—give him an unusual platform from which to challenge Washington’s approach to issues including migration, poverty, and the use of force.

Though popes make public pronouncements on issues important to them, one of their main sources of influence comes through their power to appoint bishops in dioceses around the world. Just as Francis had goals in mind when he sent Leo to Chiclayo, Leo’s bishop appointments reflect messages that he wants local congregations to hear. From 2023 to 2025, Leo became well acquainted with potential appointees as he headed the Vatican’s Dicastery for Bishops, which reviews bishop candidates and recommends them to the pope.

Since becoming pope, Leo named Evelio Menjivar-Ayala, a native of El Salvador who was once an undocumented immigrant, as West Virginia’s bishop. He tapped Michael Pham, who fled communist Vietnam as a child, as bishop of San Diego. In Latin America, he named Luciano Maza Huamán, known for having deep local roots and serving his community, to lead the Archdiocese of Piura in Peru.

While Leo relies on bishops and other local clergy to connect with their congregations from week to week, his trip to Latin America will energize Catholics who get to hear from him directly, said Archbishop Gustavo García-Siller of San Antonio, a native of Mexico. “I don’t have any doubt that he will empower the laypeople,” he said.

Leo’s itinerary starts with a visit to Uruguay, where more than half of the population is religiously unaffiliated, making it the only country in the region without a Christian majority. No pope has visited Uruguay since 1988. Leo’s visit to Uruguay gives him a chance to generate interest in the faith and connect with non-Catholics who may share his views on social issues.

Uruguay has among the region’s most developed welfare states. Its government defines poverty reduction, income redistribution, development of human capabilities, and protection of social rights as major social spending goals. Leo has spoken about reducing poverty and removing its “social and structural causes,” the hope of a “different distribution of wealth,” and protecting vulnerable people during hardships. Such statements will find a receptive audience in Uruguay.

From Uruguay, Leo will head to Argentina, Francis’s home country. Francis never traveled back to Argentina after becoming pope. (No pontiff has visited the country since 1987.) Seven months before he died in April 2025, he told journalists that he wanted to return but there were “several things that need to be resolved first.”

Francis likely wanted to avoid being seen as taking a side in Argentina’s polarized political climate, Jimmy Burns, the author of a 2015 biography of Francis, told Reuters last year. During his stop in Argentina, Leo won’t bring the same complicated history as Francis. Leo will lead a tribute to his predecessor at the Metropolitan Cathedral of Buenos Aires, where Francis regularly celebrated mass as archbishop before becoming pope.

Leo’s final stop will be Peru, where he will spend as much time as in Uruguay and Argentina combined. He will travel to the capital, Lima, as well as the cities of Chiclayo, Cusco, and Pucallpa.

The visit promises to be emotion-filled for the pontiff, who served as Chiclayo’s bishop from 2015 to 2023. He is remembered in Peru for hiring basketball, swimming, and karate coaches to keep young people away from crime and taking them on weekend trips to the beach, as well as for being close to his parishioners and calmly staying in Peru despite the threats in the 1980s and 1990s.

“It’s going to be like a pastor coming home,” said Rev. Matthew Carnes, a Jesuit priest and vice president for mission and ministry at Santa Clara University in California. “People will just flock to see him and really savor the sense that they’re being seen, as well.”

Correction, Oct. 27, 2026: Due to an editing error, a previous version of this article misdated the years that Robert Prevost served as Chiclayo’s bishop.

Gene Kuleta is the editor of the Latin America Advisor publications at the Inter-American Dialogue in Washington, D.C. X: @Gene_Kuleta

#5636. Tesla (TSLA) quietly cuts 2026 capex guidance to $20B

Tesla has lowered its 2026 capital spending forecast. In its Q3 10-Q, the company now says it expects capital expenditures “to be in excess of $20 billion in 2026,” down from the “more than $25 billion” it had guided since April.

The change was in the 10-Q Tesla filed Wednesday, not in the shareholder letter it released the same day. And it lands in a quarter where Tesla reported $1.53 billion of positive free cash flow, a number that depends a lot on timing.

Tesla started 2026 guiding to about $20 billion in capex. In April, it raised that to more than $25 billion, and in July, CFO Vaibhav Taneja reiterated the figure while Elon Musk called 2026 a “massive capex year.”

Now it’s back to “in excess of $20 billion.”

Tesla has spent $13.01 billion so far this year: $2.49 billion in Q1, $5.79 billion in Q2 and $4.73 billion in Q3. Hitting $25 billion would have required about $12 billion in Q4 alone, more than double its biggest quarter. The new floor only requires about $7 billion.

The 10-Q says 2026 spending is “driven by our SI initiatives, including investments in compute infrastructure and data centers,” along with factory ramps and Tesla’s fleet of “company-operated SI-enabled assets.”

There’s a catch in the cash numbers, though. Tesla had $3.67 billion of equipment and construction spending sitting in liabilities at the end of September, meaning it was booked but not yet paid. That’s up from $2.63 billion at the end of June and $1.63 billion a year ago. So Tesla’s actual spending is running ahead of the cash capex it reports.

Tesla reported $6.26 billion in operating cash flow and $1.53 billion in free cash flow after capex. Here’s how it got there:

Depreciation and stock compensation are normal add-backs. They reduce profit without using cash.

The working capital line is the one to watch. Without that $2.59 billion, Tesla’s free cash flow would have been about negative $1.06 billion.

Most of it came from bills Tesla hasn’t paid yet. Accounts payable rose $1.92 billion in the quarter and accrued liabilities rose $1.95 billion, including $722 million more in “accrued purchases,” goods Tesla received but hadn’t been invoiced for.

Another $1.26 billion came from deferred revenue, mostly energy storage. Tesla’s energy deferred revenue jumped to $3.30 billion from $2.31 billion. But not all of that is cash. Tesla disclosed that it billed SpaceX $588 million for Megapacks “that have not been delivered,” which SpaceX hadn’t paid at quarter-end. That amount sits in both deferred revenue and accounts receivable, which rose $565 million.

Those gains were partly offset by a $1.82 billion increase in prepaid expenses and other assets, and a small inventory build.

If you also count the $1.03 billion increase in unpaid capex, Tesla’s free cash flow on an “as spent” basis was closer to $500 million.

The capex cut comes a month after Tesla signed $30 billion of new credit facilities. The 10-Q details them: a $20 billion delayed-draw term loan maturing in 2029, and $10 billion of revolving credit replacing a $5 billion facility. Tesla hadn’t drawn on either at quarter-end, and it had $45.4 billion in cash and investments.

Rearranging the deck chairs ahead of the merger?

None of this is improper in today’s accounting standards, which we can argue are pretty awful. Paying suppliers later, collecting deposits, and booking capex when it’s incurred are all somewhat normal accounting, and Tesla disclosed them. But they explain why the “$1.5 billion of free cash flow” headline from Wednesday’s results says less about the business than it seems.

Underneath, Tesla made $718 million of operating income, less than its $1.17 billion in stock compensation, and before working capital, it burned cash. The timing items that helped this quarter will reverse when the invoices come due and the Megapacks ship.

The capex cut is the more interesting signal. Six months ago, Tesla was telling investors 2026 would be a massive spending year to build out AI compute, Optimus and Cybercab. Now it’s lowering the target in its 10-Q instead of the shareholder letter, while lining up $30 billion of debt. Either projects are slipping into 2027, or Tesla is spending less to protect its cash. Tesla should say which.

If you’re paying attention to what EVs cost to own, home solar is how you get the “fuel” side close to zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #5634. A Less-Is-More Strategy for America

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

BARRY R. POSEN is Ford International Professor of Political Science at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology.

The Folly of Expanding the Exhausted U.S. Military

On October 8, the USS Abraham Lincoln and its accompanying strike group returned to the United States after 320 days at sea. The deployment—a near record for the modern American military—had become controversial. As the group’s ships went month after month without a port call, conditions deteriorated precipitously. Sailors reported supply shortages, plumbing issues, and water contamination. Morale dropped markedly; eight sailors even tried to commit suicide. By the time the ships finally left the Middle East, their struggles had made headlines across the country.

The travails of the Lincoln are hardly the only story about the U.S. military to generate alarm since the war in Iran began last February. Military analysts have expressed concern about other carrier groups’ lengthy deployments. They have fretted about the Trump administration’s decision to pull soldiers and weapons stationed in the Indo-Pacific out of the region and position them in the Middle East. Perhaps most of all, they have worried as the United States drains its stockpile of sophisticated munitions.

These problems all share the same root cause: the U.S. military is not built for the near-constant use it has experienced for decades. Ever since the Cold War, American defense planners have structured the U.S. armed forces to be ready to fight two major conflicts simultaneously. In practice, that means Washington has constructed a force that is designed to win short, sharp wars. For that reason, the military does not have enough units, weapons, or personnel to handle never-ending conflicts and simultaneously use its forces around the world to coerce enemies and reassure partners.

American officials are now trying to modestly increase the size of the military. More important, they are hoping that huge infusions of money will increase the capability of the existing armed forces. But making these changes will be exceptionally challenging—and they are unnecessary. The United States is a highly secure country with many capable and wealthy partners. It does not need a defense apparatus capable of fighting everywhere all at once. Instead, it would be much better off focusing its attention on a smaller set of issues and scaling down accordingly.

The U.S. nonnuclear forces, which consume more than 80 percent of the defense budget, are structured and sized for particular contingencies—currently, a clash with China, Russia, or both nearly simultaneously. The military’s weapons are therefore designed for maximum lethality and survivability in what military planners expect to be an unbelievably dangerous environment. To that end, the Pentagon has emphasized purchasing precise, long-range, and stealthy weapons. These are supposed to kick open the doors of enemy defenses and allow other expensive advanced platforms, such as F-35 fighter jets or B-2 or B-21 bombers, to deliver large quantities of slightly less sophisticated but still quite lethal precision weapons.

Washington has never expected these wars to last long. Instead, it imagines that under the fierce pressure such conflicts would generate, the adversary will cry uncle or the war will escalate to nuclear threats or—worse yet—nuclear use. During the Cold War, for example, the United States and its allies planned for an extremely intense, roughly 30-day conflict against the Soviet Union. Today’s military planners have probably made similar assumptions about a war with Beijing or Moscow. Highly capable conventional forces and the risk of nuclear escalation, American thinking goes, should deter an attack. If deterrence fails, U.S. planners hope to stop an enemy in its tracks and create escalation risks so great that the adversary will realize its errors and try to reverse course.

But such thinking means that Washington does not have enough resources to sustain long, great-power conflicts. At $100 million each, the F-35 fighter aircraft, a mainstay of the air force, navy, and Marine Corps, is simply not designed for mass production. Neither are the long-range stand-off missiles that would be the preferred munitions in the early days of such a war. The U.S. military has developed sophisticated theater ballistic missile defenses, but their main purpose is to defend U.S. forces and essential military infrastructure—not towns, cities, and civilian industries. They can be used for this purpose under certain conditions, but doing so quickly exhausts the supply of missiles. In a war with China or Russia, civilians in local, allied countries will need to look after themselves.

These problems have become readily apparent in Washington’s war against Iran. The United States likely thought it could rapidly overpower the Islamic Republic—at best a middle power, albeit one with great ambitions. After all, under the right circumstances, the U.S. military handily overmatches these kinds of enemies, as it did Iraq in the 1990–91 Gulf War. But Washington’s enemies have smartened up since then. They no longer simply produce smaller and less capable versions of the great-power militaries that American forces are designed to deter and combat. Instead, they have prepared themselves to take a beating and keep on fighting, even if at modest levels of effectiveness.

Overly ambitious war plans and faulty policies are not the only reason the U.S. military is struggling. Politics is also creating difficulties. Because the American people hesitate to support wars abroad, U.S. politicians will do anything to avoid casualties. That means they take all the high-end weaponry that the military stockpiles to break down great-power defenses and instead spend it profligately on regional conflicts. Similarly, they deploy scarce air defense weapons to protect allies’ towns and cities, lest those allies consider finding new partners or decide to settle a war by compromising with their enemies.

U.S. policymakers also generally overuse the military. Politicians like to accompany diplomatic messaging with action, in part because they do not believe in diplomacy anymore, which pushes them to parade or use the armed forces far more often than is warranted. When asked to act, commanders usually salute and say yes. Others simply cannot sit still. Commanders, after all, took on their roles because they wanted to command something.

The result is a worn-down military. The defense press is full of stories about ships and aircraft that are not up to standard because they have been out too long, resulting in deferred maintenance. This, in turn, creates repair problems for other units. When a ship must stay in dry dock longer than usual to receive adequate repairs, other ships must wait longer for servicing—which means their needs also grow. Ultimately, the navy has fewer fully ready combat ships available in the event of a great-power challenge. The army has similar problems, with combat and support vehicles arriving at maintenance depots in unexpectedly poor condition. In the air force, the F-35’s “full mission capable rate,” which was never great, has deteriorated from 38 percent to 25 percent since 2021. And the overall readiness of air force aircraft has declined in recent years, in part simply because of overwork.

All the while, U.S. soldiers, sailors, pilots, and marines are constantly on guard, coercing and deterring U.S. enemies and reassuring allies. This, too, creates high levels of activity that can negatively affect retention. Although the military has generally been successful at retaining senior enlisted personnel in recent years, it may soon run into difficulty. Since 2024, navy warships have repeatedly gone to sea undercrewed. The ultimate result of this overuse is that the sharp edge of the military is dulled, and the steel blade is weakened.

U.S. defense officials are aware that they have a problem. Currently, they are trying to solve it by building a somewhat bigger and far more technologically capable military. Congress, for example, appears poised to increase the Pentagon’s so-called base budget—which encompasses what the Congressional Budget Office defines as the “enduring costs of manning, training, and equipping the military”—by over 25 percent, or to $1.1 trillion for the 2027 fiscal year. The Pentagon hopes to secure an additional $350 billion over the base budget in a subsequent bill. Defense officials also want to add over 40,000 new service members. But this is, at best, a personnel increase of three percent, which is much too small to fill the additional units needed to solve extant readiness problems. And the fancier weapons Congress hopes defense officials will buy cannot overcome the strain of constant deployments and repeated wars. The defense industrial base is barely keeping up with current demands, and recruiting large numbers of new service members would likely require offering much higher wages, which would then need to be extended to everyone else. Personnel costs would start consuming an ever-greater share of the defense budget. U.S. officials could instead opt for conscription, but that would produce a backlash.

There is, however, an alternative: scaling down. Instead of pretending that it must be able to fight two great-power wars at once, Washington should cut its policy coat to fit the available cloth. It should continue to build forces for major contingencies, such as deterring adventurism by China. But policymakers should agree to slow down other projects. Congress should task the Pentagon with reviewing the armed forces and come up with an activity budget, which would be based on an assessment of how much real military activity—flying hours, ship days at sea, training days in the field—can be undertaken without harming the readiness of the total force. It would then allocate that activity appropriately. Based on what they ought to know about the virtual attrition the military has suffered thanks to 30 years of hyperactivity, commanders should determine what level of exercises, forward deployments, and regional wars the military can actually support without doing damage to the capabilities it wishes to have ready every day to deter a great-power war and, if need be, wage one effectively. The U.S. Navy already knows the activity level that its fleet can support, which it has detailed in its Optimized Fleet Response Plan. But American presidents and defense secretaries have simply ignored this plan whenever they wanted to deploy more forces.

The activity budget should not be classified but instead should be public information, listed along with the dollar budget that the Pentagon publishes annually, voluminous summaries of which can be found at the Defense Department’s comptroller’s website. When politicians want to exceed the activity levels on which they have previously agreed, they should have to explain why and how they plan to repair the force after intense use. Commanders should think of this as an ethical issue. Every now and then, one should resign when overuse causes serious problems.

To help lighten its load, Washington will need to hand off some responsibilities to others. This should be feasible despite the need to manage Moscow’s aggressive impulses, which are the presumed cause of one of the contingencies—a war with Russia—that drives U.S. defense planning. The principal European states have more than enough economic and technological resources to deal with Russia on their own, if they so choose. The transatlantic bargain should thus be more forthright about the limited liability that the United States is prepared to assume for the continent’s defense.

The National Defense Strategy of the Trump administration is honest about this fact, but U.S. military activity levels and deployments in Europe suggest that the United States has not truly reduced its role in Europe’s defense. The supreme allied commander of NATO is still an American, when the position should be filled by a European. Tens of thousands of U.S. troops are still based all over Europe; many should come home. Pentagon websites and those of American commands in Europe feature laundry lists of exercises and maneuvers across the continent that involve not only forward-based U.S. forces but also rotating deployments of units based in the United States. These numbers, too, should diminish.

American officials may want to be able to solve most of their global security challenges by simply buying more forces. But a much better solution is for the United States to change its grand strategy and act with greater restraint. Washington simply does not have the will or the resources to provide for both Asia’s and Europe’s defense while fighting protracted conflicts elsewhere, most recently in the Middle East. Nor, for that matter, does it need to. The United States is inherently a very secure country. It is rich, endowed with bountiful natural resources, insulated by vast oceans, and blessed with relatively weak and pliant neighbors. Only huge changes in the political constellation of power in Asia, Europe, or the greater Middle East could seriously affect U.S. security.

Washington should buy sufficient military power and stock adequate reserves of raw materials to ensure against such changes. But in general, it can afford to be less proactive. A change in grand strategy might even allow the United States to cut the defense budget. If concerns about the ballooning U.S. deficits are correct, then the Pentagon will need to help pay the bills to set things right.

Why the Future of War Can’t Escape the Past

How the Search for Economic Security Will End in Geopolitical Calamity

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

Dependence on America Has Left It With Little to Offer Would-Be Partners

How Battlefield Cooperation Is Binding Together America’s Adversaries

Andrea Kendall-Taylor and Richard Fontaine

How Europe Can Take More Responsibility—Without Starting an Arms Race

The Narrow but Feasible Path to Collective Good

Alex Imas, Mohamed A. El-Erian, and James Manyika

Niall Ferguson, Lisa Klaassen, and Eric Schmidt

The Constraints and Costs of the New Security Consensus

John Foster Dulles and the Cold War Consensus

How the Search for Economic Security Will End in Geopolitical Calamity

Henry Farrell, Abraham Newman, and Yeling Tan

#5633. Trump Has Lost the Foreign-Policy Debate

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Trump Has Lost the Foreign-Policy Debate

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

U.S. President Donald Trump’s America First agenda has transformed U.S. foreign policy. He has applied tariffs against long-standing U.S. trading partners, taken unilateral military action in Venezuela and Iran, waived new economic sanctions on Russia, questioned the U.S. commitment to the NATO alliance, retreated from U.S. participation in multilateral organizations, and ended much foreign and humanitarian assistance.

But does Trump’s foreign policy resonate with U.S. foreign-policy leaders or the public? To answer this question, we drew on unique surveys recently carried out by the Chicago Council on Global Affairs and the University of Texas at Austin. These surveys polled a nationally representative sample of the U.S. public, as well as 449 leaders in business, government, media, universities, and religious groups, among others. Respondents were asked questions on a variety of issues related to foreign affairs, including economic policy, national security, and the ongoing conflicts in Ukraine, Iran, and Israel. Of the leadership groups we surveyed, approximately one-third either currently or formerly served in government positions related to international policy. These surveys allowed us to compare the foreign-policy views of different segments of American society, as well as across political parties. The survey of the public was fielded from July 10 to July 21. The survey of leaders was fielded from July 27 to Sept. 19.

U.S. President Donald Trump’s America First agenda has transformed U.S. foreign policy. He has applied tariffs against long-standing U.S. trading partners, taken unilateral military action in Venezuela and Iran, waived new economic sanctions on Russia, questioned the U.S. commitment to the NATO alliance, retreated from U.S. participation in multilateral organizations, and ended much foreign and humanitarian assistance.

But does Trump’s foreign policy resonate with U.S. foreign-policy leaders or the public? To answer this question, we drew on unique surveys recently carried out by the Chicago Council on Global Affairs and the University of Texas at Austin. These surveys polled a nationally representative sample of the U.S. public, as well as 449 leaders in business, government, media, universities, and religious groups, among others. Respondents were asked questions on a variety of issues related to foreign affairs, including economic policy, national security, and the ongoing conflicts in Ukraine, Iran, and Israel. Of the leadership groups we surveyed, approximately one-third either currently or formerly served in government positions related to international policy. These surveys allowed us to compare the foreign-policy views of different segments of American society, as well as across political parties. The survey of the public was fielded from July 10 to July 21. The survey of leaders was fielded from July 27 to Sept. 19.

The results are not good for the president, especially before an already tricky midterm election. Many of Trump’s foreign-policy views are unpopular with U.S. foreign-policy leaders and the public. Trump has failed to sell fellow Republican foreign-policy leaders or voters on key parts of his vision. Rather than mark a turning point in America’s approach to the world, Trump’s foreign policy may not outlast Trump himself.

On international economic policy, large majorities of every leadership group believe that tariffs worsen the cost of living in the United States, including 86 percent of business leaders, 100 percent of labor leaders, and approximately 80 percent of officials in Congress and federal executive branch agencies. While Republican leaders are more supportive of his trade agenda, 65 percent of them acknowledge that tariffs are worsening the cost of living in the U.S., and 81 percent of Republican leaders said tariffs are bad for U.S. relationships with other countries.

The public has also rejected the Trump trade agenda. Eighty-one percent of the overall public believe that Trump’s tariffs do not improve the cost of living in the U.S., while 63 percent believe that they are bad for creating jobs. Even for Republicans, Trump’s tariff agenda is divisive. While approximately 56 percent of Republicans agree that tariffs help the economy, 62 percent also believe that they raise the cost of living.

At the same time, when asked to identify the most important issue in the upcoming midterm election, more Republican voters point to inflation and the cost of living than any other single issue. Although Trump has repeatedly turned to the message that the U.S. has been “ripped off on TRADE … by friend and foe, alike, for DECADES,” a bipartisan majority of the public believe that international trade benefits both the U.S. and other countries, including 62 percent of Republicans.

Trump has similarly failed to persuade leaders and the public about the wisdom of his approach to America’s traditional allies. Trump’s belief that the United States has been exploited by its security alliances has been a centerpiece of his foreign policy since entering politics in 2015. In May 2025, for example, Trump told a graduating class of West Point military officers, “We’ve been ripped off at the NATO level. We’ve been ripped off like no country has ever been ripped off.” At a campaign rally in February 2024, Trump stated that for those NATO members that did not meet defense spending targets, he would encourage Russia “to do whatever the hell they want.”

This view of U.S. security alliances is decisively opposed by leaders in every professional group surveyed and the U.S. public, including by bipartisan majorities of voters in both parties. While Republican leaders support greater burden-sharing, over 60 percent of Republican leaders and over 90 percent of Democratic leaders support either maintaining or increasing the level of U.S. commitment to NATO, while 85 percent of respondents across all leadership groups support using force if Russia invaded a NATO ally in Eastern Europe. Only 15 percent of the public supports decreasing the U.S. commitment to NATO. If Russia invaded a NATO ally like Poland, 60 percent of the public would support using military force, including majorities of Republican, Democratic, and independent voters.

The conflict in Ukraine further illustrates the gap between Trump and both foreign-policy leaders and the public. Since returning to office in 2025, Trump has ended U.S. military aid to Ukraine, told Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky that he didn’t “have the cards” to reach a favorable settlement with Russia, and called for Ukrainians to replace Zelensky with a new leader.

Yet 84 percent of foreign-policy leaders, including 79 percent of Republicans, favor sending additional military aid to Ukraine. More than 92 percent of leaders, including 94 percent of Republicans, favor increasing economic and diplomatic sanctions on Russia. The public also is supportive of Ukraine. Almost 60 percent of the public supports sending additional military aid to Ukraine, 70 percent support increasing economic and diplomatic sanctions on Russia, and almost 70 percent support admitting Ukraine into NATO, including 60 percent of Republican voters.

And, in the crisis triggered by Trump’s claims over Greenland, 90 percent of the public opposes forcibly annexing Greenland, including 82 percent of Republican voters and almost 90 percent of Republican elites.

In the conflict in Iran, the public is not persuaded that the war is justified, though leaders are more divided. Fifty-eight percent of Republican leaders still believe that the Iran war was the right decision, while 98 percent of Democratic and 73 percent of independent leaders say it was the wrong decision.

After almost eight months of war, however, only a third of the public believes Trump made the right decision. Conversely, 68 percent of the U.S. public believes the war was not worth fighting, and the same percentage opposes using force to overthrow the Iranian government. A majority of Republican voters still approve of the decision to attack Iran but also believe that costs of the war have been high, including by raising the cost of living in the United States.

Perhaps most damning of all for Trump, only 20 percent of the public thinks the United States is more respected on the world stage compared to 10 years ago, including only 40 percent of Republicans.

There are genuine partisan gaps on some issues, including areas where Trump’s foreign-policy agenda retains the support of both the Republican Party leadership and its base, such as on strengthening immigration enforcement, hostility to action on climate change, and withdrawing from numerous multilateral organizations. Yet these tend to be areas where Trump has pursued more traditional Republican foreign-policy priorities, rather than his own idiosyncratic beliefs.

That foreign-policy leaders reject key aspects of the America First agenda may not be surprising. Trump himself has framed his agenda as a rejection of classic elite attitudes. But much of the public is equally hostile to his approach, suggesting that project may not be durable. These attitudes provide an opening for members of Congress and the next U.S. president to pursue a new foreign-policy vision that will resonate more with leaders and the American public, while strengthening America’s standing in the world.

Jonathan Monten is an associate professor in the Department of Political Science at University College London.

Jordan Tama is a professor in the Department of Foreign Policy and Global Security at American University and a senior nonresident fellow at the Chicago Council on Global Affairs.

Josh Busby is a professor of public affairs at the University of Texas at Austin and former senior climate advisor at the U.S. Defense Department. He is the author of States and Nature: The Effects of Climate Change on Security, which was the winner of 2026 Grawemeyer Award for Ideas Advancing World Order.

Fox News provides coverage on the controversy surrounding Minnesota Rep. Ilhan Omar as she faces two federal probes, with one focusing on her immigration history and another on financials.

Nearly 200 House Democrats are moving to shield Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., from further investigation after federal investigators raided her Washington, D.C., home earlier this month.

Rep. Pramila Jayapal, D-Wash., led 175 members of the House Democratic Caucus in a letter to Department of Homeland Security (DHS) Secretary Markwayne Mullin and Attorney General Todd Blanche, criticizing the probe into Omar's immigration history as a partisan attack and accusing them of trying to sway her bid for another term in Congress.

"Congresswoman Omar is seeking reelection in Minnesota’s 5th congressional district in just one week. This type of interference strips Minnesotans of their right to a free and fair election guaranteed to them under the Constitution," the letter said.

"We stand with Congresswoman Omar and condemn the weaponization of law enforcement against her. You have already disgraced the offices you hold, and we demand you refrain from taking any further actions that could affect the outcome of the upcoming election."
