#5630. Brazil’s Economic Impasse

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MONICA DE BOLLE is Senior Fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics, an Adjunct Professor at Georgetown University, and a co-author of The New Economic Nationalism.

Neither Lula nor Bolsonaro Can Solve the Country’s Problems

On October 5, the day after the first round of Brazil’s general elections, the country’s financial markets were euphoric. Brazil is struggling with high debt and low growth, and investors are eager for leaders to reduce budget deficits, deregulate, cut taxes, and perhaps even privatize state assets. After nearly four years under the left-wing President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, known as Lula, who has been slow to implement such measures, investors hope a right-wing government will create opportunities for bold reform.

Rule by the right might be exactly what Brazil gets. The far-right Liberal Party made substantial gains in the legislature, becoming the single largest party in both the Senate and the Chamber of Deputies. Its presidential candidate, Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of the notorious former President Jair Bolsonaro, led the aging incumbent, Lula, by nearly two percentage points in the first round of voting. Lula has shown himself capable of impressive political comebacks, but Flávio Bolsonaro is still the favorite in the runoff vote scheduled for October 25.

Even if Bolsonaro wins, however, the prospects for the Brazilian economy are dim. The policies he has put forward are nothing close to the substantial fiscal consolidation program that will be necessary to bring down debt and stimulate private investment, and his determination to weaken Brazil’s political institutions creates the kind of uncertainty that scares investors away. Economic growth has been sluggish under Lula, whose administration has struggled to relieve the high debt burdens afflicting the public sector and households. What would truly doom the country to economic crisis, however, is the kind of authoritarian-inspired politics that a Bolsonaro government could usher in.

Brazil is in serious economic trouble. The country’s sovereign debt ratio has ballooned to 83 percent of GDP, fiscal deficits leave no room for additional government spending, and high interest rates are hurting consumers and investors. Meanwhile, the Iran war has raised the price of fuel and other goods, weighing on household balance sheets already strained by high debt and leaving many Brazilians desperate for leaders who can alleviate their daily hardships.

This predicament is years in the making. Before Jair Bolsonaro was elected in 2018, his predecessor, President Michel Temer, enacted a cap on public spending to contain Brazil’s runaway fiscal problems. Temer introduced the cap as a constitutional amendment to prevent future governments from changing the spending ceiling to suit their needs. Yet Jair Bolsonaro raised the cap several times anyway, which required convincing Congress to amend the constitution. In return, the executive branch effectively ceded control of the budgetary process to the legislature.

Lula therefore inherited a system in which he had little room for fiscal adjustment, and he made few attempts to fix the problem during his term. The result was high budget deficits and persistent inflation, which forced the central bank to raise interest rates substantially. In turn, high interest rates damaged household balance sheets and lowered overall investment and growth.

If Lula were reelected, he would find it incredibly difficult to introduce new fiscal reforms and other measures to support growth. He promised to reduce spending and has proposed a comprehensive overhaul of Brazil’s tax system, which would help increase the budget surplus and reduce public debt. Yet Bolsonaro’s Liberal Party and its allies now hold a majority of seats in the Senate and the Chamber of Deputies, allowing them to block any legislation Lula might pursue.

Yet it is overly optimistic, if not completely unrealistic, to expect that Bolsonaro would fare better. Neither candidate has provided much detail during the campaign about what he would do to fix the economy. Bolsonaro has instead spoken often about “institutional reforms,” which he has said would include amending Brazil’s 38-year-old constitution to make existing labor and environmental regulations more flexible, among other changes; meddling with the Supreme Court; and granting amnesty to his father, who is serving a prison sentence for leading a 2023 coup attempt. Those steps are likely to drive away investors because stable institutions are necessary for an economy to function well. A government that undermines the judicial system and the constitution will compromise the integrity of its economic policies, no matter what those policies are or how good the reputations of the officials making them may be.

Gathering support for such an overhaul of the country’s political institutions, moreover, would require compromises that could worsen Brazil’s economic troubles. To secure enough votes in Congress to advance Bolsonaro’s priorities—for example, two-thirds of the Senate would need to approve the removal of a Supreme Court justice, and three-fifths of both houses must approve a constitutional amendment—the government would have to bring legislators outside its coalition on board. Those politicians would almost certainly demand substantial funds for their constituencies in exchange for their support, adding to the budget deficit.

Bolsonaro’s far-right movement will also be focused on holding together its recently formed coalition of governors, mayors, and voters. Much of its constituency is lower-middle-class Brazilians who rose out of poverty during the boom years two decades ago, while Lula was serving his first two terms in office. After seeing their income growth stagnate in the years since, these voters have turned to the Liberal Party, but their loyalty is new. Most are self-employed and face economic precarity. Both Bolsonaro and Lula are now trying to appeal to these voters by promising initiatives that boost income and offer household debt relief, but delivering such programs—if it is even possible—would only exacerbate Brazil’s economic woes.

What Bolsonaro has shared about his economic policies does not inspire much confidence, either. His party has said it would reduce government spending by $40 billion, or 1.5 percent of GDP, and deliver primary budget surpluses—a measure of the budget balance that does not include interest payments. But it has not explained how it would accomplish this, nor has Bolsonaro elaborated on his vague plans for controlling the rise in public debt. Ultimately, it is unlikely that Bolsonaro can deliver on any of these promises, especially if he keeps his pledge not to cut pensions and key social programs.

Part of the problem is that the Liberal Party’s economic program lacks a viable plan for reducing costs. It proposes eliminating at least ten government ministries but has no estimate of the resulting cost savings. When Jair Bolsonaro took similar steps after he assumed office in 2019, removing cabinet positions and laying off public servants, those moves had little effect on fiscal outcomes. Other promised measures include reducing Brazil’s value-added tax, phasing out a tax on financial transactions, lowering taxes on electricity and fuel, spending $180 billion on infrastructure over four years, and building 2.5 million new homes. None of them would fix Brazil’s fiscal problems; they would only make those problems worse.

The same goes for the rest of Flávio Bolsonaro’s tax plan. His economic advisers have said they intend to reverse every tax created or raised under Lula’s finance minister, Fernando Haddad. Haddad’s tax increases were intended to shift the tax burden to high-income brackets, raise government revenue, and reduce inequalities in the tax system. These taxes have contributed to a modest increase in revenue but fell short of delivering budgetary surpluses. Bolsonaro has argued that the measures are impeding investment because they tax higher income brackets more heavily, but there is no evidence to support this idea. Instead, repealing all these tax increases would worsen Brazil’s fiscal situation. A simple calculation shows that Brazil needs to increase its primary budget balance by about six percent of GDP, either through increased revenue or decreased spending (or both), to keep its debt from rising further. That adjustment would have to be spread over at least three years, giving indebted households and firms time to change their consumption and investment patterns, to avoid a recession. Slashing taxes, however, would reduce government revenues, raising the debt.

Brazil’s high interest rates are another constraint. The central bank’s current rate is 13.75 percent, astronomical by Latin American standards, dictating interest rates everywhere in the economy. Public debt service payments alone consume some 8.7 percent of GDP. Bolsonaro would not be able to lower interest rates significantly without sparking a recession, so he would face the same problem that has plagued Lula during his latest term—few good options for containing a rapidly growing debt.

Neither Lula nor Bolsonaro is well positioned to solve Brazil’s economic problems. A decisive turn toward illiberalism under Bolsonaro, however, would all but assure economic disaster.

Flávio Bolsonaro has never disavowed his father’s championing of Brazil’s dictatorship, which lasted from 1964 to 1985, and his Liberal Party traces its lineage to the party that sustained the military regime. Moreover, he has pledged to release his father and his father’s associates who are in prison for staging an insurrection and a coup attempt in 2023. Flávio’s brother Eduardo Bolsonaro, a former member of Brazil’s Congress who would likely play a significant role in his brother’s government, has openly expressed his admiration for Viktor Orban’s illiberal regime in Hungary, claiming a desire to learn the “best practices” of a movement that rewrote Hungary’s constitution, reshaped its courts, silenced its press, and deployed other overtly authoritarian tactics. And the substantial far-right bloc in Brazil’s Congress supports policies to undermine institutions, including by impeaching members of the Supreme Court—a step these parties advocated even before corruption allegations emerged against several of the court’s justices. (Flávio Bolsonaro is also under investigation for his involvement in the same corruption scandal.)

Although Brazil is not at risk of returning to military rule, the former authoritarian regime serves as a warning of what could happen if a Bolsonaro administration follows its illiberal inclinations to their logical conclusion. During those two decades of military dictatorship, Brazil spent at unsustainable levels, mostly funding state enterprises, to preserve the regime’s hold on power. The country defaulted on its public debt, endured corrosive hyperinflation, and saw income inequality skyrocket. Six economic stabilization plans failed and growth stagnated. Brazil is not alone in this experience. No Latin American country that swung from democracy to authoritarian rule in the past century has benefited economically from the shift. Countries elsewhere in the world that prospered after such a transition, including some in East Asia, became less authoritarian during their economic ascent.

Brazil’s rightward shift fits a regional pattern. The common thread that unites Brazil with Argentina, Chile, and Colombia is the crude libertarian worldview that each of their far-right movements espouse. All of them talk about citizens becoming entrepreneurs free of the state. They tell voters who have unstable employment and little economic security that their policies will bring prosperity. But their populism rarely wins the confidence of investors. Like its peers, when the Brazilian far right says it can overcome the country’s substantial problems with institutional demolition and a threadbare economic agenda, what it really offers is empty promises.

#5627. Space Junk Is a Problem We Know How to Solve

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Space Junk Is a Problem We Know How to Solve

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In August, a stray upper stage from a SpaceX Falcon 9 struck the moon. Roughly the size of a school bus and weighing 4 metric tons, it hit the lunar surface near Einstein Crater at approximately 5,400 miles per hour, carving out a substantial new crater. Had a similarly massive piece of debris survived reentry and struck a populated area on Earth, it would have caused significant damage and loss of life. And, just last week, large pieces of space debris fell on land in both Minnesota and Indonesia, presumably from a Chinese rocket launched on 14 October.

These events highlight an increasingly visible problem of space exploration: Satellite mega-constellations aren’t only transforming our night skies. Humanity’s expansion into space is beginning to reproduce the same take-make-waste model that has caused significant environmental damage on Earth.

In August, a stray upper stage from a SpaceX Falcon 9 struck the moon. Roughly the size of a school bus and weighing 4 metric tons, it hit the lunar surface near Einstein Crater at approximately 5,400 miles per hour, carving out a substantial new crater. Had a similarly massive piece of debris survived reentry and struck a populated area on Earth, it would have caused significant damage and loss of life. And, just last week, large pieces of space debris fell on land in both Minnesota and Indonesia, presumably from a Chinese rocket launched on 14 October.

These events highlight an increasingly visible problem of space exploration: Satellite mega-constellations aren’t only transforming our night skies. Humanity’s expansion into space is beginning to reproduce the same take-make-waste model that has caused significant environmental damage on Earth.

The number of objects in Earth’s orbit has risen dramatically and is likely to increase several-fold again over the coming decade. In 2010, the active satellite population was roughly 1,000. Today, space surveillance networks track approximately 40,000 objects in the Earth’s orbit. But only around 17,000 of these are still active payloads; the rest are retired satellites, spent rocket parts, and other fragments.

When it comes to smaller space debris, the European Space Agency (ESA) estimates that more than 1.2 million debris fragments larger than 1 centimeter are already orbiting Earth. The greatest danger posed by these smaller fragments is that they will collide with functioning spacecraft. Traveling at several kilometers per second, even a small fragment can disable a satellite, while a larger impact can shatter it into thousands of additional pieces.

Those fragments can then strike other objects, producing a self-sustaining cascade of collisions known as the Kessler syndrome. The ESA’s latest Space Environment Report, published in September, identifies the potential development of the Kessler syndrome as the most serious long-term threat to space exploration.

The consequences of such an event would extend far beyond space, with profound impacts on global infrastructure on Earth. Satellites supporting communications, navigation of shipping and aviation, banking, weather forecasting, climate monitoring, disaster response, and even military early-warning systems could be severely damaged or even completely lost. This would create gaps in essential services and heighten the risk of geopolitical miscalculation, as disrupted communications and surveillance systems could be mistaken for hostile acts by other governments, instead of technical failures.

Some projections cited in United Nations space-sustainability discussions suggest that more than 100,000 satellites could be orbiting Earth by 2030, if proposed mega-constellations proceed as planned. As orbital traffic grows, so does the probability that a single collision could initiate further fragmentation and make valuable orbital regions increasingly hazardous or even unusable.

Rather than rely on maneuvers to avoid a collision (overseen by surveillance networks such as the U.S. Space Surveillance Network), more systemic solutions are needed. We should not reproduce the same wastefulness that we exhibited on Earth—a wastefulness that brought about the plastics pollution crisis. Instead, we should build a circular space economy, and fortunately, we can use solutions from Earth to do so.

Instead of treating rockets and satellites as disposable objects that are launched, used, and then abandoned or burned up, a circular space economy would entail spacecraft designed for durability, repair, upgrading, refueling, and eventual recovery.

This is no longer merely a hypothetical idea; it is becoming a major frontier of space technology and commercial innovation. The ESA has set out a vision for a zero-debris approach and a circular space economy by 2050. This approach will keep spacecraft, components, and materials in use through repair, refurbishment, upgrading, reuse, and eventually recycling in orbit. ESA-backed research is also exploring orbital recycling plants and spacecraft designed from the outset for disassembly and material recovery.

Circularity could therefore become not only an environmental safeguard but also a foundation for the next phase of space exploration. There are already commercial operators such as ClearSpace and Astroscale that are trying to develop spacecraft capable of approaching, inspecting, and capturing defunct satellites, while robotic arms and standardized docking interfaces could allow satellites to be repaired, refueled, or safely removed.

There is also an important connection to critical minerals: A circular space economy could turn orbital debris from a costly liability into a valuable economic resource. Satellites and rocket components contain aluminum and titanium, alongside smaller quantities of cobalt, nickel, and rare-earth elements used in batteries, magnets, electronics, and communications equipment.

Rather than allowing these materials to remain stranded in orbit or burn up during reentry, future recovery systems could capture, dismantle, and process defunct spacecraft. Some estimates show that the reuse of space junk could have a net value of between $570 billion and $1.2 trillion. This amounts to somewhere between 5,312 metric tons and 19,124 metric tons of scrap metal that can be recovered, not considering future materials yet to be launched. Recovering and reusing these materials in orbit could reduce the financial and environmental costs of launching replacement materials from Earth.

Elon Musk’s SpaceX is no stranger to circular solutions in space. In fact, it was reuse that (quite literally) lifted the company to the forefront of commercial spaceflight. By landing and reflying the Falcon 9’s first-stage booster, SpaceX transformed one of a rocket’s most expensive components from single-use hardware into a repeatedly used asset. It is also less expensive to refly than to build new launchers. A NASA assessment estimated an average cost of around $50 million for a flight using a previously flown booster, compared with roughly $65 million for one using a new booster. Circularity, in other words, is not a constraint on the business model; it is a major source of its competitive advantage.

Commercial success has helped turn reusable boosters into an industry standard that China and other launch providers are now pursuing. On July 10, China recovered an orbital-class booster for the first time, catching the returning first stage of a Long March 10B rocket in a net above an offshore platform after it had placed a satellite in orbit.

But the solutions are not only technical. Addressing the problem will also require some innovation of the space governance architecture and the resolution of several important legal questions. The core international rules governing space use were written in the 1960s and 1970s, when space activity was the purview of governments and relatively few objects were in orbit. Currently, defunct satellites remain under the jurisdiction and ownership of their registering states, meaning that companies cannot simply capture and repurpose them as abandoned material. Also, existing guidelines provide no standardized procedures for obtaining consent, transferring ownership, or allocating liability if a recovery operation causes damage.

Space is already becoming militarized. The U.S. shouldn’t let adversaries take the lead.

The United States is racing ahead where Europe, China, and India struggle.

Negotiating a new treaty to manage space seems hopeless, but a workable mechanism exists.

Encouragingly, making these zero-waste and circular practices routine would not require constructing an entirely new system of space governance. The foundations already exist in the Outer Space Treaty of 1967, the Space Liability Convention, the Registration Convention, and broader U.N. sustainability guidelines. Several international and intergovernmental bodies already exist as well and have made orbital debris and space sustainability a priority.

The space governance architecture must be further updated and operationalized for an era increasingly dominated by commercial operators such as SpaceX, Amazon Leo, or Geespace (a private Chinese commercial operator backed by Geely) and their satellite mega-constellations. To support sustainability in orbital space, measures to prevent or minimize the creation of debris were introduced at the international level by the U.N. Committee on the Peaceful Uses of Outer Space (COPUOS) in 2007 in the form of the Space Debris Mitigation Guidelines.

Furthermore, the Inter-Agency Space Debris Coordination Committee (IADC)—an international forum of space agencies, authorized governmental and intergovernmental entities which coordinates efforts to deal with space junk—first launched its Debris Mitigation Guidelines in 2002, with subsequent updates, the latest one in 2025. An important update has been a new constellation-wide perspective. The revised guidelines recognize that risks depend not only on whether each satellite is eventually removed but also on where thousands of satellites are placed and how they travel through other orbital shells.

These guidelines have not been entirely unsuccessful: They have helped establish widely accepted practices such as passivating spent rocket stages, limiting routine debris release, and disposing of satellites after use. Compliance is also improving among commercial operators.

But the guidelines were never strong enough to reverse the overall growth in risk. There’s still a mismatch between being useful mitigation guidance drafted 20 years ago and being fit for purpose in the age of mega-constellations. And the guidelines are still voluntary and too weakly enforceable to manage the full systemic risks.

Under the Outer Space Treaty, states remain responsible for supervising private space activities, while the 1972 Liability Convention channels responsibility for damage through launching states, rather than directly to commercial operators. Back then, space exploration was solely the domain of specialized government agencies and the military, and the involvement of private companies and commercial operators as we see today was not anticipated.

Modernizing the liability framework could introduce binding end-of-life obligations, standardized consent procedures for servicing, and clearer liability rules for the growing number of commercial actors. Ideally, circularity would be a condition of receiving a launch license.

Even deposit-based systems such as “space junk bonds” have been proposed by insurers as a more market-based approach. Such as system would require operators to help cover future recovery and cleanup costs. This approach would be modeled on surety bonds, which have been used to ensure the decommissioning of oil rigs and mines. Before launch, satellite operators would pay a bond proportionate to the number, mass, orbital lifetime, and collision risk of their spacecraft. The bond would be refunded once safe de-orbiting and recovery were completed and verified.

The upcoming 60th anniversary of the Outer Space Treaty in 2027 offers a timely opportunity to address these governance gaps. The treaty itself need not be renegotiated; instead, states could develop and adopt a “Protocol on Circular Space Operations”— a supplementary instrument developed through COPUOS that would translate existing treaty principles into practical guidance and implementation.

This protocol could clarify procedures for transferring ownership of defunct satellites, obtaining consent for their capture, allocating liability during removal missions, and recognizing recovered materials as economic resources.

Space debris and the risk of catastrophic collisions are not unavoidable consequences of human space exploration. The ideas already exist, the technologies are emerging, the institutional foundations are in place, and the economic opportunities could be substantial.

Albert Einstein once argued that “a new type of thinking is essential if mankind is to survive and move toward higher levels.” If space exploration is to take humanity to those higher levels and further into outer space, we must move beyond disposable spacecraft and toward circular design that enables repair, reuse, and recovery.

Patrick Schröder is a senior research fellow at the Environment and Society Centre, Chatham House.

#5625. What Israel’s Election Won’t Change

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

ALUF BENN is Editor in Chief of Haaretz and a Senior Fellow at the Bill Graham Centre for Contemporary International History at the University of Toronto.

The Constraints and Costs of the New Security Consensus

When Israelis go to the polls on October 27, much of the world will be rooting for the defeat of the country’s longtime prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu. Under his nearly two decades of rule, Israel has become steadily more aggressive. It has abandoned any pretense of supporting a two-state solution; started constructing hundreds of new Jewish settlements in the West Bank; and, following Hamas’s cross-border attack on October 7, 2023, laid waste to the Gaza Strip, occupied parts of Lebanon and Syria, and launched war against Iran, its archnemesis. Meanwhile, its democratic institutions have faced endless attacks by Netanyahu and his allies, who have proposed laws that would, among other things, greatly curtail the power of Israel’s courts. Should he return to power for an unprecedented seventh term, many fret that the country will permanently transition into a rogue theocratic actor.

To many voters, this election thus marks a crucial turning point in Israeli domestic policy. The prime minister’s main political opponent, former Israel Defense Forces Chief Gadi Eisenkot, holds a more liberal worldview than his competitor. He supports an independent judiciary, a free press, and a professional civil service. Should he win, he will likely try to reverse Netanyahu’s efforts to curtail each institution’s independence.

But an Eisenkot victory is unlikely to change Israel’s foreign policy or approach to the Palestinians. Almost all of Israel’s Jewish political parties, Eisenkot’s included, are opposed to Palestinian statehood, not just Netanyahu’s Likud. They all believe that Israel’s brutal war on Gaza was morally just and practically necessary. And almost all of them think that Israel was right to attack Iran and to establish and create what its government and military call “security zones”—occupied territory—in Lebanon and Syria. In fact, the only real disagreement between Eisenkot and Netanyahu on matters of security is whether the latter made the right tactical decisions in each of these conflicts, such as when Netanyahu rejected a cease-fire in Gaza that would have freed Israeli hostages earlier.

That is not to say he will follow exactly the same course. Eisenkot and Netanyahu differ in important ways on the barrage of West Bank settler attacks against helpless Palestinians, which are aimed at driving them out of their land. The prime minister, committed to his far-right allies, shrugs off the issue, whereas his competitor calls their behavior “Jewish terror.” Even if Eisenkot has little pity for the Palestinian victims, he realizes that the West Bank is on the verge of a bloody explosion and that Israel is at risk of even greater international opprobrium because of its reckless policy there. But overall, no matter what happens in the elections, Israel will continue to repress the Palestinians and strike its enemies. It will thus face diplomatic and economic costs, as the country’s frustrated partners begin cutting off aid and perhaps even enacting sanctions.

There are many issues at play in Israel’s election. Politicians, for example, have debated ultra-Orthodox exemptions from the draft, Netanyahu’s corruption charges, and what, if anything, the government should do about Jewish terrorism. But on the whole, the contest has been focused on who is to blame for failing to stop Hamas’s October 7 attack, which killed 1,200 Israelis and resulted in 251 more being taken hostage in one day. The opposition has argued that Netanyahu is responsible for the country’s worst-ever security failure, pointing out that he sustained Hamas with Qatari money, ignored intelligence warnings about Hamas plots, and then blocked an independent investigation into what happened. Eisenkot, who lost a son and two nephews to fighting in Gaza, has been particularly unsparing in his criticism, repeatedly hammering Netanyahu for refusing to take any accountability for the calamity. The prime minister and his supporters, for their part, have brushed off these accusations. Instead, they have blamed October 7 on the military and security services. They have also promoted conspiracy theories that there is what Netanyahu’s cronies and supporters call “treason from within”—or that someone inside Israel helped facilitate Hamas’s brief invasion.

But despite this blame game, both sides agree on the tragedy’s primary lessons: that Israel is surrounded by diehard merciless enemies and that the Palestinians can never be trusted. This attitude is, in part, a raw expression of anti-Arab racism, which is more visible in Israel’s post–October 7 Jewish society. But it is also an electoral play. If the polling is correct, Eisenkot will need the support, however tacit, of Israel’s two Arab parties to put together a government. Netanyahu is thus trying to win over voters who, much as they hate him personally, cannot stand the idea of a government propped up by Palestinians.

This strategy is unlikely to work on liberal Israelis, who view this election as the last chance to save their homeland from sliding into dictatorship. Many Israelis who live abroad, including immigrants who left the country because of Netanyahu’s leadership, thus intend to come home and vote for his replacement. But these election day visitors and likeminded liberal voters are rallying to protect and preserve their way of life in a country that is becoming more religious and nationalistic. And even many of the anti-Netanyahu voters would be unhappy about partnering with the Arab parties to replace the current government. Few would bother to think about the two-state solution, viewing the idea as a relic from the overly optimistic 1990s. Eisenkot, the current leader of this electorate, has declared his personal opposition to a Palestinian state, as have Israel’s other centrist and conservative opposition figures. Only Yair Golan, the leader of Israel’s sole left-wing Jewish party, supports what he calls “separation from the Palestinians” and a return to diplomacy—and even he lacks a plan for how to make that happen.

In one sense, the country’s rejection of a two-state solution is not new. The last detailed negotiations over Palestinian statehood took place in early 2001. Most Jewish Israelis turned against the idea following the second intifada and Hamas’s 2007 takeover of Gaza. Netanyahu has railed against Palestinian sovereignty for his entire political career. (His brief ostensible endorsement of such a state, in 2009, was heavily conditioned and widely considered insincere.) And after October 7, opposition to the notion has become far more resolute. Before the attack, some major Jewish parties would still pay lip service to the notion of making peace with the Palestinians, and their politicians expressed tentative openness to a settlement. Now, they either reject it out of hand or simply dismiss the issue altogether.

Israeli officials have also become far more aggressive. The country may have turned against Palestinian independence years ago, but for most of the last two decades, it avoided full-scale wars and preserved the low-cost status quo. That restraint, even if disrupted by periodic rounds of fighting with Hamas, endeared Netanyahu to mainstream Israelis, who could pretend that the conflict was a thing of the past and all but forget about the Palestinians. Yet this attitude changed when Hamas crossed the border. Afterward, Israel adopted its current policy of destruction and occupation—which Netanyahu’s election campaign is bragging about, since it knows that bombing Iran and invading Gaza, Lebanon, and Syria have been extremely popular among the population at large. The prime minister’s main opponents all endorsed these tactics—criticizing him only for failing to achieve his pledge of “total victory.”

Netanyahu, in turn, blamed Eisenkot for being soft on Hamas when he served in the country’s war cabinet. Eisenkot then said that he had voted for a more aggressive invasion than what the prime minister ultimately undertook. Eisenkot later pledged to “end Hamas rule in Gaza within 18 months” if elected. This line is not necessarily as hawkish as Netanyahu’s posture, since it suggests a willingness to consider diplomatic means of removing the group from power. Eisenkot has also strongly criticized Netanyahu’s campaign pledge to overthrow the Iranian regime. Instead, he supports measures to take the enriched uranium out of Iran and to put its nuclear program under a verification regime. “You can’t overthrow a government from the air; it’s a delusion,” he said. But he nonetheless backed bombing the country to weaken its military and nuclear program, even if he disagreed with the ultimate end.

The rest of the world, of course, does not share Israel’s view. Quite the contrary—in the wake of the destruction of Gaza, polls show that Israel’s international approval ratings have hit new lows. Politicians in every region are arguing that Israel is guilty of major human rights violations, with many claiming that the country has committed genocide. Even some of Israel’s friends are warning that the state has gone overboard. In July 2025, for example, U.S. Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer declared that although Israel “has a right to defend itself and end Hamas’s brutal rule,” the “reporting and images out of Gaza—depicting the devastating levels of hunger, starvation, suffering and death—are wholly unacceptable.”

Outside countries are also particularly furious about its settlement expansion in the West Bank, which cannot be justified as a reaction to October 7. As a result, sanctions have reached Israel’s shores. British Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband declared on September 8 that Israel’s occupation of the West Bank is unlawful and announced a sanctions regime against the settlements and companies involved in their financing and construction. Given that London is a major financial center, Israeli banks—which are financing the major settlement projects—are now in trouble.

Across the Atlantic, Israel still has the support of U.S. President Donald Trump, who has endorsed Netanyahu’s reelection campaign. But most of the Democratic Party’s politicians—and even many Republicans—now want to stop or limit U.S. aid and technology transfers to the Israel Defense Forces, citing the use of American munitions in the destruction of Gaza. Many prominent Democratic Party officials want to sanction the country, too. If Democrats take control of at least one house of Congress in the midterms, the United States could stop sending blank checks to Israel.

Israelis generally attribute such rising international anger to anti-Semitism instead of sincerely held concerns about the rights of Palestinians, Middle Eastern stability, or the proper allocation of military resources. But they are nonetheless aware of their growing isolation and fear that, after the election, they might get hit by what former Israeli Prime Minister Ehud Barak has called a “diplomatic tsunami.” This assessment is widely accepted in the mainstream media and by Netanyahu, who argues that only he—with his superior English skills—can stop such an onslaught, much as he maneuvered to stop the Biden administration from ever imposing real consequences on the country for its war in Gaza. The Israeli right wing has been arguing for many years, with justification, that Israel can continue its occupation and denial of civil rights to the Palestinians while manipulating the international community with hasbara (public diplomacy and lobbying). Eisenkot, by contrast, will try to win the world back by being upfront about what his country is willing to accept and then negotiating honestly—two things that Netanyahu has rarely done.

Eisenkot’s approach would likely win over more international partners, at least for a time, as did the leaders of two other former military chiefs, Barak and Yitzhak Rabin, who unseated Likud governments. Netanyahu has burned so many bridges with his parade of lies that Eisenkot would probably get an international grace period simply by virtue of his relative honesty. And Eisenkot might indeed make changes that please Israel’s partners, such as slowing settlement construction. He opposes the controversial E1 settlement project, which is aimed at slicing a future Palestinian state by half, and has pledged to tackle Jewish terrorism.

But eventually, Eisenkot would disappoint any overseas Israel supporters who expect a thorough reversal of Netanyahu’s foreign and Palestinian policies. And even if he somehow changed his own mind on Palestinian statehood—which he insists he has never supported—his freedom of action would be severely restricted by his governing coalition. Eisenkot’s power bloc relies on two right-wing parties whose leaders strongly oppose the two-state solution and mistrust the Palestinians. One of them, Avigdor Lieberman, even wants to dismantle the Palestinian Authority and bring in Jordan to rule over the Palestinians who reside in the West Bank (an idea that Amman rejected decades ago). They would bring Eisenkot’s government down if he expressed openness to a Palestinian state.

Polling shows that the October 27 election will be extremely tight. In fact, if the surveys are right, neither Eisenkot nor Netanyahu will win an outright majority. The country is thus likely to experience a period of horse-trading, as both politicians jockey to peel off the other’s partners. Netanyahu might even try to lure Eisenkot himself into a kind of unity government, promising to put his judicial overhaul or other culture-war campaigns on hold in return. (Eisenkot has adamantly rejected the idea of serving under Netanyahu, even for a rotation, but about-faces have been a staple of Israeli coalition politics.) Alternatively, negotiations could fail, and the country would almost immediately return to the polls. It would not be the first time that an Israeli contest failed to produce a lasting government: the country held five general elections from 2019 to 2022 before its political leaders established a coalition that could hold on to power for more than 12 months.

But no matter the outcome—a Netanyahu caretaker government, a Netanyahu majority government, a unity government, a minority Eisenkot cabinet supported by the Arab parties, or even an Eisenkot majority government—Israel’s next prime minister will keep occupying parts of its neighbors and brutalizing the Palestinians. He will thus have to contend with Israel’s increasing isolation from the rest of the world. The government might end up spending even more on local arms production to replace U.S.-funded systems, working hard to bypass export controls on military and dual-use items (as sanctioned Iran and Russia do today, and as Israel did in its early years). Israel might face its ultimate fear—losing the United States’ veto at the UN Security Council, which has protected its occupation since 1967 from international censure, even under critical Democratic presidents such as Jimmy Carter and Barack Obama.

This may not be the outcome that Israelis actually want. But without a sea change in how they think about their problems, it is the one they are careening toward. The odds, however, are particularly pronounced if Netanyahu and his partners stay in power. In that case, more liberal Israelis will not wait to see how badly the world punishes their country. They will instead flee to pursue their way of life in a quieter place.

The War in Gaza Will Likely Reinforce the Country’s Rightward Tilt

#5623. Judge who apologized to accused would-be Trump assassin lights up DOJ in Hunter Biden FBI case

Fox News correspondent David Spunt reports on two former FBI officials being charged by the DOJ with lying in the investigation into Hunter Biden's taxes.

U.S. Magistrate Judge Zia M. Faruqui, who previously apologized to a man accused of attempting to assassinate President Donald Trump, rebuked the Justice Department on Tuesday over its attempt to bring new obstruction charges against two former FBI officials without first securing judicial approval.

Former FBI officials Nikki Floris and Bradley Benavides were arrested last week after the Department of Justice accused the duo of misrepresenting their involvement in investigations concerning former President Joe Biden’s son, Hunter Biden, in statements to Congress.

Hours before prosecutors were scheduled to defend their original allegations at a preliminary hearing, where the government must establish probable cause, the DOJ filed a superseding criminal complaint seeking to add new charges against the former officials. The new complaint sought to add felony obstruction allegations against the former officials while softening some of the language prosecutors had used to describe their involvement in the Hunter Biden investigations.

Faruqui sharply criticized the DOJ for filing an unsigned and unsworn criminal complaint in the middle of the night without first obtaining judicial approval.

WHO IS JUDGE ZIA FARUQUI? WHAT WE KNOW ABOUT THE FEDERAL MAGISTRATE WHO APOLOGIZED TO COLE ALLEN

Magistrate Judge Zia M. Faruqui speaks before a debate between incarcerated students and James Madison University students on the topic of abolishing life without parole at Prettyman Federal Courthouse in Washington, D.C., on Nov. 22, 2024. (Craig Hudson for The Washington Post via Getty Images)

"I think they should have filed nothing," Faruqui said, according to reports from inside the courtroom. "They never had complaints filed on their record that are unsigned, waiting for a judge to make a probable cause finding. I think that it is inappropriate and harms the defendant. I'm frustrated that I got it in the middle of the night, but I'm much more concerned that it's filed on the public record."

"Why today on this one case have you changed the entire history of how you charge cases?" Faruqui asked the DOJ. "What is it? It appears that – it makes it look like the United States is afraid of me trying to find probable cause."

John Borchert, a prosecutor from the U.S. Attorney's Office in Washington, D.C., said it was the first time he had filed new charges against someone without going through judicial review.

Faruqui rejected the superseding complaint because prosecutors had not secured the required judicial authorization, allowing the preliminary hearing to proceed on the original allegations that Floris and Benavides lied to Congress. Floris’ lawyers argued that the changes in language between the two documents calls into question the factual basis of the DOJ’s case.

JUDGE WHO APOLOGIZED TO WHCA SHOOTING SUSPECT WAS APPOINTED BY COURT FULL OF ANTI-TRUMP JUDGES

Hunter Biden and former FBI official Nikki Floris. (AP Photo/Susan Walsh; AP Photo/Eric Thayer)

In the superseding complaint, prosecutors described Benavides and Floris as having been "involved in" the Foreign Influence Task Force's work concerning Hunter Biden, a softer characterization than the original complaint's suggestion that they had been at the "center" of those activities. Floris, who was previously said to have been involved in "scores of conversations" related to Biden’s son, is now only accused of participating in "conversations."

In what may have been a reference to the recently unsealed charges against former FBI Director James Comey, Faruqui said that moving on a superseding complaint without a probable cause finding "would be like charging someone by information... but in a complaint context."

"If you want a superseding complaint, like every other case that’s happened in this district, we will have a judge make a probable cause finding," the judge said. "Not send me something in the middle of the night and expect that it gets done."

Faruqui has previously faced criticism from conservatives over his support for diversity, equity and inclusion (DEI) initiatives, criticism of Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) and positions on pretrial detention. He also drew backlash after apologizing to Cole Allen, the man accused of attempting to assassinate President Donald Trump at the 2026 White House Correspondents' Association Dinner, and criticizing the government's use of five-point restraints against him.

"To me, it’s extremely disturbing that he was put in five-point restraints, a person with no criminal history," the judge said about Allen at a hearing earlier this year. "It’s troubling. I never heard of one Jan. 6 defendant who was put in five-point restraints or in a safe cell. If the only way to keep him safe is the most punitive thing, that’s a problem."

The DOJ has also been questioned for charging via complaint rather than through a grand jury, a procedural shortcut usually reserved for imminent threats to public safety.

DEMOCRATS HAVE ‘DIRTY HANDS’ OVER COMPLAINTS ABOUT OMAR RAID WEEKS BEFORE ELECTION, EX-PROSECUTOR SAYS

Critics have questioned the timing of the charges, with midterm elections being just days away when news of the two former officials’ arrests broke.

Floris’ lawyers have similarly argued that the timing and content of the charges are meant to engineer publicity.

"What is going on at the D.C. U.S. Attorney's Office? In the middle of the night, the government filed an unsigned and unsworn complaint attempting to fix the issues with their original charge against Ms. Floris and adding a new charge just as meritless as the first one," said Floris' legal team in a statement provided to CBS News.

"In our combined decades of DOJ service, we have never seen or even heard of something like this being filed," they went on. "That's because the public lodging of criminal charges must be authorized by the grand jury, a judge, or the defendant herself. Here, the government has taken that power for itself in a desperate attempt to delay our client's right to challenge the charges in court and litigate this case in the media, rather than the courtroom. We have asked to address this outrageous government conduct against a decorated public servant at today's court hearing."

Legal teams for Floris and Benavides, as well as the DOJ, did not respond to requests for comment when reached by Fox News Digital on Tuesday.

Get the latest updates on the Trump administration and Congress, exclusive interviews and more Fox News politics content.

#5622. New Jersey may finally reverse its draconian e-bike law after massive backlash

New Jersey lawmakers are already looking to walk back one of the country’s most controversial e-bike laws, just months after the sweeping new regulations took effect and sparked widespread backlash from riders, bicycle shops, and advocacy groups.

A newly introduced bill, S4830, sponsored by State Senator Raj Mukherji, would remove licensing, registration, and insurance requirements for low-speed electric bicycles with motors rated at 750W or less and a maximum motor-powered speed of 20 mph (32 km/h). This includes Class 1 and Class 2 electric bicycles.

The proposal would essentially restore bicycle-like treatment for these two classes of e-bikes, importantly including throttle-equipped models that make up the majority of e-bikes sold in the state and more broadly in the US, while maintaining stricter requirements for faster and more powerful electric bicycles.

The move comes after New Jersey’s previous governor, Phil Murphy, signed a sweeping e-bike law in January following several tragic crashes involving young riders.

That legislation, which took full effect in July, eliminated the state’s previous three-class e-bike framework and introduced licensing and registration requirements for all electric bicycles, including ordinary low-speed commuter models. Throttle-equipped and faster e-bikes also faced insurance requirements.

As we previously reported, New Jersey bicycle shops experienced devastating sales declines after the law took effect, with some reporting drops of 20% to 90%. Several businesses were forced to lay off employees, downsize, or even close entirely as e-bike sales and usage plummeted.

Meanwhile, riders faced confusing new requirements that made e-bikes considerably less accessible, which was particularly impactful for lower-income residents, people with disabilities, and those who rely on electric bicycles for daily transportation.

I own a Class 3 e-bike, but I also acknowledge that with its potential assisted speed of 28mph it's of a different breed than Class 1/2 bikes -- that's just too fast in crowded bike lanes or shared use paths.

The first priority should be to target reckless riding by anyone, and illegal bikes should be confiscated if they're involved.

The original law was intended to improve safety, but critics argued that it largely punished responsible riders instead of addressing the growing problem of high-powered electric motorcycles masquerading as bicycles. Only responsible riders actually registered their bikes as required by law, while others were deterred from cycling, and the hooligan riders who often gave e-bikes a bad name continued to ride illegally.

After all, requiring someone riding a 20 mph (32 km/h) commuter e-bike to obtain a driver’s license and register their bicycle does little to address reckless riding on machines capable of highway speeds.

The newly proposed legislation appears to acknowledge that distinction, offering a more targeted approach that could preserve reasonable safety regulations without making ordinary e-bike ownership unnecessarily complicated.

S4830 still needs to work its way through the legislature before becoming law, but for New Jersey’s struggling e-bike industry, it’s a promising sign that lawmakers may finally be listening. It is still widely argued that requiring electric bicycles from 21-28 mph (32-45 km/h) to require a driver’s license and registration is overly burdensome, but at least the state appears to be moving back toward the right direction.

The e-bikes that make up Micah’s current daily drivers are the $999 Lectric XP 2.0, the $1,095 Ride1Up Roadster V2, the $1,199 Rad Power Bikes RadMission, and the $3,299 Priority Current. But it’s a pretty evolving list these days.

#5621. Is Israel a Good Ally?

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Books: Is Israel a Good Ally?

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

As Israelis head to the polls on Oct. 27, Americans are increasingly asking: Is Israel a good ally for the United States?

The long-standing bipartisan consensus holds that Israel is the United States’ closest regional ally and among its most important partners globally. However, more and more young Americans on both sides of the political aisle seem to be holding a different view. They see Israel as a genocidal and militaristic state, dragging the United States into unnecessary foreign wars.

As Israelis head to the polls on Oct. 27, Americans are increasingly asking: Is Israel a good ally for the United States?

The long-standing bipartisan consensus holds that Israel is the United States’ closest regional ally and among its most important partners globally. However, more and more young Americans on both sides of the political aisle seem to be holding a different view. They see Israel as a genocidal and militaristic state, dragging the United States into unnecessary foreign wars.

In the War Room: The Inside Story of Israel’s Fight Against Hamas and the Iranian Axis, Yonah Jeremy Bob and Elliot Kaufman, Wicked Son, 352 pp., $32.50, September 2026

A new book helps shed light on this question by telling the remarkable story of how the Jewish state redrew the map of the Middle East and enabled Washington to address one of its gravest national security challenges. Absent the bravery and ingenuity of Israel’s armed forces, the United States could very well be living under the threat of a nuclear-armed Iran today.

Written by Yonah Jeremy Bob and Elliot Kaufman, In the War Room chronicles Israel’s national security policy and decision-making starting from Hamas’s attacks on Oct. 7, 2023, to the Gaza peace plan going into effect on Oct. 10, 2025. As leading journalists (Bob works at the Jerusalem Post and Kaufman works at The Wall Street Journal), the pair gained enviable high-level access to senior decision-makers in both Washington and Jerusalem and have provided a gripping first draft of this remarkable period in world history.

According to a quote apocryphally attributed to Vladimir Lenin, “There are decades when nothing happens, and there are weeks when decades happen.” This quote applies to the decades of history that transpired in the 104 weeks covered in Bob’s and Kaufman’s 300-page book.

So much has changed in this time that it is almost hard to remember what the Middle East looked like back in September 2023.

Iran was enjoying its greatest level of global power and influence since the time of the ancient Persian empire. It was a near-nuclear power with a proxy network projecting malign influence throughout the region. Experts at the time estimated that Iran’s breakout time to the bomb was essentially zero.

The Islamic Republic was a terrorist puppeteer pulling the strings on Hezbollah in Lebanon and Hamas in Gaza. Hezbollah alone possessed more than 100,000 rockets, essentially holding Israel hostage. The Assad regime in Syria was an Iranian ally serving as a transshipment hub for weapons and training from Tehran to Beirut.

In their detailed yet highly readable book, Bob and Kaufman provide a blow-by-blow account of how Israel almost single-handedly disarmed Iran and its “ring of fire” and literally cleared the way for U.S. bombers to degrade Iran’s nuclear threat by the summer of 2025.

According to Bob and Kaufman, Israel was caught unprepared on Oct. 7 because the national security establishment was so fixated on the greater threats posed by Iran and Hezbollah. Jerusalem underestimated Hamas, and it paid the price.

After briefly considering a preemptive blow against Hezbollah, Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his war cabinet decided that a full-scale invasion of Gaza would be necessary to eliminate the threat from Hamas.

Bob and Kaufman are mostly critical of Israel’s strategy in Gaza, which they describe as achieving “mixed results at a great cost in human life and global opinion.” There was no off-the-shelf plan for a full-scale invasion of the enclave. Instead of clearing and holding territory, as the United States eventually learned to do in its counterinsurgency campaigns in Iraq and Afghanistan, Israel cleared areas of Hamas fighters only to depart and allow Hamas to return and regroup.

Instead of invading all of Gaza simultaneously, Israel segmented its operations regionally and temporally, starting with Gaza City in the north and continuing south to Khan Younis and, eventually, Rafah. The segmented strategy meant the war dragged on, casualties mounted, and public opinion in the United States and the world turned against Israel.

Still, in the end, Bob and Kaufman credit Israel with decimating Hamas as an effective fighting force.

The most captivating part of their account, however, covers Israel activating secret war plans to eviscerate Hezbollah and Iran. Here, Bob and Kaufman describe the effort as “spectacular and decisive” with “shockingly little diplomatic cost.”

Hezbollah struck first, raining down daily rocket fire on Israel shortly after Oct. 7 and causing Netanyahu to clear inhabitants from their homes in Israel’s north. By summer 2024, Israel was ready to strike back with a trap that had started to be laid in the mid-2000s. Hezbollah’s longtime leader, Hassan Nasrallah, feared that cell phone communications were not secure and ordered his fighters to communicate through beepers and walkie-talkies instead. Through years of careful work, the Mossad set up front companies and secretly became the key supplier of Hezbollah’s communication devices.

Placing explosives in beepers made them bulky, so the Mossad’s front companies took out advertisements touting the beepers’ enhanced security and durability. The ruse was so successful that orders came pouring in from other eager customers from around the world. On Sept. 17, 2024, the beepers beeped their last. Hezbollah commanders removed the devices from their pockets only to have them explode in their faces, “wounding some 3,500 Hezbollah fighters in a matter of seconds.” The next day, Hezbollah walkie-talkies self-destructed, injuring another 500 fighters. What is less known is that Iranian officials used the same communication devices and more than 100 fighters in the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps were also injured in the blasts. The effect on Hezbollah as an organization, however, was much broader as Hezbollah fighters, not knowing what might blow up next, trashed electronic devices and avoided communications.

Next, Israel targeted Hezbollah’s massive weapons caches. On Sept. 23, Israel hit an astounding 1,300 targets in a single day. The authors quote Netanyahu boasting, “In six hours, we wiped out most of the ballistic missile stockpiles Hezbollah has amassed.”

Four days later, Hezbollah’s leader was removed from the battlefield. Israel dropped 85 bombs on Nasrallah’s underground compound in Beirut, eliminating the person that Netanyahu called “the axis of the axis.” The strike was aided by targeting systems that Mossad operatives had planted inside the building a month earlier and by the distraction of Netanyahu’s speech at the United Nations General Assembly, timed to finish just before the strikes began.

Next came Israel’s invasion of Lebanon, in which the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) seized 85,000 military items and killed or wounded more than 10,000 Hezbollah fighters. By late November, Hezbollah was decimated and signed a one-sided cease-fire on Israel’s terms.

Netanyahu did not plan to overthrow President Bashar al-Assad in Syria, but Hezbollah’s demise left the Syrian dictator naked. Hezbollah propped up Assad in his long-running civil war, but with Hezbollah decimated, Ahmed al-Sharaa and his Turkish backers saw an opportunity. In an 11-day blitz, rebels stormed Damascus and toppled Assad on Dec. 8, 2024.

Amid the confusion in Damascus, Netanyahu saw an opportunity to neuter a longtime rival and the IDF sprang to action. Israel’s 480 strikes destroyed 70 percent of Syria’s most important military capabilities. Almost overnight, Syria was transformed from a potent pro-Iranian proxy to a weakened and staunchly anti-Iranian regime.

Of course, the font of all these other dangers was the biggest target of them all, the Islamic Republic of Iran.

Israel got in some early shots to disable Iran’s air and missile defenses following large-scale Iranian attacks in April and October 2024. The major action, however, occurred during the 12-day war in June 2025. The new Trump administration pursued negotiations over Iran’s nuclear program, and when talks deadlocked on June 12, Israel enforced U.S. President Donald Trump’s redline.

Starting on June 13 and continuing for nearly two weeks, Israel unleased 4,600 bombs on approximately 900 targets, decimating nuclear facilities and killing at least 30 top Iranian officials and nine nuclear scientists. Israel’s air force was supported by hundreds of Mossad agents and special forces soldiers on the ground inside Iran, launching swarms of drones on critical targets. As Bob and Kaufman explain, “with tens of millions of Iranians who detest the regime—Persian, Azeri, Kurdish, or otherwise—there is a nearly endless supply of Iranians for the Mossad to recruit.”

The big outstanding question was, would the United States help finish the job? It was not a foregone conclusion. Trump was sympathetic. Where U.S. President Joe Biden warned Netanyahu not to strike Iran’s nuclear program back in October 2024, then-candidate Trump asked, “Isn’t that what you’re supposed to hit?”

Others in the Trump administration, including U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance, were more skeptical. But Bob and Kaufman tell the captivating story of how Eyal Zamir, Israel’s chief of the general staff, briefed Vance on the generational opportunity for U.S. security. He told Vance, “We are already flying over Fordow freely. There is a straight-line aerial lane open.” The United States could waltz in, destroy Iran’s nuclear program, and depart with very little risk to U.S. forces. Vance was persuaded.

On June 21, Operation Midnight Hammer began—more than 125 planes, seven B-2 bombers,12 30,000-pound bunker-busting bombs. Zero U.S. casualties. Trump crowed the next morning: “Iran’s key nuclear enrichment facilities have been completely and totally obliterated.”

Did Israel drag the United States into this war? In Trump’s own words prior to the strikes, “like I’d go in unwillingly. … If we don’t make a deal, I’ll be leading the pack.”

The Biden administration repeatedly asked Israel to restrain itself to prevent escalation. Notably, when Iran’s first direct attack on Israel failed to cause much damage in April 2024, Biden urged Netanyahu not to retaliate and to “take the win.”

Jerusalem mostly ignored this advice and often only informed Washington of the next major escalation after the operation was already underway. Had Netanyahu followed Biden’s advice, Tehran’s ring of fire might still be standing, backed by nuclear arms.

But Biden eventually came around and claimed credit for Israel’s aggressive stance. In December 2024, he said, “Our approach has shifted the balance of power in the Middle East.”

Similarly, as late as May 2025, it was not at all clear that Trump was willing to employ U.S. military power to strike Iran’s nuclear program. It was only Israel’s effective military campaign against Iranian leadership, air defenses, and above-ground nuclear plants that convinced Washington to join the fray.

According to Bob and Kaufman, “Jerusalem needed to be daring enough to act first, so that the opportunity would be too good for President Trump to pass up.”

So, is Israel a good ally of the United States? To be sure, peace in the Middle East has not yet broken out. Iran is stubbornly resisting the United States’ naval blockade. Iranian-backed Houthis are targeting Saudi Arabia and shipping in the Red Sea. And Hamas, Hezbollah, and the Iranian nuclear program may one day be reconstituted.

But still, Iran is much further from achieving its nuclear ambitions today than it has been in more than 20 years. Hamas, Hezbollah, and Assad’s Syria—the ring of fire protecting Iran’s nuclear program—are no longer relevant factors in Middle Eastern security.

As Bob and Kaufman argue, “From these blows, the anti-Israel and anti-America proxy axis that Iran spent decades building might never recover.” For the sake of international peace and security, let’s hope they are right.

Walsh School of Foreign Service at Georgetown University, president of CNQ Global Advisors, and a senior fellow at the Atlantic Council’s Scowcroft Center for Strategy and Security. His latest book, cowritten with Dan Negrea, is We Win, They Lose: Republican Foreign Policy and the New Cold War. X: @matthewkroenig
